DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
EHD:
MAY 18 1935
SKH:
DEPANIMLNI UI SIATE
Tokyors 1225 of April 4, 1935.
This despatch is one of a series in
which the Embassy has discussed the various
factors which have led to Japan's expan-
sionist activities in recent years. This
present despatch, prepared by Mr. Dickover,
attempts to calculate the probable duration
of the present wave of expansion on the
basis of some law of periodicity or
frequence.
The writer sets forth that the various
waves of expansion in Japanese history,
marked by the wars with China and Russia,
the occupation of Siberia, etc., are funda-
mentally due to economic pressure at home.
"Military ambition and the need for the
military to justify their existence, as
well as certain highly idealistic and
carefully inculcated theories of the
'manifest destiny' of the Japanese people,
play their part in the formation of the
expansionist waves, but behind it all lies
the urge of the Japanese people for more
economic opportunity."
The writer considers that the
acquisition of Manchuria will furnish the
Japanese an outlet for their surplus
economic

=== Page 29 === (confidence 0.99)
5
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
economic energy for several years to
come, while at the same time the internal
forces which oppose the expansionist waves
are growing in strength. It is therefore,
he declares, not unreasonable to assume
that the wave of expansion which started
in 1931 has reached its furthest point and
is now receding, and it appears likely that
there may be a period of six to ten years
of quiescence in Japanese expansionist
activities, after which another wave of
expansion may be expected to arise.
Although the despatch is extremely
interesting from a speculative viewpoint,
I do not think the argument is convincing.
No law of periodicity which takes into
account only the short period of Japanese
history since the Restoration can be
considered reliable. Nor can these waves
of expansion be predicted without taking
into account a vast number of incalculable
factors such as the conditions of affairs
in other parts of the world.
In the interest of historic accuracy
it should be pointed out that economic
pressure was not solely, or even mainly,
responsible for Japan's expansion in
certain instances cited by the Embassy.
Japan took steps to acquire the Lu Chu,
Kurile and Bonin Islands because she had
claimed

=== Page 30 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 3 -
claimed an interest in those regions for
many years, and because she feared their
acquisition by some foreign power. The
Sino-Japanese Var is commonly supposed to
have been started in order to resolve an
internal political situation, and the
Russo-Japanese War was caused by Russian
expansion in the direction of Japan.
Thus, these several crises, advanced by
the Embassy in support of the thesis that
Japan's urge toward expansion is caused
by economic pressure, were in fact brought
about by entirely different causes.
The
reader receives the impression that the
writer has attempted to interpret events
in Japanese history in accordance with
a pre-conceived view of causative factors.
There can be little doubt that
economic pressure, particularly in recent
years, has played an increasing part in
the urge to expand of the Japanese, but
whether the next wave of expansion can
be predicted on the basis of the writer's
argument is highly debatable.
894.00/542.
WTT
WTT/DLY

Subject:
The Rising Tide of Japanese Expansion.

DEPARTMENT

The Secretary of State,
mat

Sir:

MAY 15 193
FILED
In previous despatches this Embassy has attempted to
analyze and evaluate the various factors which have led to
Japan's expansionist activities in recent years, but as ye
no attempt has been made to calculate, on the basis of past
periods of activity and present tendencies, the probable
duration of the present wave of expansion. The growth of
nations resembles, in some ways, the tides of the sea, and,
as is true of the tides, the ebb and the flow of the power

=== Page 32 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
and growth of nations might conceivably be foretold if the
necessary data could be obtained. However, the factors
which cause the flow of the tides in the affairs of nations
are not so easily ascertainable, nor so susceptible of
exact interpretation, as are those which cause the rise and
fall of the tices of the sea.
The following, therefore, is not an attempt to predict
the probable period of the peak of the tide of Japanese ex-
pansion, but instead an endeavor to calculate the probable
duretion of the present wave of expansion. For, like the
tides of the ocean, the tides of growth of nations are apt
to rise in a series of waves, which roll forward and recede,
to be succeeded by other waves, each rolling farther than
the preceding one, until the height of the tide is reached.
The Japanese nation, since its emergence as a member of
the family of nations, has experienced five distinct waves
of expansion. There has been no real halt at any time in
the rising tide of Japanese expansion since the consolida-
tion of the country, but there were waves of expansion, fol-
lowed by periods of inactivity, in 1875-1879, 1894-1895,
1904-1905, 1914-1922, and 1931 to 1933.
In the period from 1875 to 1879, Japan, then only be-
ginning to feel itself as a distinct entity after the restora-
tion of power to the Emperor, took steps (none of which re-
quired any considerable show of military force) which re-
sulted in the acquisition by Japan of the Lu Chu Islands,
the Kurile Islands and the Bonin Islands. The Lu Chu Islands
had formerly been a primitive state, paying tribute both to
Japan

=== Page 33 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
Japan and to China, but during the period from 1875 to 1879
Japan brought pressure upon the native royal family, causing
it to disclaim all allegiance to China, and in 1879 the
group of islands was formally incorporated into the Japanese
Empire, the islands being made a Japanese prefecture and
the ruling family being given a Japanese title of nobility.
The Kurile chain of islands was obtained from Russia in 1875
in exchange for Japan's nebulous claims to the southern
half of the island of Saghalien. In 1878 Japan formally
annexed the Bonin Islands, then not in the actual possession
of any Power, although Great Britain had previously laid
claim to the islands.
The wave of expansion then subsided, and for nearly two
decadesJapan concentrated its efforts upon the consolidation
of the state and the strengthening of its military power. For
further territorial expansion was impossible without conflict
with one of Japan's two great mainland neighbors - China and
Russia. During this period, however, Japan was working
steadily but quietly to detach Korea, then a kingdom tribu-
tary to China, from that vast Empire.
In 1894 and 1895 came the next great wave of expansion,
during which Japan fought China, emerged from the struggle
victorious, and obtained as the spoils of war the large is-
land of Formosa, the Pescadores, and the tip of the Liaotung
Peninsula in Manchuria, although this last acquisition had
to be retroceded to China after the joint intervention of
Russia, Germany and France. By far the most important gain
to Japan, however, lay in the renunciation by China of all
claim

=== Page 34 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
claim to suzerainty over the kingdom of Korea - a step
which led gradually to the annexation of that kingdom by
Japan in 1910.
The wave of expansion then subsided, and for ten years
Japan concentrated its efforts upon building up a military
force capable of dealing with the Russian Empire, the next
obstacle to further expansion. In 1904 and 1905 came the
Russo-Japanese War - the next wave of expansion - and as a
result Japan drove Russian influence out of Korea and South
Manchuria and acquired the Russian rights to the tip of the
Liaotung Peninsula, the South Manchuria Railway, with its
Railway Zone, mines and other properties, and the southern
half of the island of Saghalien. The most important gain
to Japan, however, lay in the fact that it obtained, as a
result of the war, a foothold in Manchuria.
Again for a period of ten years the expansionist wave
subsided, and during this period Japan consolidated its former
gains, including the final step of annexing Korea. In 1914
began, as a result of Japan's participation in the World War,
the next great wave of expansion. In 1914 Japan took posses-
sion of the German islands in the South Sea - the Mariana,
Marshall and Caroline groups - and the German leased terri-
tory in the province of Shantung in China. In 1915 Japan
presented to China the famous 2l Demands", which would have
made virtually a Japanese protectorate of China had they
been accepted in full. Because of the intervention of the
United States and Great Britain, however, Japan obtained
only a few minor concessions, such as the extension of the
1ease

=== Page 35 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
lease to the tip of the Liaotung Peninsula to ninety-nine
years. In 1918 Japan joined the Allied and Associated
Powers in occupying Eastern Siberia, but Japan refused
to withdraw its troops when the other nations involved in
the joint intervention withdrew theirs. The troops likewise
stayed in Shantung Province after the close of the war. In
1920 Japanese troops occupied the Russian half of Saghalien
as a reprisal for the massacre of resident Japanese in Niko-
laievsk. It seemed reasonably certain at that time that
the Japanese military intended, if in any way possible, to
retain control in some form of the former German possessions
in Shantung, of the Maritime Province of Siberia, and of
Russian Saghalien. The force of world opinion, however,
combined with domestic pressure against the military and
the lack of funds, caused the expansionist wave to recede
without the acquisition of the territory which had been oc-
cupied. The Japanese troops withdrew from Siberia and Shan-
tung in 1922, but continued to occupy Russian Saghalien
until 1925. Japan's only territorial gain from the World
War was, therefore, the mandated right to the former German
South Sea Islands north of the Equator, although Japan ac-
quired economic rights in Shantung and obtained some con-
cessions from China in regard to Manchuria as a result of
the Twenty-One Demands of 1915.
For some nine years thereafter the wave of expansion re-
ceded, although unwillingly and under compulsion. Twice
during the nine years the Japanese military made tentative
clutches at Chinese territory, on both occasions invading
andoccupying Shantung for brief periods, but again pressure
from

=== Page 36 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
from within Japan, as well as from outside, drove them out.
In l93l, however, the repressed forward movement of
the expansionist wave was too great for the restraining
forces of Japan's statesmen and of world opinion, and
Japanese troops occupied Manchuria, detached the territory
from the Republic of China, and set up the fictional "state
of Manchukuo". This was followed in early 1932 by the tem-
porary occupation of a part of Shanghai and in 1933 by the
occupation and annexation to "Manchukuo" of Jehol. Later
in the same year an excursion by the Japanese troops to the
gates of Peiping resulted in the consolidation of "Manchu-
kuo" territory down to the Great Wall and in the establish-
ment of a demilitarized zone south of the Wall.
In this last wave of expansion the Japanese Army (which
has usually been the principal instrument of Japanese imperial.
ism) maintained its expansionist activities for about two
years, from September, 1931, to the summer of 1933; since
then its activities, at least on the Asiatic mainland, have
decreased. Does this fact signify that the latest wave of
expansion has spent its force and is now receding?
Examining the subject on the basis of the duration of
previous waves of expansion, it will be found that the first
wave lasted for four years, from 1875 to 1879, after which
came an interval of quiescence of fifteen years, until 1894.
The next wave lasted for only a year (1894-1895) and was
succeeded by nine quiet years, until 1904. The next wave
(1904-1905) also lasted only about a year, and again was
followed by nine years of quiescence, until 1914. The next
wave of expansion lasted for about eleven years, from 1914
to 1925, during all of which time Japanese troops were in
occupation

=== Page 37 === (confidence 0.99)
-7 -
occupation of alien soil. This wave was followed by some
six years of reduced activity, until the latest wave started
in 1931.
These figures reveal only the fact that there appears
to be no definite duration of the waves of expansion, which
have in the past lasted from one to eleven years. The dura-
tion of a wave of expansion appears rather to depend upon
the length of time taken to achieve the immediate objective
of the wave, than upon any fixed law of periodicity. Thus
in 1894-5 and in 1904-5 the objectives were attained quick-
ly by the defeat of China and Russia, respectively, and by
the cession to Japan by those countries of the territories
or concessions which were desired by the Japanese. The wave
of expansion which started in 1914, however, endured for
eleven years, because the Japanese military, who apparently
were determined to acquire something substantial for Japan
out of the welter of world political conditions, found their
moves blocked in every instance (except in the acquisition
of the German South Sea islands) by internal or external
forces.
Some law of periodicity does appear to apply, however,
to the intervals between the subsidence of one wave of ex-
pansion and the rise of the next. It will be observed that
there was an interval of 15 years between the first and second
waves, of 9 years between the second and third, of 9 years
between the third and fourth, and of 6 years between the
fourth and fifth. The intervals between the waves are grow-
ing shorter, apparently because of the increasing pressure
of

=== Page 38 === (confidence 0.99)
- 8 -
of the rising tide of expansion.
The figures presented above indicate that a wave of
expansion recedes either when the immediate objective has
been attained or when the wave has spent its strength
against opposing forces, external or domestic. The pro-
blem then arises of endeavoring to determine the character
of the objective of each wave, and the character and strength
of the opposing forces, before any attempt can be made to
calculate the peak and probable time of recession of a wave.
As a general proposition it may be stated that the waves
of expansion in Japanese history are fundamentally due to
economic pressure at home. Military ambition and the need
for the military to justify their existence, as well as cer-
tain highly idealistic and carefully inculcated theories of
the "manifest destiny" of the Japanese people, play their
part in the formation of the expansionist waves, but behind
it all lies the urge of the Japanese people for more economic
opportunity. As has been pointed out in previous despatches,
the natural resources of Japan, although larger than usually
believed, are limited; the population of the country is in-
creasing at an absurd rate; the people are vigorous, in-
telligent and progressive; the standard of living is rising
in consonance with the ambitions of the people; and indus-
trialization of the country has not yet succeeded in assur-
ing livings for the rapidly increasing population. These
factors have resulted in a primal urge for more economic
elbow-room and have always gained the support of the mass of
the people for the expansionist activities of the Army. The
urge was satisfied for the time being by the acquisition
of the Bonin and Lu Chu Islands, then Formosa and the Pesca-
dores, then Southern Saghalien, the Kwantung Leased Territory
and

=== Page 39 === (confidence 0.99)
- 9 -
and the South Manchuria Railway; then Korea, and then the
German South Sea Islands and economic concessions in Shan-
tung and Manchuria. It is now reasonable to assume that
Manchuria will satisfy the urge for economic opportunity of
the Japanese people for some time to come. Manchuria is a
vast land, with considerable undeveloped natural resources
which the Japanese can exploit, even though they cannot hope
to colonize the land in the face of competition from Chinese
already there. There is no doubt that the Japanese military,
the real rulers of "Manchukuo", are making every effort to
place the whole of the undeveloped resources, as well as the
industry and trade, of Manchuria in the hands of the Japanese.
With these circumstances in mind, it is not unreasonable to
assume that Manchuria will absorb the surplus economic energy
of Japan for several years to come, and thus will divert,
for the time being, the basic urge which drives the Japanese
nation into waves of expansion. It can also be assumed that
the immediate objective of the wave of expansion which started
in 193l, the securing of the economic opportunities of Manchu-
ria for the Japanese people, has been attained.
At the same time, the internal forces which oppose the
expansionist waves are growing in strength. Military ambi-
tion, one of the impulses to an expansionist wave, tends to
defeat itself, at least temporarily, by its excesses. Fired
by their success in Manchuria, the Japanese military are de-
manding more and more of the Japanese people, and opposition
to their demands is slowly developing. Their excessive ex-
penditures evoked considerable criticism in the recent Diet
session, and doubts are beginning to creep into the minds
of

=== Page 40 === (confidence 0.99)
- 10 -
of the people in regard to the reality of the much-advertised
"crisis of 1935 and 1936". Thus the need for economy is
becoming a force opposing itself to the excessive activities
of the Army.
The force of liberal sentiment is also tending to be-
come stronger, in opposition to the chauvinist sentiment
inculcated by the military. There is little doubt that the
present Minister for Foreign Affairs advocates a return to
the methods of the "Shidehara diplomacy", utillzing friend-
ship instead of force in dealing with China and cooperation
instead of isolation in dealing with the Western nations.
The rumored possible appointment of Mr. Hachiro Arita, a
pronounced liberal, as Japanese Minister to China, is an in-
dication of the strength of the liberal forces opposing
themselves to further chauvinism. It cannot be said that
this liberal sentiment entirely excludes the conception of
the "manifest destiny" of thc Japanese people - the concep-
tion of the vast superiority of the Japanese over other races
and of their destiny to bring a "pax Japonica" to the Far
East and perhaps to the world. It is possible that Mr. Hirota
and othcr liberals,as well as such exponents of the theory as
General Araki, honestly entertain this idea, but the liberals
believe in accomplishing the result by non-military means.
When,therefore, the facts that the immediate objective
of the latest wave of expansion, the securing of Manchuria
as a field for the surplus economic energies of the Japanese
people, has been attained, and that the internal forces in
opposition to further military activities are growing in
strength, are borne in mind, it is not unreasonable to assume
that

=== Page 41 === (confidence 0.98)
-11-
that the wave of expansion which started in l93l has reached
its furthest point and is now receding. The possibility
always exists, however, that the Japanese military, in
order to maintain their position of predominance in the
affairs of Japan, may plunge the nation into catastrophic
excesses. If this can be avoided, it appears probable that
there may be a period of some six to ten years of quiescence
in Japanese expansionist activities, while the nation is
absorbing and digesting its opportunities in Manchuria,
after which another wave of expansion may be expected to
arise.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

S i r:
In informal conversation with the Minister for
Foreign Affairs after the dinner which he gave on
April 10 for the American Economic Mission, Mr. Hirota
told me that when he had recently announced that there
would never be war while he was in office he had rather
expected an outburst from some of the reactionaries,
but the truth was no one dared to raise a voice because
they

=== Page 56 === (confidence 1.00)
6-2
-2-
they knew that he was directly expressing the views
of the Emperor. Mr. Hirota said that there were
reactionary elements in Japan but that the "democratic"
elements were stronger and were growing in strength.
The political parties are looking for a leader who
will command the confidence of the public and he said
that he was today approached by one of the parties
in this connection; he however refused positively to
have anything to do with party politics because he
must represent the nation and must therefore keep out
of domestic politics and avoid making political enemies
or having political ambitions. He emphasized his
conviction that the "democratic" influence in the
country is steadily growing in strength.
While there is some talk of a waning influence
on the part of the military, I am not yet prepared to
confirm Mr. Hirota's stated conviction that the so-
called "democratic" influence in Japan is steadily
growing in strength. The Embassy in due course will
endeavor to analyze this situation.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

Tokyo's despatch No. 1279 of May 3,
1935:
In commenting on a previous despatch
from Mr. Grew, in which reference was made
to an affirmation by the Japanese Minister
for Foreign Affairs that the liberal ele-
ments in Japan were growing in strength,
the Far Eastern Division stated that, in
its opinion, there was reason to give
credence to Mr. Hirota's assurance that
he is endeavoring to solve Japan's inter-
national problems by peaceful means; that
the reactionary element in Japan was
apparently giving Mr. Hirota an opportunity
to seek solution of such problems by
peaceful means, but without sacrifice of
the objectives which the reactionary ele-
ment has in mind; and that if Mr. Hirota
should overstep the limits within which the
reactionary element is permitting him to
operate, the reactionary element would
immediately reassert its authority.
The foregoing view is substantially
confirmed by the Embassy in the appended
despatch, which should be read if time
permits.
The

=== Page 59 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
The Embassy concludes that despite
the apparent trend of Japanese policy away
from war towards peaceful methods, and
despite the increasing influence of
Mr. Hirota and his conciliatory policies,
there has been no abandonment of the gains
of the previous aggressive regimes; there
has occurred no decline of military
preeminence in the Government, and there
is no evidence of a growth of democratic
influences in the American sense.
Any shift of policy which may have
occurred is due to a feeling that a more
restrained policy will allow scope at the
present time for Japanese interests.
Mr. Hirota's warless program is being
given a trial because he has persuaded
those in power that he can attain the de-
sired objectives without war, at less cost.
The Embassy suggests that a tempering
of the methods of Japan's foreign policy,
without material change in its aims, is
what lies ahead. Consequently the Embassy
thinks it unwise to predicate American
policy in the Far East on the hope of
democratic developments in Japan.

EMBASSY OF THE

Subject:
Analysis of the Alleged Strengthening of
the Liberal Element in Japan.
Cofaegin FE (301)
JUN 3-1935
894.00
1935
PM :ECO
OF EASTERN
894.00/544
EUROPEAN AFFAIRS
JUH 3- 1935
OF STATE
For Distribu:ion-Check
No
Grade
Tofi 1.1

CONFIDENTIAL
TheHonorable
JUN 6 - 1935
ASSISTANT SEOTARY
EPARTMENT OF

The Secretary of State,
OF STATE
Washington.
I have the honor to submit the following analysis in
543
S
anglification of my despatch No. 1255/of April 20, 1935,
T
othe subject of the alleged strengthening of the liberal
element in Japan. It will be recalled that that despatch
reported a conversation with Foreign Minister Hirota in
which he stated that the absence of any reactionary out-
burst to his recent announcement of no war while he is in
office is a result of the common knowledge that he was
direetly

=== Page 61 === (confidence 0.98)
7-2.
-2 -
directly expressing the views of the Emperor; that
Mr. Hirota asserted that the democratic influence in the
country is steadily growing in strength; and that, in
spite of the Embassy's inability to confirm the Foreign
Minister's stated conviction, an analysis of the situation
would be attempted in due course.
A treatment of this subject which suggests itself
is to consider the three questions (1) is Japanese policy
less inclined toward war? (2) is the military losing in-
fluence? and (3) is democratic strength growing?
The trend of events in Japan in recent months has
been away from a policy of war. The purchase of the Soviet
interest in the Chinese Eastern Railway was effected peaceably,
and in the press aftermath of the successful negotiations
a tone of moderation and quiet in the relations of Japan
and Russia has contrasted conspicuously with the inflam-
ing tactics previously employed. Barga border incidents
have been handled cautiously, penetration in Chahar has
advanced without bluster, and in north and south China
an improved Japanese position is sought by means of economic
and political understandings. As for publicity against
the United States Navy, for some time (except for an out-
burst of the last few days) the newspapers have not played
up antagonistic items. The tenor of the press is clearly
on the side of the methods of peace.
Nor is there cause for believing that this new ex-
terior hides an inner belligerent intention. So long
as the chief objectives are brought appreciably closer
ithout

=== Page 62 === (confidence 0.97)
7-3
- 3 -
without fighting, war is unnecessary. Japanese policy
appears less inclined to war than it did a year ago, and
this waning of the bellicose spirit seems mor than
merely superficial.
To find Japan desirous of avoiding war, however, is
quite different from asserting that the military is
losing influence. The evidence points to a continuing
preeminence on the part of the military. Certainly
the resignation of Prime Minister Saito and the succession
(Kudak
OBF
of Admiral Okada to his position, with the support of
two uncompromising men in the present cabinet as ministers
(Hay25%)
of war and of the navy, does not argue a movement toward
civilian power. It should be remembered that the dominant
military group, notably idealistic and convinced of its
responsibility for the nation's destiny, thinks in terms
of ambitious plans which comprise no less than the future
of the Empire. With so extensive a self-assumed field
of action, justification for its existence and importance
is not lacking even under a warless program. While
peaceful methods are the methods now employed, the change
of tone need. not mean that the guidance of affairs is pass-
ing from the military to the civilian, and facts to indicate
such a shift are absent. Continuing direction by the mili-
tary is, furthermore, favored by the constitution of Japan
and the psychology of the people.
Mr. Hirota has apparently convinced those in power
that he can reach the Government's objectives without war,
at less cost. He is being given a try because the pro-
posal has plausibility, not because the army is in
eclipse.

=== Page 63 === (confidence 0.98)
7-4
-4-
eclipse. Under Mr. Hirota's administration there has
been no abandonment of the gains of the previous aggressive
regimes. There has been no retreat. Japan's foreign
policy, in fact, maintains a strong arm under his guidance,
in spite of his protestations of gentleness.
Whether a policy of peace will later lead to a de-
cline in the influence of the military, which to regain
its position might then turn in the direction of war, is
a question for the future. The present despatch is con-
cerned only with reporting existing conditions.
As for the specific question of whether democratic
influences in Japan are increasing in strength, observers
here would probably be unanimous in answering in the negative.
Whatever the changes in political control, they have not
thus far enhanced the powers of the people. The recent Diet
was singularly outspoken, and a new tone adversely critical
of the government and military was audible; but its efforts
were strikingly ineffective. The Foreign Minister, however,
probably had in mind a slightly different type of develop-
ment when he spoke of the growing strength of the democratic
elements, and to understand the statement a brief biographi-
cal digression is necessary.
Mr. Hirota is a native of Fukuoka, where he was born
of a family of humble estate. As a young man he became a
member of a patriotic society called the Kokuryu Kai, an
NGF
organization which had its origin in Fukuoka at the time of
the Russo-Japanese war of 1904-1905, under the 1eadership
CBF
ofMitsuru Toyamaand Ryuhei Uchida, both of whom have
since
NP

=== Page 64 === (confidence 0.98)
7-s
- 5 -
since become conspicuous public figures in the chauvinist
movement. An aim of the society was Japanese expansion
in Manchuria. Throughout his distinguished career Mr.
Hirota has maintained his membership and still continues
the habit of consulting the leaders of the organization.
This relation now works both ways. When Mr. Hirota tells
the Kokuryu Kai that Japan is not in danger and that
rabidly chauvinistic tactics are not necessary, he is
able through the confidence which he enjoys at the society's
hands to restrain its activities. At the same time Mr.
Hirota's weight in governmental matters is enormously
increased through the support of this powerful society.
This is a new situation in Japanese politics. For the
first time, a foreign minister who is not a soldier is
nevertheless in a powerful position, having the organized
backing of a strong civilian society. This is in fact an
innovation which should not be ignored, although Mr. Hirota's
reference to it as a growth in democratic trend is far from
exact from an American point of view. Mr. Hirota's rela-
tion to the Kokuryu Kai also helps explain his other remarks
about refusing the invitation of a pclitical party which
is in search of a popular leader. Like other Japanese
patriotic societies, the Kokuryu Kai probably has very
little faith in the political parties, and Mr. Hirota very
likely does not envisage his future political career in
such terms. He has been singularly successful in obtain-
ing general confidence, and it would be strange if the
frequently-suggested idea of his becoming the nation's
next prime minister were repugnant to him. His conversa-
tional remarks indicate that his judgement is against party
politics

=== Page 65 === (confidence 0.99)
76
- 6 -
politics as the course to this goal, and in this decision
the present state of affairs in Japan bears out his
shrewdness.
It is worthy of note that Mr. Hirota is fully con-
scious of the unique position in which he finds him-
self as a result of his membership in the Kokuryu Kai.
In private conversation with me shortly after he assumed
office, he discussed the fact with frankness and stated
that in his opinion the support of the Kokuryu Kai would
prove a factor of considerable weight in increasing his
political importance. He realizes that his unusual back-
ing is a strong lever in dealing with his colleagues in
and out of the government. The months that he has been
in office have produced many instances to show that his
expectation in this respect, as stated in his conversation
with me, was justified.
Mr. Hirota's influence increases rather than de-
creases. He has publicly announced that there will be
no war while he is in office, and no outburst followed
this announcement. But at the same time the military
group is not shelved. Many Japanese leaders no doubt
now realize in retrospect that the country's difficulties
since 193l have been multiplied by an unnecessary over-
riding of treaties. If a more restrained policy allows
scope for Japanese interests without the costs of the last
four years, the military party is sufficiently opportunist
to give it a trial. It may well be that a tempering of the
methods of Japan's foreign policy, without significant
change

=== Page 66 === (confidence 0.97)
クーク
- 7 -
二
change in its aims, is what lies ahead.
It would seem unwise to predicate American policy
in the Far East on the hope of democratic developments
in this country. The military should be kept in mind as
still the strongest single element in Japanese affairs.
But the fact that the leaders are persons of military
training is incidental only. They are not primarily
soldiers but are strong nationalists who, in a country
in which the army is a popular institution which offers a
career to talent, happen to have been trained as soldiers.
They have no aversion to making use of non-military men
such as Mr. Hirota when there is coincidence of nationalistic
aim and reasonable promise of success.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

Tokyo's 1332 of May 31, 1935, reports
the creation by Imperial Crdinance on
May 10 of two new advisory bodies of the
Japanese Government, both subsidiary to the
Cabinet.
The first of trese bodies may be termed
the Cabinet Deliberative Council [Naikaku
Shingi Kai], and is composed of about 17
members all appointed by the Emperor. It
is apparently designed to act as a sort of
board of directors for the Government.
Appointees to this Council, of which the
Premier is ex-officio president, are all
of Cabinet calibre. Political considera-
tions appear to have had influence in
determining their choice, as membership
has been distributed among the three
political parties and peers, and includes
representatives of Mitsui and Mitsubishi.
Neither the armymor the navy is represented.
The second body, called the Cabinet
Investigation Bureau [Naikaku Chosa Kyoku],
is apparently to be nominally subordinate
to the first-named body, but in actual
practice may prove to be more powerful.
Shigeru Yoshida is the director, and
membership is to include army and navy
Lale Amtamsade lo ray
officers,

=== Page 69 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
officers, albeit of relatively low rank.
The men named have been selected more
for their qualifications as experts than
for their rank, and are largely taken from
the bureaucracy.
The raison etre of these bodies seems
to be a need of some coordinating agency
to advise the Government on national plans.
Public dissatisfaction with representative
government and political parties lies be-
hind the creation of these organs. Undoubt-
edly there is a feeling of need for some
high authority to take the place of the
Genro in advising the Government on the
nation's policies.
Note that the army and navy are re-
presented on the Investigation Council,
but not on the Deliberative Council. This
suggests that the military desire to be
directly informed regarding all the
technical research connected with national
planning, but do not wish to be committed
by the decisions of the political advisers.
It is doubtful whether the Government
itself has a clear idea of the functions
and future of these two bodies, and it is
probable that their future will depend
upon the degree of their usefulness. All
sources

=== Page 70 === (confidence 0.96)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 3 -
sources of information agree that the
functions of these bodies remain to be
worked out in practice.

CABINET DELIBERATIVE BODY AND CABIN

O.N.I. AND M. I.D
The Hororaple
R
2
A-C
ecretary of State,
Tashington.
sir
have the honor to report the creation on May 10,1935,
of two new advisory bodies of the Japanese government, both
subsidiary to the cabinet, designated respectively as the
Naikaku Shingi Kai and the Naikaku. chosa Kyoku. Naikaku
JUL 11
FILED
Shingi Kai is translated literally as "cabinet deliberative
body" and is rendered in the English language newspapers by
a number of titles including mational policy counoil",
"cabinet advisory board", and "cabinet inquiry council";
usage has not yet become uniform. Naikaku Chosa Kyoku is
translated "cabinet investigation bureau".
The Cabinet Deliberative Body (Naikaku Shingi Kai) was
established

=== Page 72 === (confidence 0.99)
8-2
-2-
established by Japanese imperial ordinance number 118 of
May 10, 1935, published in the official gazette on the
following day. The ordinance states that the body shall
be under the direction of the cabinet, and shall investigate
and deliberate upon such important policies as may be refer
red to it by the cabinet. The premier is president, the
Emperor designates a cabinet minister as vice president, and
the other members (who shall be not more than fifteen) are
also appointed by the Emperor. The premier is given au-
thority to determine rules for the submission of questions
to the body. The final article of the ordinance states that
the general affairs (shomu) of the Cabinet Deliberative Body
shall be managed by the Cabinet Investigation Bureau. This
article leaves much to be desired in definiteness of meaning.
The word shomu is the same word which is used in the title
of various general affairs bureaus of departments of the
"Manchukuo" government, where the experience of the last few
years has been that these bureaus are the powerful policy-
determining organs of the departments to which they are nomi-
nally subordinate.
The official gazette for May 13, 1935, announced the
appointment of the finance minister, Mr. Takahashi, to the
position of vice president of the Cabinet Deliberative Body,
and named fifteen other men as its members, These are
Viscount Admiral Saito, former premier; Takio Izawa, formerly
governor general of Taiwan, Eiichi Baba, former president of
the Iegislative bureau, Viscount Nobumitsu Aoki, and Baron
Nagatoshi Kuroda, all four members of the house of peers;
Seihin Ikeda, managing director of the Mitsui interests;
Kenkichi

=== Page 73 === (confidence 0.99)
83
Kenkichi Kagami, president of the Nippon Yusen Kaisha and
member of the house of peers, who is associated with the
Mitsubishi interests; four Minseito members in the persons
0M
of Baron Tatsuo Yamamoto, former cabinet minister, Takukichi
Kawasaki, member of the house of peers, formerly chief cab-
inet secretary and president of the legislative bureau,
Keikichi Tanomogi, former parliamentary vice minister of
communications, and Kojiro Tomita, member of the lower house;
Kenzo Adachi of the Kokumin Domei party; and three Seiyukai
men who have lost their party membership by reason of serv-
ing under the present administration contrary to Seiyukai
orders: Rentaro Mizuno, former cabinet member, Keisuke
Mochizuki, also a former cabinet member, and Kiyoshi Akita,
former speaker of the lower house and parliamentary vice
minister of communications.
At the present time, then, the Cabinet Deliberative
Body, under the leadership of the premier and the finance
minister, consists of the former premier, four distinguished
peers, one representative each for the two largest business
interests, four leaders of the Minseito party, the leader
of the smaller Kokumin Domei party, and three politically
experienced men whose cooperativeness with the government
has cost them their membership in the Seiyukai party. The
appointees are of cabinet caliber. Experience in politics
is their common qualification, and political considerations
appear to have stood high in determining their choice. The
body stands as a sort of board of directors for the government.
Neither the army or the navy is represented.
The Cabinet Deliberative Body held its first meeting on
May

=== Page 74 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-8-4
9
May 17, l935. The session was given over to matters of
organization. Cabinet members were in attendance, as is
their privilege under the ordinance creating the body. A
regular monthly meeting was decided upon.
Meantime the Cabinet Investigation Bureau has pró-
gressed more slowly. Established by imperial ordinance
number 119 of May 10, 1935 (published in the official
gazette of May ll), its work is defined as the investigation
of important policies, the study of proposed governmental
actions as directed by the premier, and the general affairs
(shomu) of the Cabinet Deliberative Body. Its most important
power is the authority to call upon any department of the
government for any information required. The bureau is
stated to be under the premier's direction and to comprise
a director, a secretary, an assistant secretary, fifteen
investigators, and twenty low-rank members. The bureau is
to maintain a standing executive committee and also a
standing committee of experts. The normal term of service is
two years. Shigeru Yoshida resigned fron his position as
chief secretary of the cabinet to accept appointment as
director of the Cabinet Investigation Bureau (official
gazette, May 13, 1935). The official gazette for May 27
announced the following appointment as investigator:
Tatsuo Yamada and Hideo Matsukuma (finance department),
N1
Yasuya Kohama and Hiroo Wada (department of agriculture and
forestry), Kuninosuke Fujita (industry rationalization bureau),
Issei Tinuma (governor of Saitama prefecture), Keinosuke
Nakamura and Shigeyuki Tanaka (home affairs department),
Gembei Uchida (national resources bureau), Kiwao Okumura
(communications department), Shin Hashii (department of
commerce and industry), Mikine Kuwabara (Tohoku rehabili-
tation

=== Page 75 === (confidence 0.98)
8-5
-5-
●
OGF
tation bureax), Colonel Sadaichi Suzuki (army), and
Captain Kisuke Abe (navy). Several features of these
appointments should be noted. First, the men named
have been chosen more for their qualifications as
specialists than for their rank. Second, they are taken
from government circles rather than from the outside, and
to that extent the bureau is an organ of the bureaucracy.
Third, the army and navy are represented, but by officers
of relatively low rank.
The ordinence setting up the Cabinet Investigation
Bureau mentions that its composition shall include also
twenty low-rank members. The appointment of these members
has not yet been announced, nor are the choices of parti-
cular importance. As defined by the civil service regula-
tions of Japan, these members can be expected to be persons
of annual salary of fifteen hundred to two thousand yen
whose service will be in assisting the researches of the
investigators. The bureau, in fact, is so organized that
the investigators heve ample assistance to make their work
really effective. A first formal meeting was held on May
30 but no business was done.
The tendency so conspicuous in recent years for states
to develop nationalistically and for their governments to
superimpose new agencies of national planning to meet the
urgent need of coordination has blossomed in Japan in the
Cabinet Deliberative Body and the Cabinet Investigation
Bureau. Both advise the cabinet. The deliberative body
is a political board of directors whose advice on national
policy is valued. The investigation bureau is a commission
of experts, organized for active research in technical matters
growing

=== Page 76 === (confidence 0.99)
8-6
-6-
8
growing out of national policy. National planning is its
field.
Influences other than the necessity of nationally
planned coordination in economic activities have contri-
buted to the present innovation in Japan. Public dissat-
isfaction with the manner in which the political parties have
operated is well known, and this has had its effect in bring-
ing the new organs into existence. At the time of the pro-
mulgation of the constitution in 1889 Japan was not ready for
representative government in reality, and a sense that extra-
constitutional institutions must be looked to for guidance
has never since completely disappeared, although there could
be no reasonable hope of duplicating the remarkable group of
elder statesmen of the Meiji period. Particularly since
193l the prestige of the political parties has suffered.
Partly because of their notoriously bad record, partly because
of military pressure against them, and partly because of the
ingrained distrust of representative government as an alien
thing, the political parties have been losing such popular
faith as they previously had possessed. The Okada cabinet,
it will be recalled, is not a cabinet of the majority party
of the lower house. of the 4ö6 seats of the lower house, the
Seiyukai at the present time holds 257 but has not been given
an opportunity to form the government, and because of this
treatment it has not permitted its members to accept cabinet
positions. Several Seiyukai men who did so were read out of
the party.
It is difficult to see how the Seiyukai could be com-
plaisant. The party's vision is no doubt accurate when it sees
danger in the new bodies. Whether the Minseito, which is
lending

=== Page 77 === (confidence 0.97)
8-7
-7-
8
lending ts support, will later regret its action remains
for the future to answer.
The army and navy have meantime stepped cautiously.
As enemies of the parties they are in principle favorably
inclined to non-party governnental bodies, but at the same
time both the army and the navy would probably prefer to
rely directly on their privileged position under the con-
stitution than on these new institutions. The fact that the
army and navy are represented in the investigation bureau and
not in the deliberative body suscests that they desire to be
directly informed regardin: all the technical research con-
nected with national plannin but do not wish to be committed
by the decisions or the new organ of political advisers as they
would be by implication ir participating in both. They are
by nature suspicious of politics and unsure of their ability
to cope with politiciens on the politicians' ground. The
army end navy ap ear to be willing to vork with the experts
but not with the p. iticians.
Muny ques ions about the Cabinet Deliberative Body and
the Cubinet Investigation Bureau can be answered only with the
passage of time. Starting with a very real need for coordi-
nating the work of the various departments, for which the
cabinet was deemed inadequate, the overnment has launched
two new bodies and is feeling out the way for them. They
have been created simply by the Emperor's ordinance power,
presumably in accordence with an article of the constitution
which states that the Emperor determines the organization
of the difierent branches of the administration. Not con-
templated in the constitution, these important organs have
been established at a time when their operation can be
expected

=== Page 78 === (confidence 0.97)
8-
C
-8-
expected to have serious effect upon the development of
political parties in Japan. On the other hand, they could
be as simply terminated when the need for their existence
is considered to be past. It is doubtful whether the
government itself has a clear idea of the future of these
bodies, and it is probable that their future will depend
upon the degree of their success. Public statements of
officials and others, the uncerteinty of the press, con-
versational remarks to members of the mbassy staff --
all confirm the impression that the functions of the two
new organs remain to be worked out in practice.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

Enclosure No. 1 to despatck
Embassy at Tokyo.
Conversation
June 6, 1935.
Mr. Neville and ur. Kopf, Manager
of the Ford Motor Co., Yokohama.
Ford Motor Co.
Mr. Kopf came to see me at about 10:30. He said that
he wanted to tell me of his company's plans and the dirri-
culties he had been meeting with. He said that he did not
wish the Embassy to do anything, as he felt that any sort
of intervention would probably do more harm than good, but
he would appreciate talking the matter over. He said that
he was afraid that there might be some publicity, possibly
of an unfriendly nature, and he would prefer to have the
Embassy know the facts from him direct. I told him I should
be glad to help him in any way I could.
Mr. Kopf said that the Ford Company had been negotiating
with the City of Yokohama for the purchase of additional
land. He said that the company was contemplating consider-
able additions to their plant and that their present site
had insufficient space for the efficient operation of the
projects they had in mind. The negotiations had been pro-
ceeding satisfactorily, when he had been notified that the
city had decided not to sell the property. No reason was
given for the decision. Mr. Kopf said that he had there-
upon opened negotiations with the Asano interests whe have
developed a large traet of made land on the bay between
Yokohama and Tokyo. The piece of land which appealed to
the Ford Company was located near the mouth of the river
at Tsurumi. Mr. Kopf said that he was about to exeroise the
option, pay the money and take over the property, when the
representative

=== Page 83 === (confidence 0.99)
-8
representative (Mr. Komatsu) of the Asane interests eams
to see him and said that Asano had been told by the war
Department not to sell the property to Ford. Mr. Kopf
said that of course he had a claim for damages against
Asano, but he had no intention of bringing suit, as what
he wanted was the land, and asked me my views on the whole
situation.
I told him that I was not surprised by what had taken
place. I said further that in my judgment he would gain
nothing by hasty action; that what he needed was patience;
that if it was any satisfaction to him I could tell him
that I had heard of other cases similar to his own, and
that the authorities were not "picking" on the Ford Company.
I reminded him that ever since the opening of the country to
foreign intercourse there has beea instances of opposition
to foreign enterprises in Japan, and that the present was
a time when waves of nationalism were running high. I said
that matters would quiet down if given time, in my opinion.
He then said that those were his own views, more or less;
that he was prepared to wait, and that he felt sure he could
accomplish his purposes in time. I asked him whether the new
Japanese motor cars and trucks were giving him much competi-
tion. He said that he did not think so. He said that the
Army specifications for trucks made them impracticable for
general commercial use, as did the Government specifications
for bus chassis. He said that Ford, generally speaking, was
not interested in other peoples: specirications; that Ford
was prepared te design vehicles for a specific use if there
was

=== Page 84 === (confidence 0.99)
3
was a commercial need for them, but not te alter its
engineering to meet some engineer's design; it was tee
expensive, and what he wanted was volume business, with
gradual changes to meet new conditions.
On taking leave he said that if worst came to worst,
and he could not get new land, he was prepared to put addi-
tional stories on his present plant; it could be done, he
said, but of course the operation would not be as efricient
as a plant of the general Ford type, laid out flat.

REMOVAL OF GENERAL MASAKI.

COPIES SENT TO
O.N.I. AND M. I.D
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
S1r:
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's telegram
793.94/7186
No. 151 of July 16, 1935, 4 p.m., on the subject of the
FILED
removal from office of General Jinsaburo Masaki as in
4
35
army, and to submit the present despatch in amplification.
The telegram was to the effect that General Masaki had
been removed from his duties because of differences with
Minister of War Hayashi regarding forthcoming assignments
and promotions, and that Foreign Minister Hirota in con-
versation with Ambassador Grew on July 16 attached signi-
F月
ficance to the removal as evidencing the slowly expanding
control

=== Page 88 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
control by the government over unruly elements in the
army. The Embassy's present purpose is to explain
General Masaki's removal and to appraise Mr. Hirota's
estimate of its significance.
Announcement of General Masaki's removal was made
by the War Office at half past one on the afternoon of
July 16. On the same day the evening edition of the
KOKUMIN, a Tokyo newspaper which is reportedly subsidized
by the Japanese army, referred to this news and stated
that there have been two aims held by the Minister of War,
General Hayashi, during his incumbency, namely to unify
and effectuate control of the army and to place capable
men in important positions; that in pursuit of these
aims General Hayashi recently drafted a schedule of ex-
tensive changes in the army, for announcement in August;
and that, in attempting to carry out this program, General
Hayashi came to realize the necessity of removing General
Masaki from office.
All the newspapers featured the subject, and support
was surprisingly general for the Minister of War against
General Masaki in what was represented as a momentous
personal feud. Imperial sanction was obtained, and the
Supreme War Council met and concurred in General Hayashi's
action. Meantime the newspapers were unofficially publish-
ing the list of August promotions and assignments which
had precipitated the Hayashi-Masaki difference. This
list, although unofficial, can be relied upon for substantial
accuracy because it originates from a War Office release,
n

=== Page 89 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
in accordance with a practice established when the army
discovered some time ago that when such lists are with-
held from the press the newspapers concoct guesses which
are frequently embarrassing.
Before turning to a consideration of some of the changes,
brief mention of the men who have held the highest mili-
tary positions of Japan since the Mukden incident will
recall to mind the necessary background. The three pre-
eminent military offices in Japan are chief of the general
staff, minister of war, and inspector general of military
education. On September 18, 1931, General Minami, present
ambassador to "Manchukuo", was war minister. General
Kanaya was chief of staff, and General Muto was inspector
general of education. In December 193l the Inukai cabinet
came into office, with General Araki as war minister, Field
Marshal Prince Kanin as chief of staff, and General Hayashi
as inspector general. General Masaki was assistant chief
of staff. On May 15, 1932, Premier Inukai was assassinated
and Viscount Saito succeeded to the office, General Araki
remaining as minister of war. In this period, when assassi-
nation was in the air and factions in the army were involv-
ing themselves in major political and social questions of
the nation, General Masaki, as General Araki's righthand
man, made extensive use of the gendarmerie, with the full
cooperation of its commander Lieutenant General Shinji
Hata, for the purpose of closely shadowing the activities
of all important or suspected persons in or connected
with the army. In January 1934, without a change of
cabinet.

=== Page 90 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
cabinet, Araki resigned and Hayashi became war minister;
Masaki at the same time became inspector general of
military education. In July 1934 the Okada cabinet was
organized, without change in the three highest military
positions. It is to be noted that both Masaki and Hayashi
have served in important office both during and succeeding
the Araki regime.
General Masaki has the reputation of being a man of
energy, a schemer with something of the politician's
temperament, a wire-puller who likes to consider himself
the power behind the scenes. While General Araki was war
minister this role was a fitting one, because Araki is
regarded as an idealist of extremely magnetic personality,
lacking comprehensive plans, of entirely unpolitical talents,
and personally confident of Masaki's ability. The situation
is, however, quite different with the war ministry in the
hands of General Hayashi, who has his own plans. Hayashi
and Masaki simply have not worked as a team, and on several
occasions friction has been apparent. For example, when
General Hayashi was considering resigning several months ago
on account of his brother's being implicated in a municipal
bribery investigation, practically all his friends urged
him to remain in office; but Masaki recommended resignation.
General Hayashi presumably is intent on a number of
moves in the path of which General Masaki has stood as an
obstacle. Hayashi,it is said, favors the orthodox in the
army, wishes the military to return to a normal state on an
even-moving career basis, and is opposed to the type of
political

=== Page 91 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
political activity in the army which came about when
the Manchurian issue was at crisis. The military political
cliques have become less active with the apparent Japanese
success in Manchuria, where the present phase of the state
of affairs now calls for the efforts of civilians with
plans rather than of soldiers with zeal. General Hayashi
seems to believe that the morale of the career calls for a
correction of the undisciplined dislocations which accom-
panied the Manchurian incident, and in coming to an open
issue with General Masaki, who wished them perpetuated,
he has found immediate and general support and an improved
tone in the army.
It would be an error to conclude that this Hayashi
victory is a victory over General Araki. Araki, in fact,
has refrained from any attack on the present policy of
the ministry of war, and to all outward appearances supports
it. The condition which is being corrected appears to be
more the creation of Masaki than Araki.
General Jotaro Watanabe is the new inspector general
of military education. He is a quiet and studious man, of
the steady career type, not connected with any particular
group and not known to be an advocate of any particular
domestic or foreign policy.
The list of August changes is expected to affect over
three thousand officers. Generals Hishikari and Matsui
of the Supreme War Council are being retired on account
of seniority. Lieutenant General Hata, mentioned above as
commander

=== Page 92 === (confidence 0.96)
- 6 -
commander of the gendarmerie when Araki was in office, is
placed on the waiting list although his age is but fifty-
six years and he has not reached the rank of full general.
This action receives the endorsement of practically all
army officers and newspaper men, the latter of whom resent
his treatment of the press at the time of the partial
execution of the political assassinations plotted for May
15, 1932. A large number of men in the Kwantung army are
promoted, all considered of orthodox army leanings, most
of whom were overlooked by the previous regime. A notable
assignment of a lower-ranking officer is the transfer to
the post of chief of strategy in the general staff office
of Colonel Ishihara, the officer of ascetic type who carried
a.
out the incident at Mukden on September 18, 1931. Lieutenant
General Umezu, commander of the China garrison, is recalled
2
and placed in charge of a division in northern Japan, but
the assignment is a normal one and it is impossible to
find in it any expression either of approval or of disapproval
of his handling of the recent events in north China; but
in any event he is relieved of his command there.
Any attempt to interpret the significance of the
changes in the Japanese army which have necessitated the
removal of General Masaki should bear in mind that these
changes have come from within the army, not from without.
of
There is nothing in this coercion of the military by the
civilian. The effect is an important step in the return
of the army to a settled condition, under the leadership
of a war minister who is a soldier, not a politician, and
who

=== Page 93 === (confidence 0.96)
-7-
who believes that the army should be a disciplined body
at the service of the nation. The trying days of practieal
insubordination on the part of certain officers in connection
with the Manchurian incident are thereby removed the fur-
ther.
Insofar, therefore, as Mr. Hirota's remarks to the
Ambassador on July 16 are taken to mean only that the re-
moval of General Masaki can be expected to result, for the
time being at least, in fewer and fewer complications
caused by the machinations of unruly elements in the army,
a study of the facts tends to confirm the reasonableness
of such an expectation.
Respectfully yours,
Edain L. wille
Edwin L. Neville
Chargé d'Affaires ad interim.

FORD MOTOR COMPANY
of Japan Limited.
YOKOHAMA
July 11, 1935.
His Excellency
The Hon. Joseph C. Grew
American Ambassador
American Embassy
Tokyo.
Your Excellency:
We regret very much to have to ask for your kind
intercession in a matter involving an extralegal inter-
ference by the Japanese authorities in a legitimate
business transaction between ourselves and a Japanese
Company.
The facts of the case are as follows:
For some time we have been negotiating with the
Tokyo Bay Reclamation Company Ltd. (Asano interest)
for the purchase of a property of approximately
1l1,O0o Tsubo, located at Suehiro-cho, Tsurumi,
Yokohama. On February 26th the Tokyo Bay Reclamation
Company gave us a written option for 6o days on that
property and before the expiration of the option we
exercised our rights by letter of April 25th in which
we notified them that we were ready to complete the
purchase of the site.
All the necessary preparations were made by
the Tokyo Bay Reclamation Company when at the last
moment they were summoned by a certain quarter and
requested not to sell the property to us.
Since then the owners of the property have been
endeavoring to have the opposition of the authorities
withdrawn but apparently without success and on the
8th of this month we notified the Tokyo Bay Reclamation
Company that we expected them to fulfil the agreement
into which they had entered, by completing the sale of
the property to us.
We know that the owners are extremely anxious to
sell that property and we want to buy it for the pur-
pose of extending our assembling facilities and
establishing manufacturing on a limited scale in con-
nection therewith.
We

=== Page 98 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
We cannot understand why there should be such
interference from the authorities especially as there
is no legal justification whatsoever for same and we
would therefore kindly ask you to communicate with the
Foreign Office of the Imperial Japanese Government and
solicit through them the withdrawal of the opposition
to the sale of the property in question to us.
Thanking you in advance for your trouble, and
very much regretting the necessity for asking your
help, we are,
Yours very truly
FORD MOTOR COMPANY
of Japan Ltd
(Signed)
BENJAMIN KOPF
Manager

=== Page 99 === (confidence 1.00)
COPY
Enclosure No. 2 to despatch
No. 1422 of August 8, 1935,
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
Conversation
July 21 and 22, 1935.
Mr. Neville and Mr. Kurusu,
Chief of the Commercial Bureau
of the Foreign Office.
SUBJECT: Ford Motor Company's purchase of land.
I met Mr. Kurusu in Karuizawa on July 21 and rode
down to Tokyo with him on the train on July 22. On
both days I took occasion to discuss the question of
the Ford Motor Company's land purchase difficulties as
set forth in Mr. Kopf's letter to the Embassy of
July 11, 1935.
He told me that he had had an opportunity to con-
fer with the Vice Minister of Commerce and Industry
who told him, he said, that the latter Department had
not been concerned in the matter. He said that the
Vice Minister of Commerce and Industry had told him
that he anticipated a suit by Ford against Asano.
Mr. Kurusu said that they had agreed that in case of
a suit, Ford would very likely win. I then asked him
what he thought would happen in case the suit were
begun: whether the Army would permit one of its of-
ficers to appear in court and allege national defence,
or whether an effort would be made to cause so much
trouble for the two companies involved that they
would be glad to drop the whole matter. He said that
it was an interesting speculation, but that the
judiciary was pretty independent and not disposed to
countenance interference from any department of the
Government. He said that it was quite possible that a
court order would be the best way out for Asano, who
would then be relieved of embarrassment in turning
the land over to the Ford Company.
Mr. Kurusu then told me that there was definitely
a project on foot to license automobile manufacture in
Japan. He said that the details had not yet been worked
out, but that the plan would be presented to the Cabinet
for approval, and legislation introduced at the next
session of the Diet. He asked me whether I thought
Ford would want to increase the plant in the face of
this probable development. I said that so far as I knew,
Ford had taken the possibility into account; that in
any case it was the company's responsibility. What
the concern had in mind as I understood the matter, was
to increase its producing capacity in Japan, for which
its

=== Page 100 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
its present plant was inadequate, and which, it seemed
to me, it had a perfect legal right to do. He said
there was no doubt of its right to do so, and he thought
any court of law would so hold.
He then said that the people behind the project
were the army, or certain people in the army who had
got the ears of their superiors. He said that these
men were determined to have a completely Japanese
automobile industry. He asked me what I thought the
Ford people would think about admitting Japanese
capital. I said that I had no idea of what they would
think, but Ford's record indicated a determination to
follow his own plans. I said that I could not under-
stand what difference Japanese participation could
make, in view of the obstacles which had been placed
in the way of General Motors doing that very thing.
He then said that he had reason to believe that the
General Motors program would not be again opposed. I
said that I knew nothing of any new General Motors
plan for cooperation with Japanese capital. I did
not question him further on this point.
I then said that my present inclination was to
advise the Ford interests to bring suit against Asano
to compel compliance with the contract. This would
bring the matter out into the open. In any event,
they would have to do something to keep their legal
hold on the option they had obtained from Asano. In
the meantime, I sked him to keep me informed of
developments, ant to tell me, if he should find out,
just who the people were in army circles who were
trying to prevent Asano from selling the land. He
promised to do so.
E.L.N.

YOKOHAMA
July 30, 1935.
Mr. E. L. Neville
American Embassy
Tokyo
Dear Mr. Neville:
I believe that Mr. Ankeny has already informed
you that we are now the proud owners of the property
we have been trying to buy for some time and I now
want to take this opportunity to express my thanks for
your most effective help in the negotiations, without
which a satisfactory conclusion would undoubtedly have
taken much longer.
While we now have a site the next question is
what we can do with it. However, we are still hoping
for the best.
I should have called personally on you to convey
my thanks but I have just returned from a trip and
shall leave for my vacation on Friday and am kept
very busy with certain important matters so that it is
not possible for me to visit you until after I come
back.
Very truly yours,
(Signed) BENJAMIN KOPF
Manager

EHD:
Appended to this despatch from Tokyo
entitled "Urge Toward Expansion in Japan",
are two articles, presumably by Mr. H. G.
Woodhead, from ORIENTAL AFFAIRS. These
articles review Japanese policies and
Japanese action Qn the Asiatic continent
from the Japanese viewpoint, and urge the
Western Powers to recognize that Japan will
not submit to a status quo which ignores
the needs of a vigorously growing people.
They are worth reading.
It is worthy of note that Mr. Wood-
head's line of reasoning follows closely
that of Mr. Grew in his despatch of
February 6, 1935, also appended hereto.
Note the opinion of two well-informed
Britishers as recounted in the covering
despatch.

The Secretary of State,

Washington.
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatch
1156, dated February 6, 1935, on the subject of the
varidus factors connected with and entering into the urge
toward territorial expansion in Japan. In the conclusion
of the despatch referred to, it was given as the opinion
of this Embassy that, short of war, which could only re-
FILED
strain the Japanese for a generation or two, the only ap-
30 1935
parent means by which the recurring expansionist waves
of the Japanese nation could be checked consisted of the
voluntary granting to Japan by other nations of the world
of

=== Page 104 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
of greater economic opportunity, thereby satisfying
Japan's needs by economic, rather than territorial,
expansion.
1/2
I now have the honor to transmit clippings from
the JAPAN ADVERTISER, issues of August 9 and 10, 1935,
containing a reprint of an article which appeared in
ORIENTAL AFFAIRS. The article was published anonymously,
but as the magazine is edited by Mr. H. G. Woodhead, the
well-known British journalist, the possibility exists that
the article was from his pen. It will be observed that
the writer of the article reaches much the same conclusion
as that reached by the Embassy:
"It is not too much to say that the integrity
of China Proper depends upon the relaxation of
pressure upon Japan, either by new outlets for
her surplus population, or by economic access
to foreign markets, or a combination of both."
A member of the staff of the Embassy recently took
advantage of opportunities to discuss this general sub-
ject casually with two Britishers, Mr. H. A. Macrae, the
Commercial Secretary and Acting Commercial Counsellor of
the British Embassy, and Mr. E. Manico Gull, the Joint
Secretary of the China Association and the China Committee
of London. Mr. Macrae was of the opinion that the grant-
ing of greater economic opportunities to the Japanese
would not restrain them in their gradual conquest of Asia.
He thought that the Japanese nation as a whole was so con-
vinced of its own power that it would no longer be satis-
fied with half-way measures - that it would seek free
marBets

=== Page 105 === (confidence 0.96)
- 3 -
markets for Japanese goods throughout the world as well
as political domination of all Eastern Asia. Mr. Gull,
who previously had resided in China for some twenty years
and who is now studying present conditions in China and
Japan on behalf of the British organizations which he
represents, was of the opinion that, short of war, the
granting of economic privileges to Japan in Europe, the
Americas, Africa, Australasia, and elsewhere would ap-
pear to be the only means of checking the Japanese advance
on the Asiatic mainland, but he objected that the main
burden of supplying Japan with adequate economic privileges
would have to be carried by the British Empire.
Respectfully yours,
Ehin P. hoill
Edwin L. Neville
Chargé d'Affaires ad interim

Assassination of Major General Nagata: Publio reaction.
REGARDING:
The affair indicates that a policy of organizing the army
as a disciplined unit,free from political entanglements,
is a policy which has obstacles to overcome. It is not
probable that the assassination had any great significance.

NMH:
The Tokyo Embassy's 1532 of
November 1, 1935, reports further in
regard to the trial of civilian
participants in tre "May 15th affair" of
1932. I do not think it is necessary to
read the despatch.
All twenty civilians sentenced in
1934 promptly appealed. However, in order
to benefit by the amnesty in celebration
of the birth of the Crown Frince, all
but three withdrew their appeals. The
sentences of these three were reduced
materially by the higher court, and cu-
riously, this court sentenced them under
the provisions of the Naval Criminal
Code as accessories to the naval officers
previously sentenced.

O.N.I. ANDM.I.D
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatch
507
No. 685/of February 23, 1934, transmitting a report of
the trials of the civilians who were involved in the
May 15(1932) Incident. Previous despatches, No. 565/of
6
October 31, 1933, transmitted a report of the court mar.
1494
tial of the Military Cadets and No. 581/of November 15, 1933,
transmitted a report of the court martial of the Naval offi-
cers involved in the Incident. This despatch reports
the decision rendered on October 14, 1935, by the Court of
F/FG
Cassation to which three of the civilians previously
sentenced

=== Page 112 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
sentenced carried their appeals.
All twenty civilians sentenced on February 3, 1934,
because of the part which they took in the May 15 Incident
promptly appealed to the Court of Appeal. In order to be
able to benefit by the amnesty to be granted by the Emperor
of Japan on February 12, 1934, in celebration of the birth
of H.I.H. the Crown Prince of Japan, all but three with-
drew their appeals on February 1l. The appeals of these
latter three civilians were carried from the District
Court, through the Court of Appeals to the Court of Cassa-
tion, and on each appeal the terms of the prison sentences
were reduced.
The three defendants directly affected by the new
decision are Dr. Shumei Okawa, Hidezo Toyama and Kenichiro
Homma (Embassy's despatch No. 685 of February 23, 1934).
Upon appeal, the prison sentence of Dr. Okawa of fifteen
years decreed by the District Court was reduced to seven
years by the appellate court and to five years, with a
deduction of four hundred days for the period of detention
pending trial, by the Court of Cassation. Mr. Toyama's
original sentence of eight years was successively reduced
to four and finally three years, with a deduction of
three hundred days for the period of detention pending
trial. Mr. Homma's sentence of ten years was reduced to
five and finally four years, with a deduction of three
hundred days.
More remarkable, however, than the fact that the
sentences were so materially lightened are the grounds
cited by the Court of Cassation as the basis of its
decision

=== Page 113 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
decision. In the court of original jurisdiction the
action against the twenty civilians was based upon charges
of murder, attempted murder, abetment of murder and
violation of the explosives law (Embassy's despatch
No. 685 of February 23, 1934), al1 offences under the
Civil Criminal Code. In reaching its decision the
Court of Cassation, however, ruled that the previous
courts had erred in basing the decisions upon the Civil
Criminal Code. The JAPAN ADVERTISER of October 25, 1935,
reviews the decision as follows:
"Judge Shimizu argued in his speech preceding
the decision that the three civilians, Okawa,
Toyama and Homma, were involved in the May 15
case as accessories to Kiyoshi Koga and Yoshio
Namakura, the navy officers connected with the
case, and therefore the navy's criminal code
should have been applied in the trial. As the
defendants were accessories, Paragraph Two of the
naval code was applicable, which reads: 'Those
persons who have plotted . . . or have been as-
signed to a part in a plot against the State are
punishable with a teim of servitude of more than
three years either with or without hard labor.' "
Commenting on this decision the TOKYO ASAHI calls
attention to the unique precedent which has been set by
the application for the first time of the Naval Criminal
Code to a trial involving civilians. It will be recalled
that the civilians played a relatively insignificant role
in the May 15 Incident, their activities being limited
to attacks upon certain electric power stations in the
outskirts of Tokyo in an unsuccessful attempt to throw
the

=== Page 114 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
the city into darkness and confusion. The severity of the
original sentences imposed upon the civilians had evoked
general surprise because the military and naval officers
implicated in the case, who as their share in the May 15
Incident had assassinated Premier Inukai, thrown hand
grenades at the home of Count Makino, Lord Keeper of
the Imperial Seal and attacked the offices of the Metro-
politan Police Board, had been given comparatively light
sentences, fifteen years imprisonment to the two naval
ofricers who were the leaders in the plot and four years
to the eleven military cadets. It is therefore believed
that this lightening of the sentences of the civilians
meets vith the epproval of the Japanese public although
the action of the Court of Cassation in taking cognizance
of the motives of the civilians and sentencing them under
the provisions of the Naval Criminal Code as accessories
to the crime of rebellion, thus linking their fate to
the fate of their non-civilian fellow conspirators, is
looked upon as a most significant legal precedent.
The remaining seventeen civilians who participated
in the May 15 Incident are not directly affected by this
new ruling, as they withdrew their appeals in order to
be eble to benefit by the amnesty of February 12, 1934,
and failed to re-file their appeals within the prescribed
number of days. As the JAPAN ADVERTISER points out, how-
ever, Article 516 of the code of criminal procedure pro-
vides that the Procurator-General of the Court of Cassation
may order re-examination of a case in which misapplication
of the law has been discovered, and it is reported that the
Procurator's

=== Page 115 === (confidence 0.98)
-5-
Procurator's office of the Court of Cassation has asked
for all documents in the cases of the civilians. The
re-opening of the cases of the remaining seventeen
civilians and the revision of their sentences in accordance
with the new ruling may, therefore, be a development
of the near future.
Respectfully yours,
Edwin L. Neville
Chargé d'Affaires ad interim.
