Enclosure No. 1 to despatch No. 1578
of December 4, 1935 from the Embassy
at Tokyo.
OFFICIAL GAZETTE, May 11, 1935.
Cabinet Inquiry Council (Naikaku Shingi Kai)
We hereby sanction the official organization of the
Cabinet Inquiry Council and order its promulgation.
Imperial Sign Manual.
May 10, 1935.
Keisuke Okada
Prime Minister.
Imperial Ordinance No. 118.
Official Organization of the Cabinet Inquiry Council.
Article 1. The Cabinet Inquiry Council shall be at-
tached to the Cabinet and shall investigate and deliberate
on important policies which the Cabinet shall refer to its
consideration.
The Cabinet Inquiry Council may submit its suggestions
to the Cabinet with respect to important policies.
Article 2. The Cabinet Inquiry Council shall be com-
posed of a President, a Vice President and a committee of not
to exceed fifteen members.
Article 3. The Prime Minister shall be the President
of the Council. The Vice President shall be selected from
among Ministers of State and appointed by Imperial Order.
Members of the committee shall be skilled and experienced
persons appointed by Imperial Order.
Article 4. The President shall supervise all affairs of
the Council.
The Vice President shall assist the President and if the.
President should be prevented from discharging his duties, the
Vice

=== Page 124 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Vice President shall deal with all matters in the Pre-
sident's stead.
If both the President and the Viee President are
prevented from discharging their duties, a member of the
committee shall be nominated by the Prime Minister to
discharge the functions of the President.
Article 5. Ministers of State may attend meetings
of the Council and express their views.
Article 6. Rules of procedure of the Cabinet Inquiry
Council shall be decided by the Prime Minister.
Article 7. Secretaries shall be appointed to the
Cabinet Inquiry Council. The Chief Secretary of the
Cabinet, the Director of the Bureau of Legislation and the
President of the Cabinet Investigation Bureau (Naikaku
Chosa-kyoku) shall be named as secretaries. These secre-
taries shall perform their functions in accordance with the
instructions and under the supervision of the President
and the Vice President of the Cabinet Inquiry Council.
Article 8. General affairs of the Cabinet Inquiry Council
shall be managed by the Cabinet Investigation Bureau.
Supplementary Rule.
The present ordinance shall become effective from
the day of promulgation.

=== Page 125 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2 to despateh No.
1572 of December 4, 1935 from th
Embassy at Tokyo.
OFFICIAL GAZETTE, May 11, 1935.
Cabinet Inquiry Council (Naikaku Shingi Kai)
We, with the approval of the Privy Council, hereby
sanotion the official organization of the Cabinet Investiga-
tion Bureau and order its promulgation.
Imperial Sign Manual.
Keisuke Okada.
Prime Minister.
Imperial Ordinance No. 119.
Official Organization of the Dabinet Investigation Bureau.
Article l. The Cabinet Investigation Bureau shall be
placed under the supervision of the Prime Minister and shall
perform the following functions:
(1) Conduot investigations in regard to important
policies.
(2) Study proposals for important governmental action,
specially submitted by the Prime Minister.
(3) Manage the general affairs of the Cabinet Inquiry
Council.
The Cabinet Investigation Bureau may require the various
governmental agencies to submit or explain data necessary for
the Bureau's investigations.
Artiole 2. The personnel of the Cabinet Investigations
Bureau shall be as follows:
Director -
- - - - "chokunin" rank
Investigators, 15 (full time service)- "sonin" rank
(Five of the fifteen may be of "chokunin" rank)
Secretary - - - - -
- - -"sonin" rank
(This office shall be filled by one of the "sonin" rank
investigators.)
Junior Secretary (full time service)- -"sonin" rank
Petty officials, 20 (full time service)"hannin" rank.
Article 3.

=== Page 126 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Article 3. The Cabinet Investigation Bureau shall
have councilors who shall participate in the affairs of the
Bureau.
Councilors shall be appointed in accordance with a peti-
tion by the Prime Minister to the Emperor from among offi-
cials of "chokunin" rank in the various government offices
or from among persons of wide learning and experience.
Councilors shall be accorded treatment corresponding to
"chokunin" rank. If a Councilor already holds a position
in active service, however, he shall be accorded treatment
corresponding to that position.
The term of service of a Councilor shall be two years,
but for exceptional reasons a Councilor may be relieved
of his duties before the expiration of his term of service.
Article 4. The Cabinet Investigation Bureau shall
have a standing committee which shall deal with the every-day
affairs of the Bureau.
The Standing Committee shall be composed of the Chief
Secretary of the Cabinet and the Director of the Bureau
of Legislation.
Article 5. The Cabinet Investigation Bureau shall
have a technical committee to study technical matters.
The technical committee shall be appointed in accordance
with a petition by the Prime Minister to the Emperor, from
among high officials of the various government offices or
from persons of wide learning and experience. The term of
office of members of the technical committee shall be two
years but for exceptional reasons, members may be relieved
of their duties before the expiration of their term of
service.
Article 6.

=== Page 127 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
Article 6. The Director of the Bureau shall conduct
the affairs of the Bureau and direct the work of the staff
in accordance with instructions and under the supervision
of the Prime Minister. However, in regard to affairs men-
tioned in Article 1 (3), the Director shall act under the
instructions and supervision of the President and Vice Pre-
sident of the Cabinet Inquiry Council.
Article 7. "Sonin" rank officials of the Bureau
may be dismissed only after referring the matter of their
dismissal to the Prime Minister, but power to dismiss
"hannin" rank officials of the Bureau shall be vested in
the Director.
Article 8. If the Director of the Bureau is unable
to discharge his duties, a senior investigator shall act
in his place.
Article 9. Investigators shall conduct their investi-
gations and deliberations in accordance with the instruc-
tions of the Director.
Article 10. The Secretary and the Junior Secretary
shall deal with all matters coming before the Bureau in
accordance with the instructions of the Director.
Article ll. Petty officials shall handle general
affairs under the direction of superior officials.
Supplementary Rule.
The present ordinance shall become effective from
the day of promulgation.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
January 18, 1936.
WTT:
Tokyo's attached despatch No. 1612,
December 24, 1935, is a brief enumeration
of various evidences in 1935 of a
nationalistic tendency in Japan. The
despatch closes with the sentence
"Rather than hold up this despatch for
interpretive comment I shall have
occasion to refer to it later as back-
ground for a further discussion of the
nationalistic and expansionist urge
in present-day Japan." A fuller
despatch is therefore to be expected
later. The present despatch, in review-
ing the year, mentions Japan's notice of
abrogation of the Washington Naval
Treaty of 1922, demand for a common
upper limit among the naval Powers,
withdrawal from the League of Nations,
the giving of effect to the Amau
statement, "autonomy" movement in north
China, victory of the fundamentalist
theory of the Japanese Emperor,
organization of the Domei Tsushinsha
under stricter governmental control,
decision to enact automobile industry
legislation, gains by Shintoism and
developments in the control of the
petroleum industry.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE

U:
THIABY
Mr. Phixiips.
FEB 7- 1936
$:
Mc. Secretary.
DEPARTMENT OFSTATE
It is suggested that you read Tokyo's
attached despatch No. 1630, January 7, 1936,
GENERAL SITUATION FACING JAPAN AT THE OPEN-
ING OF THE YEAR 1936. Chief items are:
Although the Japanese nation had an
unusually favorable year in 1935, there is
little reason to believe that 1936 will be
any less favorable. Practically all stu-
dents of Japanese economics and finance
predict a steady continuance of Japan's
prosperity, in spite of the fact that the
total outstanding debt of the Japanese
Government has passed the ten billion yen
mark. During the coming fiscal year the
Government plans to issue bonds in the sum
of ¥680,000,000 to balance the budget, an
amount but slightly less than that issued
in the present fiscal year. The Japanese
have proved themselves very able financiers
and can probably surmount this difficulty.
During 1936 the tendency throughout the
world to restrict the importation of Japa-
nese goods will probably be Japan's most
serious economic danger.
The orders of the General Staff
issued last June forbidding troops of the
Kwantung Army from moving below the Great
Wall without sanction is still in force.
In the domination of north China military
occupation is not envisaged. Domination of
north China, as well as of Chahar and
possibly

=== Page 131 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
possibly Suiyuan, the army is determined
to obtain as a defense against Soviet
Russia and Russian and Chinese communism,
and as a protection of sources of essential
raw materials and Japanese trade and
economic expansion; but the present military
program aims to achieve this purpose by
methods other than by military occupation.
For the present at least it is not believed
that the intention exists to create an in-
dependent buffer state and to place Pu Yi
on a newly created throne, for that pre-
sumably would require military occupation.
The westward expansion of Japan into
Mongolia has begun, but it is not possible
to say that Japanese interest in Outer
Mongolia is becoming critical; and for the
present the danger of Japanese aggression
in Siberia appears to be in abeyance. The
personal influence of the Emperor is, if
not pacific, at least moderate. The army
can hardly become unpopular within Japan
but more and more Japan is developing into
a commercialized and industrialized nation
and considerations of economy must eventu-
ally present themselves to the people.
Economic conquest and not military conquest
appeals to the present mood of Japan.
The expansionist urge is fundamental
and the Ambassador believes there is no
doubt that whether quietly and gradually or
openly and aggressively Japanese energies
will be found, from now on, steadily direct-
ed towards consolidating Japan's control in
north China and Mongolia as a primary axiom
of her future strategic safety and economic
welfare.

EMBASSY OF THE

SUBJECT: NATIONALISTIC TENDENCIES IN JAPAN.

Sir:
FE8 5- 1936.
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatch
894.0538
FLILED
No. 1079 of December 11, 1934, which dealt with the
pronounced nationalistic urge in Japan, the efforts
being made to free Japan as much as possible from foreign
influence, from which nothing more is to be gained in
the opinion of the exponents of nationalism, and the
steps being taken to attain economic self-sufficiency.
During the year that has elapsed since the writing
of that despatch, the tendencies analysed therein have
increased in force and concrete evidenoes of the na-
tional

=== Page 133 === (confidence 0.98)
10-2
-2-
national conceit have multiplied. First of all, Japan
offioially gave notice of abrogation of the Washington
Naval Treaty of 1922 on December 29, 1934, and since
then has firmly clung to its demand for the establish-
ment of a common upper limit among the naval Powers,
even at the risk of causing unrestricted building com-
petition in the not distant future. Japan's withdrawal
from the League of Nations took effect on March 27, l935,*
and no ill results have followed this separation. In fact,
Japan's position on the Asiatic mainland seems to have been
strengthened partly as a consequence of the disentanglement
from the European scheme of things. The "Amau statement"
of policy, first enunciated on April 17, 1934, has been
put into practice with apparent success from the Japanese
point of view. In any case, in the past year there has
been an obvious decline in European and American influence
in China with a concomitant increase in Japanese influence
there. The so-called autonomy movement in North China,
fostered by the Japanese military, whose conneotion there-
with has not been officially disavowed by the Japanese
Government, may be taken as an instanoe of Japanese ac-
tive penetration in China and the enhancement of Japanese
influence there. In the economic field, the vernacular
newspapers and prominent business leaders point with pride
to the continued expansion of Japanese foreign trade and
the healthy condition of the internal economic structure.
Aside
Embassy's despatoh No. 1244 of April 19, 1935.
500.C 0011105

=== Page 134 === (confidence 0.98)
10-3
-3-
Aside from the broad considerations set forth above,
there have been a number of less important though no less
illustrative evidences of the intensification of the urge
toward nationalism and toward substantial independenoe
from foreign influence in Japan. The "fundamentalist"
theory of the Japanese Emperor as the supreme and semi-
divine head of his people gained a complete victory in
the Minobe controversy, the upshot of which was the
suppression of objectionably liberal ideas of foreign
origin. In this connection and in connection with the
growing belief that Western civilization has nothing
further to offer Japan, it is interesting to note the
views of the Government regarding the aims of the newly
established Education and Stuay Council for the clarifi-
cation of the form of the Japanese State.* Among these
aims, as set forth by the Cabinet on November 24, are the
eradication of the defects of Occidental culture and the
inculcation among the people of the true spirit of Japan.
Another pertinent development is the recent organization
of the Domei Tsushinsha (United News Agency), around
Rengo as a nucleus, the object of which is the setting up
of strict Government control over the news published in
Japan so that only the approved nationalistic point of
view shall be presented.** Still another evidence of this
nationalistic tendency is the decision reached. by the
Government on August 9 to enact legislation for the con-
The main purpose of the proposed law is to prevent the
foreign automobile companies from expanding further in
this
*Monthly report for November, 1935.
* *Embassy's despatch No. 1603 of December 26, 1935.
***Embassy's despatches No. 1431 of August 10, 1935, and
NO1490-123NH1/221
894.297/8.

=== Page 135 === (confidence 0.99)
10-4
-4-
this country and to secure the natural increase in
the demand for automobiles for the Japanese manufacturers.
Self-sufficiency in the supply of automobiles in time of
war is the ultimate goal of the desired legislation, which
is sponsored by the War Office, whose obstructionist poli-
cies have frequently hampered the foreign automobile com-
panies in their operations in this country.
In the realm of religion in its relation to education,
the nationalistic urge has manifested itself through the
movement now on foot for the recognition of Shintoism
as a religion and the compulsory teaching of Shinto doctrines
in all schools. Heretofore the Home Ministry has maintained
that Shintoism is not a religion in the strict sense, but
only a form of ancestor reverence. It is reported that
the Education Ministry has drafted for presentation in
the Diet a bill providing that Shintoism shall be recognized
as one of the religions in Japan, along with Buddhism and
Christianity. In addition, according to "THE JAPAN TIMES"
of December 20, the Education Ministry advocates the teach-
ing of Shinto doctrines and the establishment of Shinto
shrines in all the schools of Japan. The placing of
Shintoism on a definite religious basis would be signi-
ficant because it has for many years been the oult of
supreme patriotism in Japan. It is difficult to reconcile
this proposed action with the position that the Japanese
Government supports no religion and permits no religious
teaching in public schools. A further illustration of the
trend of affairs in this country is the intransigent atti-
tude

=== Page 136 === (confidence 0.99)
/0-5
-5-
tude recently assumed by the Government with regard to
the foreign oil companies' desire that the storage re-
quirements of the Petroleum Industry Control Law be
shortened. And, in general, the Japanese Government
has become more reticent, more independent, and rather
intractable in its dealings with foreign Powers.
Rather than hold up this despatch for interpretive
comment I shall have occasion to refer to it later as
background for a further discussion of the nationalistio
and expansionist urge in present-day Japan.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

look through the two-page memorandum here-

thought and psychology of Japan. I some-
times doubt whether anyone in this country
has an adequate realization of what the

the Japanese being what they are and think-

more of our people could be given more of
information and gain more of understanding
in regard to the inevitable facts.

=== Page 140 === (confidence 0.99)

written by General Araki on the same general subject which
was quite as nebulous and quite as incomprehensible as the
attached.
The baffling thing about these writings is that they
are written, and read with grave attention, by people quite
as practical as ourselves. Of course the Japanese is no
philosopher, and has only a dim notion of the meaning of
logic -- I speak in the general. He is, however, profoundly
moved by emotions which are set up within him by tradition,
inheritance, and the forces around him. He strives to
analyze these emotions and evolves some explanation that he
considers recondite but which we practical Westerners
consider childish. Because we fail to comprehend his reason-
ing, he concludes that the Westerner is benighted and
incapable of grasping the esoteric perceptions of the
Japanese mind.
It is worth noting that the exponents of the cult of
Japanism seem to have a crusading urge. Araki himself on
frequent occasions declared that Japanism must be spread
over the face of the earth, to bring light to the world.
The explanation seems to be that the cult has a distinctly
religious quality. In this cult, the nation itself serves
as the deity.
The cult has its dangerous aspect: Japan is sacred;
its people are descended from gods; the emperor, the nucleus
of the state, is infallible; therefore it is the glorious
mission of Japan to impose these blessings on the less
fortunate peoples of the world.
Personally, I believe that this sort of modern Japanism
grows out of a type of inferiority complex, in combination
with the natural bigoted insularity of a people so long cut
off from foreign contacts.

PART II CONSTRUCTION OF
“NIPPON KOKUTAI"
CHAPTER I EXTERNAL FORM
(1) ORIGIN OF "KOKUTAI"
Since “Nippon Kokutai” is something
special without parallel in the world, you
can easily understand that it is no ordinary
thing. Understanding this, you cannot but
desire to look into the reason for the
substance of Nippon being different from
that of any other country.
So you think, "Who founded Nippon?"
You realize that it all goes back to the Sun-
Goddess. Then you consider the other gods.
And you find that the gods whose names
were at first handed down by word of mouth
and later recorded in what are now called
myths and in such old historical chronicles
as the “ Nihongi "(1) and “ Kojiki "(2) created
or ruled this country. These numberless
gods became the standard of the mind of

=== Page 144 === (confidence 0.99)
18
the people, and from this was born the idea
that Nippon is a sacred land.()
This idea has been inherited from god to
god—from the ancestral gods to the nation
founding gods and then to their successors,
the gods in human form (the Emperors)—and
has expanded and grown and made the
people to " worship the gods," " comprehend
the gods' will," "abide by the gods' princi-
ples," and " arrive at the gods' virtue": this
is how this sacred land of ours has grown
up, and this way of thinking has become the
very nature of the "people of the sacred
land." The special characteristic of Nippon
as a state lies in the living fact beyond
(1) Chronicles of Nippon from the earliest times to 697 A. D.,
published in 722.
(2) Chronicles of Nippon from the Creation to 628 A. D., published
in 712.
Of the Kojili there is an Engiish translation by Chamberlain
(Asiatic Society of Japan, Vol. X, Supp.)
Of the Niltotgi, a translation in English by Aston (Japan
Society, London, Supp. 1), and one in German by Florenz (Mit-
teilungen der deutschen Gesellschaft für Natur und Völkerkunde
Ostasiens, Tokyo)
(3) The idea that Nippon is a sacred land has been held from time
immemorial.

=== Page 145 === (confidence 0.99)
theory that the people have come to possess
this “ sacred land idea" as their nature, half
unconsciously, as a habit from childhood,
with no help of reasoning or knowledge.
In order to comprehend this correctly, we
must duly consider " Kokutai."
We must in the first place think, since
Nippon is a country founded by the gods,
what the gods founded the country for : they
wanted a place where they could realize
their will and founded a country; the place
that was chosen first is this land of ours,
named by them “Reed-plain-eternal-fair-
rice-ear-Land." According to the old stories
of the gods, Nippon was founded because
the gods wanted it, and not only the men
living there but also all things whatever
are destined to be ruled, whether they
will or not, under the conditions under
which the country was established, and they
are also in duty bound to be obedient, and
to adjust themselves, to their will uncon-
ditionally, thus making the will of the gods

=== Page 146 === (confidence 0.99)
the standard of everything. And in order
to keep this standard, the people reverence
it as "Michi" (the Way) or make it their
principle by giving it a place in their
thoughts.
Thus it is evident that Nippon was es-
tablished in a way not to be found in any
other country : the creator gods, and “ Michi"
the will of the gods, and the Emperors, the
descendants of the gods who inherit the
"Michi" and rule, and the subjects who
work united with the Emperors in one body,
and the territory, the material element that
fulfills the work of the gods—these five have
crystalized into one. This could be inter-
preted as the embodiment of the gods' will,
or as the crystalization of “Michi"—an
organic existence, subtle and grand. All of
this has led to Nippon's national activity
and soal progress, letting its politics,
learning, military power, industry and all
other things expand for the purpose of
carrying out the gods' will. This is Nippon

=== Page 147 === (confidence 1.00)
rightly observed.
Men cannot realize the peace for which
they long above everything else, unless their
countries are like ours or such as are
founded with ours as a model. Therefore,
the day will come sooner or later when all
the countries in the world will become aware
of this fact. 7 But for the present, the study
of “Nippon Kokutai" is the duty of us
Nipponese, and the insistence of the
Emperors Jimmu and Meiji is that we shall
take thought to set a good example of a
righteous country before the world.
In tabulated form, the five elements of
the country are as follows :
Five Great Elements of Nippon
Gods (sacred ancestors who founded the
country—the gods of Nippon are not
imaginary beings, but saints who
accomplished Truth.
Michi (law with the will of the gods as its
centre)—the saints who comprehended

=== Page 148 === (confidence 0.98)
22
and practised .Michi wanted, inspired by
the gods' will, to give its blessings
to human beings, whence the divine
work was started. The work became
a fixed law, Michi.
Emperors (descendants of the gods, supervisors
of Michi)——gods' descendants and at
the same time successors to Kokutai
and Michi.
Subjects (members of the Emperor's family in
a wide sense, who carry out .ichi)
-some descended from Heaven with
the gods, some had been living in the
country previously, and some in later
years came from other lands, but all of
them have really guarded and main-
tained the prosperity of the Imperial
Throne, having been assimilated in the
work of the gods and the rule of right
and justice.
Land (possession of the gods, place where
they work)——all natural objects, such
as mountains, rivers, plants, metals,

=== Page 149 === (confidence 0.99)
birds, and animals, in which Michi is
displayed.
(2) A COUNTRY WITH NO KOKUTAI IS AN
UNFINISHED COUNTRY.
There are many countries in the world,
but there is found in their existence no
eternity at all; some new ones are es-
tablished, and some old ones cease to exist;
the map of the world is always being
repainted. These countries were originally
founded in an unnatural way on no reason
or principles, so I here give them the name
"non-Kokutai countries," that we may clearly
distinguish them from Nippon. As green is
necessary to manifest the red of a flower,
so it is important to make clear distinctions
between things to be compared with each
other; by so doing, we make it easier to
understand the essential parts of things.
It is taken for granted that a country with
its sovereign, people, and territory can be
called a state. It may be a state in the

=== Page 150 === (confidence 0.99)
一
ordinary sense, but cannot be one with
ideals. Therefore it cannot be said to have
eternity, and besides it possesses no
thorough-going ruling power. This being
the case, the existence of such countries is
often revolutionized by mere accident, or
even if they continue, theirs is a hopeless
and uncertain existence. This is because
these countries originally were founded on
no principles; they are nothing but a con-
tinuation of force; they make military or
industrial power their basis; they are only
leading parasitic lives under the shadow of
money or arms. All of this is the natural
fate of a non-Kokutai country. So long as
these countries exist side by side, each of
them is always uneasy or seeing nightmares.
For this reason, it is no wonder that no
peace is to be realized on earth.
The first Emperor, Jimmu, declared that
“a wild existence of human groups breathing
hard amidst conflicts of interests does not
mean the eternal peace of mankind." And

=== Page 151 === (confidence 0.99)
declaring that “mankind should return to
one goodness and the whole world form one
family," he clearly showed the laws of right
and justice that had been inherited from the
gods, and also indicated clearly what Nippon
is for the sake of all human beings by mak-
ing the three great principles “Accumula-
tion of Happiness," “ Amassment of Glories "
and “Cultivation of Righteousness "(1) the
basis of the land, after which he was formally
enthroned. This fact is clearly shown in the
history of Nippon. This has been expanded
and enlarged into Nippon's present world
famous existence, and yet, to our regret, the
world never pays attention to it even now,
still being in a state of coma. But stranger
still, some of the Nipponese themselves are
indifferent to it, never trying to understand
Nippon correctly, and besides there are
many (especially among scholars) who are
apt to deliberately detract from their own
(1) These are the three great principles found in the Emperor
Jimmu's declaration made at the establishment of the Empire.

=== Page 152 === (confidence 0.99)
value, driven on by the currents of the
world, giving themselves up to western
thought, or materialistic civilization, thinking
it proper to do every possible mean and
shallow thing, such as following, welcoming,
and imitating things western blindly—a
matter for the greatest regret! This being
the actual state of things in Nippon, the
original home of “ Kokutai," we cannot put
all the blame on the peoples of the world
for their lack of knowledge of Nippon.
(3) WHAT IS THE USE OF THE FOUNDATION
OF NIPPON TO THE WORLD?
It is quite easy for us to observe Nippon,
and it is not our urgent business to look into
the world, so let us put them aside for future
consideration. The most urgent business at
present is the question, “ How should Nippon
be observed from outside?"
Broadly speaking, in this world of human
beings—especially in each country—security
of the people is the ultimate aim, whether

=== Page 153 === (confidence 1.00)
the founders were gods or human beings.
As every living thing has a desire to live
and an instinct toward restfulness, so have
all countries without exception a desire for
peace and stability as the ultimate end.
Cooling medicines are produced in hot
countries and warming ones in cold ; in the
same way there should by all means be an
exemplary country of ideal peace somewhere
on earth. People naturally should look for
an example, even if such a country does not
exist propagating itself as such. We can
say that this is rather a universal instinct.
That is the point! If they come to know
that for two thousand six hundred long
years there has been in a corner of the
globe a country founded so as to meet this
demand and that it is no other country than
Nippon, the people of the world should feel
that they are destined to give attention to
this country./ If the world as well as Nippon
does not awake to the fact that Nippon has
a task to look after the world, and stands

=== Page 154 === (confidence 0.99)
2S
in a position so important and unavoidable,
both the world and the establishment of
Nippon will be meaningless. It would be a
great pity.
Let me observe again: the worid must
see that there must be somewhere in the
world at least one exemplary country of
justice and peace like this, must it not? 7
And the Nipponese themselves have no
word of excuse, as the people of the sacred
land according to the sacred intention of the
Emperor Jimmu, if they do not even think
at all what Nippon exists for.
Reader, this is the significance of Nippon's
raison d'être in the world.

CHAPTER II
SPECIAL QUALITY OF THE
“SUBSTANCE”
“Nippon Kokutai," whose construction
consists of the five great elements, never
fails to have the special quality of this
"Substance" display to the fullest extent
its "Nature," i. e. its own instinctive
function, the special quality appearing on
its surface. This quality is called the “un-
changeable characteristic." “Nature" in its
original quality displays with no exception
its characteristics just as water flows and
fire burns. The chief elements of "Nature"
originate in "Substance," but what makes
“Nature" stand out is its own functions.
“Nature" does not change of itself, so it
always expresses itself frankly and its ex-
pressions are sure to be revealed in the
form of "Michi"—the Way.
The plum-blossoms in my garden are now
in full bloom under the window before my
desk, diffusing their rare fragrance. These

=== Page 156 === (confidence 0.99)
plum-blossoms are “Substance” itself, and
the "Substance" of the plum-blossoms
spreads its special quality fully before my
eyes with its elegant colour, refined
fragrance, and tasteful arrangement of
branches.
The trunk, the branches, and
the fragrance and colour of the flowers are
“ unchangeable characteristics" of the plum.
However closely a tree may resemble a
plum, the peach or the cherry can never be
substituted for the plum, which has its own
character.
This colour, fragrance, and
refinement is no other than the "Nature"
of the plum. If this " Nature" of the plum
does not manifest itself, we cannot know
and appreciate the excellence of the plum,
even if the "Substance" has flowers,
fragrance, and beauty of form. In exactly
the same way, the "Michi" and the "Toku"
(Virtue), special possessions of Nippon,
cannot be understood clearly unless we
observe them from the direction of the
“unchangeable characterisctic," even if

=== Page 157 === (confidence 0.99)
31
there should be an excellent quality within
the "Kokutai." With this manifestation of
"Nature" in view, we have thought out the
"Morality" of the substance of "Kokutai,"
and divided it into three great principles,
namely,
(1) "Happiness," the sum of national
blessings that our sacred ancestors have
accumulated (Accumulation of Happiness)
(2) “Wisdom," the sum of national
wisdom that our sacred ancestors have
amassed (Amassment of Glories)
(3) "Right," the sum of national loyalty
that our Imperial descendants have always
cultivated (Cultivation of Righteousness)
These three principles are the essential
manifestation of “Nippon Kokutai," which
is the universal and impartial standard of
morality of the state and life with its holy
uses, beginning with the Sun-Goddess and
handed down to the descendants for un-
limited generations, and which is the
standard of everything culture, government,

=== Page 158 === (confidence 0.99)
32
learning, thought, morals, art, and every
civil and military affair-showing the real
goodness of mankind; and these three
principles are also the centre of the " High
Way" of “Kokutai" lasting eternally and
letting its light shine all over the world, as
the representative and fundamental way of
“Kokutai" and righteousness that is the
foundation of our every thought and action;
these form the very declaration to all the
world that the Emperor Jimmu made at the
beginning of the work of practising the
Imperial influences when he transferred the
capital to Kashiwara the ultimate light of
civilization which the people of the whole
world are destined to look up to for inspi-
ration, especially to us Nipponese the main
source of national spirit to which all of us
ought to be obedient.

=== Page 159 === (confidence 0.99)
CHAPTER III
MOVEMENT OF THE
“SUBSTANCE”
(1) TRUTH NEVER CEASES TO
EXIST, BUT ALWAYS WORKS CHANGING
INTO RIGHTEOUSNESS.
The characteristic of all the sacred
personages is always in their being of the
same body as truth; they are, we ought to
say, incarnations of truth. What we mean
by "god" here is not the God of such a
theocratic religion as Christianity; he is not
a mere mysterious existence in the form of
an idea constructed from the religious
demand of individuals, but a man in whom
divine quality is proved socially with the
heart of gratitude, conversion, and reverence
on the part of the people, in the relation
of the whole, social and practical. Therefore
he comes near to the idea of "god" in
theo-anthropic religions.
The gods set us examples according to
what we are, so that we may appreciate and

=== Page 160 === (confidence 0.99)
put into practice the truth they possess;
here truth works, becoming righteousness,
and reason also works, becoming relation-
ship so as to meet our desires. Truth is
natural and righteousness systematic, reason
exists a priori and relationship comes in
a posteriori becoming a regulative quality,
distinguishing between right and wrong. In
short, truth is to be considered and right-
eousness put into practice. And their ac-
tivity is most conspicuously found in the
civil and military government. Our govern-
ment originated directly in Truth, so it is
called “ justice " or “ assimilation."
(2) RIGHTEOUSNESS UNITES ITSELF WITH
TRUTH ON THE ONE HAND AND CONSISTS OF
GOVERNMENT ON THE OTHER.
The principal part of Righteousness is
the "Three great principles of Kokutai,"
which have come directly from the gods
(Truth), and the movement of which is sure
to propel " the sum of Happiness" or "the

=== Page 161 === (confidence 0.99)
35
sum of Glory"—instincts of the country,
and put "the sum of Righteousness" into
practice, and as its natural function, leaves
behind its marks on the earth through men,
and spreads itself as the work of righteous
government. Only then the gods' "Way"
begins to serve men; "Nippon Kokutai"
throws its light in the world of reality with
such correctness and delicacy.
(3) " NIPPON KOKUTAI" IS NOT AN
ACCADEMIC ORNAMENTAL THING.
Nippon's great rule of right and justice
born with Truth as its father and right-
eousness as its mother has expanded its
civil and military government along the
eight ways of justice it has been destined
to follow. These ways are the "Eight
sagacities," the fundamental national policies
for the civilization of right and justice;
these fundamental national policies shown
by the Emperor Jimmu are the standard of
our conduct, a concrete expression of the

=== Page 162 === (confidence 0.99)
36
great way of justice unchangeable for un-
limited numbers of years. These were all
given by the Emperor Jimmu and are all
well and clearly shown in Nipponese
history. These are an expansion of the
standard government we have divided into
eight branches, namely,
1) Unity of the gods and the people
2) Respect for the source
3) Unity of worship and rule
4) Centralization of power
5) Self-denial and self-reflection
6) Amassment and Expansion
7) Development and Progressiveness
8) Absolute peace
These are the national policies of the
civilization of justice which will not change
for thousands of ages, having their source
in the three great principles of "Kokutai"
and righteousness.

During my recent visit to Washington the Department
emphasized the importance and helpfulness of inoluding
in our current despatches an "evaluation of forces". So
far as such evaluation entails prophesy of future develop-
ments, it is perhaps trite to say that predictions in
this part of the world are generally unwise because the
unexpected can always happen - a fact often demonstrated
in past and recent history. Nevertheless ourrent trends
and

=== Page 166 === (confidence 0.98)
12
-2-
and their contributing factors may be gauged without
commitment as to the turn which these trends may take
in future owing to the injection of new factors into
the situation. It is on this basis that the Embassy
submits the following discussion of the general situation
facing Japan at the opening of the year 1936.
E C ON O M I C A N D
F I N A N C I A L
The year 1935 was in general a very prosperous and
successful year for Japanese commercial and financial
circles. Corporation earnings for the first half of
the year amounted to 14.8 per cent of the paid-in
capital plus reserves and unexpended balances, showing
an increase of .9 per cent over the earnings of the
previous half-year. No doubt these increased earnings
were in part due to the so-called "munitions boom",
but they were also due in large measure to the general
prosperity of the country. The average dividends
against paid-in capital amounted to 7.8 per cent (per
annum rate), or an increase of .5 per cent over the
previous half-year. Over half of the earnings were
placed to reserves, indicating not only a cautious
attitude but a surplus of earnings over dividend
necessities. Although the figures for the second half
of l935 are not available as yet, the available trade
returns indicate that corporation earnings for the
latter half will exceed those for the first half.
In foreign trade the country had the best year
since the Great War. Both exports and imports registered
large

=== Page 167 === (confidence 0.98)
13
-3-
large increases over those for l934, and the excess of
imports for the Empire dropped from Yen 123,000,000 in
1934 to about Yen 19,000,000 in 1935 (complete figures
are not yet available but the above calculations are
based on figures to Deoember 25, 1935). The invisible
trade balance, according to preliminary figures, was
slightly unfavorable for the year, showing an exoess
of payments over income of Yen 44,500,000, as compared
with an excess of income of Yen 8,700,000 in 1934. In
l935, however, the Japanese redeemed the outstanding
South Manchuria Railway sterling bonds and made large
investments in Manchuria, including the purchase of the
Chinese Eastern Railway, which account for the excess
of payments over income. Combining the visible and in-
visible balances, moreover, it will be noted that the
combined adverse balance in 1935 was Yen 63,500,000,
as compared with an adverse balance in 1934 of Yen 114,300,000.
If, therefore, Japan is exporting its capital, as some
economists claim, it is doing so at a decreasing rate.
Money remained easy in Japan throughout 1935, with
steady increases in both bank deposits and in loans out-
standing, indicating increasing business activity. Bank
deposits increased considerably faster than loans, however,
and during the year there was an increase of about Yen
700,000,000 in the spread between deposits and loans, which
naturally resulted in an easy money market. On the other
hand, although the Government finances appeared sound, there
seemed to be some doubt among the Japanese as to Government
oredit toward the year-end. During the year the Government
1ssued

=== Page 168 === (confidence 0.99)
11-4
-4-
issued large quantities of so-called "red-ink" bonds,
or bonds issued for the purpose of covering budgetary
deficits, and in the latter part of the year the private
banks and individual investors appeared somewhat re-
luctant to absorb more of these bonds. Although exaot
figures are not yet available, it is believed that the
total outstanding debt of the Government passed the
ten billion yen mark by the close of the year - a
record debt for Japan. The Governor of the Bank of
Japan, however, in his New Year message, stated that
the Bank was able to take up all further Government
issues until the close of the present fiscal year
(March 31, 1936), and appealed to the Japanese people
to uphold the credit of the Government.
Although the Japanese nation had an unusually
favorable year, economically and financially, in 1935,
there is little reason to believe that 1936 will be any
less favorable - in fact, practically all students of
Japanese economics and finance predict a steady con-
tinuance of Japan's prosperity. Their predictions are
based largely on the fact that better financial con-
ditions exist in the farming districts, due to higher
prices for rice and raw silk, and that further large
sums will be spent during the year by the Army and Navy
for modern military equipment, both of which factors
will ensure continued and perhaps increased activity in
the industries of the nation. Foreign trade will,
according to the predictions, continue to flourish
during l936, and may be considerably increased by the
conclusion

=== Page 169 === (confidence 0.98)
11-s
-5-
conclusion of a trade agreement with Canada, which
will restore trade with that country to normal, and
by the further opening of North China through the
establishment of an autonomous government there which
will be favorably disposed toward Japan. Moreover,
the economic recovery of the United States will un-
doubtedly create a larger market for Japanese goods,
while the war-nervousness in Europe will cause an
unusual demand for such war supplies as Japan produces.
On the other hand, there are various factors which
are not so favorable and which, in the opinion of this
Enbassy, may cause disturbances in Japan's economio
and financial circles during the year. The principal
unfavorable factor consists of the uncertain position
of Government credit. It is true that during the
coming fiscal year (April 1, 1936 to March 31, 1937) the
Government plans to issue a considerably smaller amount
of "red-ink" bonds than in the present fiscal year, but
even so the amount (about Yen 680,000,000) may be larger
than the capacity of the market, in which case there
may be serious dislocations of credit and serious fi-
nancial disturbances. The Japanese, however, have
proved themselves very able financiers and probably
will find means to overcome the possible financial
difficulties of the Government.
Another possible disadvantageous factor is the
erection of further and higher trade barriers against
Japanese goods. The Japanese Government is now fighting
against the erection of such trade barriers by volun-
tarily restricting the exportation of goods to countries
where

=== Page 170 === (confidence 0.98)
14
-6-
where it appears that opposition to the flood of
Japanese goods is growing. Whether or not this
means of combating the tendency throughout the
world to restrict the importation of Japanese
goods will be effective remains to be seen. The
Japanese Government also has another weapon which
was used effectively in the case of trade with Canada.
This is the Trade Protection Law, which authorizes
the imposition of greatly increased duties on goods
imported from countries which impose discriminatory
measures against Japanese goods.
Another fector which may militate against in-
creased Japanese prosperity during 1936 is the ex-
treme economic nationalism now apparent in Japan.
This economic nationalism, principally promoted by
the military for purposes of national defense, was
illustrated in 1934 and 1935 by the passage and en-
forcement of the Fetroleum Industry Law, which is
designed to develop oil refining within Japan and
to provide large stores of petroleum for use in time
of emergency. The effect will be greatly to restrict
the operations of the American and British oil companies
in this field, and perhaps to eliminate them from the
field altogether. In 1936 the Government plans to
enact an Automobile Industry Law, which would have much
the same effect upon the two large Amerioan au tomobile
assembling and distributing organizations in Japan.
The American and British oil firms have already been
eliminated from the Manchurian field, and it is ex-
peotea

=== Page 171 === (confidence 0.99)
1-7
-7-
peoted that in l936 steps will be taken to eliminate
the foreign automobile companies, turning the trade
over to Japanese interests.
This rampant economic nationalism is apt to invite
reprisals from other nations, which might seriously
interfere with the further growth of Japan's foreign
trade. This fact is not generally understood in Japan,
but the Embassy is aware that some of the leaders of
the Government understand the dangers of the movement
and are trying to curb it. The military, however, with
their dominant influence in the Government, apparently
do not understand the effect which their policies may
have on Japan's foreign economic relations and continue
to insist upon the enforcement of their ideas of what is
best for the nation.
POLITICAL
Domestic
The domestic political field has been largely
occupied by discussions over what has been called the
"Emperor organ" theory, a question which has been
discussed in several despatches from the Embassy. The
"fundamentalists" object to any attitude on the part of
public officials to regard the Emperor as in any sense
an instrument or organ of the State. He is to be con-
sidered Heaven's Regent, the descendant of the Sun
Goddess, and as such far above such mundane position
as the Head of a State or the organ whereby the body
politic makes its decisions known. Paradoxically this
vigilance

=== Page 172 === (confidence 0.98)
1-8
-8-
vigilance has been accompanied by agitation on the part
of these same elements to remove all "non-fundamental is ts"
from positions in the Imperial Court which would have
the effect of rendering such officials as the Minister
of the Household, the Grand Master of Ceremonies, the
Keeper of the Seals and perhaps the President of the Privy
Council, responsible to the Government for their political
views. Up to the present time these officers have been
men of experience and prestige who have been considered
as non-political appointees and have held office re-
gardless of political changes in the Government. The
agitation in effect is against the influence which these
older men surrounding the Emperor have been able to
exert without accompanying political responsibility.
It is probable that the agitators do not see the results
which would flow from the success of their efforts.
The Government has continued to be "national" or
non-party in character, and has managed to stay in
office. It has, however, been unable to control the
Diet to the extent of obtaining much new legislation.
The Diet adjourned last spring with most of the Govern-
ment's bills on the calendar.
In 1936 the Government will be confronted with a
general election, as the four year term of office of
members of the House of Representatives will expire,
but it is not yet known whether the Government will
dissolve the Diet, or allow the legislature to complete
its session and then hold the election. This question
w111

=== Page 173 === (confidence 0.99)
11-9
-9-
will be decided by the political exigencies of the
moment. There seems no doubt that this year as last,
the Government will be able to put through the budget
because the influence or fear of the military will
probably be sufficient to accomplish it. There is no
doubt, however, that the latter has not now the politioal
prestige that it had when the present Diet members were
elected four years ago. Besides, the Seiyukai have
seized upon the "Emperor organ theory" as a political
slogan and threaten to make a campaign issue of it. It
will be interesting also to see the character of the new
Diet, the first to be elected under the rigid rules of
the Currupt Practices Act, because the members will be
less subject to the charge of owing their election to
bribery or other sinister activities.
Foreign
Abroad, the main arena of Japanese interest and
activity for the moment centres in North China, Mongolia
and the Manchukuo-Siberian frontier.
North China
Few observers will be found who fail to see in
Japan's announced policy of constituting herself the
"stabilizing influence" in East Asia the thought of
eventual domination of North China and Mongolia with
exclusive Japanese influence therein. The conquest
of

=== Page 174 === (confidence 0.99)
1110.
-10-
of Manchuria, as Mr. Owen Lattimore has pointed out
in his article on "The Inland Gates of China" in the
December is sue of PACIFIC AFFAIRS, could not be isolated
or limited but was of necessity the beginning of a process.
"Japanese control in North China" he considers "is in faot
an inevitable corollary of the conquest of Manchuria; and
this involves Mongolia, because military domination of
North China cannot be made good without a solid strategic
position in Mongolia, as the Manchus discovered in the
seventeenth century......" While the soundness of Mr.
Lattimore's theory may be open to argument, this is un-
doubtedly the view held by the Japanese military, and
the only question at issue is how far and how fast the
military will be permitted to proceed with this strategio
program, and the extent to which the control of the Govern-
ment in Tokyo can or will be exerted in restraining this
movement. Herein lies the nub of the situation.
One fact is evident: the orders of the General Staff
issued last June forbidding troops of the Kwantung Army
from moving below the Great Wall without Imperial sanotion
is still in force and at least informal assurances have
been given by the Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs to the
British Ambassador that the order would remain in effect
and that there would be no occupation of North China by
Japanese military forces.* From its observations and
from all information available, the Embassy believes that
these assurances genuinely represent the intentions of the
Government in Tokyo including the higher army officers
responsible
*
Embassy's telegram 128, June 11, 1935, 6 p.m., paragraph 3.
Embassy!s telegram 245, December 19, 1935, 11 a.m.,
paragraph 1.

=== Page 175 === (confidence 0.98)
1111
-11-
responsible for military policy. In the domination
of North China, military occupation is not envisaged.
Quite apart from any question of treaty commitments
or of international ethics, there are important praotical
considerations opposing such procedure, particularly
the financial expense and the disadvantage of using
troops now stationed in Manchuria, thereby weakening
the defenses against Soviet Russia. The exertion of
control by other methods is believed to be more effective
and cheaper. This view is substantiated by current com-
ment in Japanese military magazines and by officers with
whom foreign contact has been established; they declare
categorically that not a square foot of territory in
North China will be occupied. The general conception
abroad that Japanese troops have recently invaded North
China - based on the activities of the garrison troops
already there - are, so far as this Embassy is aware,
incorrect, and save for a few additional staff officers
the Embassy has no knowledge of recent increases in the
North China Garrison forces. Domination of North China,
as well as Chahar and possibly Suiyuan, the Army is
determined to obtain as a defense against Soviet Russia
and Russian and Chinese communism, and as a pro tection
of sources of essential raw materials and Japanese trade
and economic expansion, but the present military program
aims to achieve this purpose by methods other than by
military occupation. It is therefore believed that the
Army authorities in Tokyo recommended and welcomed the
"Imperial Sanction" restriction as perhaps the only
effeotive

=== Page 176 === (confidence 0.98)
11—2
-12-
effective means of restraining irresponsible and hot-
headed officers looking south from the Great Wall.
For the purposes of this discussion it does not
appear necessary to go into the details of the so-called
autonomy movement in North China, and the part played
by the Japanese military therein, which have been fully
reported by my colleague in Peiping. The Embassy wishes
to deal here only with the broeder aspects of the situation.
Recent developments have however afforded a fairly clear
conception of the methods by which the Japanese propose
to establish control in the northern provinces, namely
by the setting up of Chinese officials who at least to
some degree will do their bidding. If these methods fail
it will remain to be seen what other methods will be
adopted, but for the present at least it is not believed
V
that the intention exists to create an independent buffer
state and to place Pu Yi on a newly-created throne, for
that presumably would require military ∞ cupation.
Mongolia
If, as Mr. Lattimore believes, the conquest of
Manchuria could not be isolated but was of necessity
the beginning of a process, we should in l936, four years
after that conquest, be able to recognize further steps
in the process. These have, in fact, not been lacking.
During 1935 skirmishes along the border between "Manchukuo"
and Outer Mongolia led to the unsuccessful Manchuli
Conference, an ill-disguised attempt on the part of
Japen to open Outer Mongolia. Moreover, to the south in
Inner Mongolia, notably in the province of Chahar, the
Japanese

=== Page 177 === (confidence 0.99)
11-13
-13-
Japanese have been increasingly active and at the
present time there appears to be emerging an autonomous
regime acceptable to the Military.
The fact is then that the westward process has begun.
In Inner Mongolia it is largely indistinguishable in
aim and method from the process which is proceeding
concurrently in North China. In both regions autonomous
movements aiming at the concentration of authority in
the hands of indiviauals subservient to the Japanese are
being created. It matters little who the individuals
are; the fact of importance with respect to Inner Mongolia
is that the Japanese now dominate Chahar.
Nor will the
westward process end there.
On the contray, one new
factor at least has appeared which tends to accelerate,
if anything, the westward momentum and that is the in-
creas ing preoccupation of the Japanese military with
Chinese communism. One hears much at present of Japan's
duty to save China from the Communists. The reported
spread of communism in Kansu, Northern Shensi, and the
Ordos region is undoubtedly a source of concern to the
Japanese military who perceive that it is not far from
these districts to revolutionary Outer Mongolia and
that an avenue of easy communication between the Com-
munists in China and those to the north may thus be
created. This factor may spur Japanese agents to
greater activity, but the same considerations which
militate against military conquest in North China
and which were discussed earlier in this despatch apply
here as well.
There

=== Page 178 === (confidence 0.98)
1114
-14-
There remains then the question of the vast desert
region of Outer Mongolia. Japan's efforts to gain a
foothold here can not be ascribed to any desire for
economic opportunity for little opportunity exists in
this inhospitable region. No section of the Japanese
people can be vitally interested save the Military and
their interest is primari y strategic. Lately the
"Manchukuo" authorities have deplored the failure of
the Manchuli Conference, which proved that Outer Mongolia
was not to be opened by diplomatic means, and have
voiced their intention of dealing with the problem of
determining the frontier towards Outer Mongolia inde-
pendently and to their own satisfaction. Although on
December 19 and again on December 24 there were clashes
on the border between "Manchukuo" and Outer Mongolian
forces, the fears of serious trouble which have been
expressed in certain quarters seem unnecessarily grave.
The Mongolian winter is not favorable to extensive
military operations.
To date Japan has only made it clear that she would
like to open Outer Mongolia. There is no evidence
that she is as yet prepared to back up this desire
with any considerable expenditure of money or resources.
In fact, according to report, the War Office prof esses
to have little knowledge of events in the northwest
and there is no available evidence that an effort to
penetrate this region and to flank Baikal and the
Transiberian from the south is being planned. Nor is
it possible to secure from the local Soviet officials
any

=== Page 179 === (confidence 0.99)
1-5
-15-
any expressions of opinion on this situation.  The
Soviet Ambassador will say nothing and, despite the
reported presence of the Mongolian Prime Minister in
Moscow for the last month, it is likely that he has
little or no information. Accordingly, although a
gradual increase in Japanese interest in Outer Mongolia
has been fostered by the Manchuli Conference of last
year, by border incidents, and by mutual recriminations
between "Manchukuo" and Outer Mongolia, it is not
possible to say that the issue is becoming critical
or that it has left the province of military strategists
concerned with possible but distant future developments.
"Manchukuo" - Siberia
At the beginning of 1936 no serious trouble between
Japan and Soviet Russia is foreseen in the near future.
The Soviet Ambassador here professes to be thoroughly
optimistic and remarked to me several days ago that the
Japanese would not dare to attack the USSR because the
latter's preparations along the border were so formidable.
He has taken the same attitude with several of my
colleagues. Doubtless the "Manchukuo"-Siberian border
will remain a danger spot and incidents will occur.
But there appears to be nothing in this situation nor
in the unsettled fisheries case nor in that involving
the oil leases of North Saghalien comparable in gravity
to the Chinese Eastern Railway issue which at this time
a year ago remained unsolved. That problem, involving
the final elimination of the Soviets from Manchuria,
was

=== Page 180 === (confidence 0.99)
11-16
-16-
was considered vital by the Japanese; to replace it no
issue equally vital has as yet appeared. In the more
or less remote future one can picture the further west-
ward spread of Jepan as a continental power presenting
a serious threat to Russia's security in Siberia, but
such forebodings belong to the realm of speculation
rather than to an appraisal of existing trends. For
the present, the danger of Japanese aggression in
Siberia, believed two years ago to be imminent, appears
to be in abeyance.
Having in mind the foregoing brief survey of the
program and activities of the Japanese military in
bringing to bear Japan's "stabilizing influence" in
East Asia, we may return to an evaluation of the forces
which may influence the Government in Tokyo in restrain-
ing or accelerating the inevitable movement westward.
First of all, the weight of the personal influence
of the Emperor himself in shaping policy and procedure
is extremely difficult for any foreign observer to
gauge. Few Japanese are willing to touch upon that
subject, but the consensus of opinion is that such
influence as the Emperor does exert, having in mind
the varied interests and viewpoints within the country
which he must so far as practicable conciliate, is pacific.
The Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs, who is generally
regarded as an ardent nationalist with somewhat
chauvinistic

=== Page 181 === (confidence 0.99)
1117
-17-
chauvinistic leanings, in recent conversation with
one of my colleagues confirmed this faot and added
that the development of the Emperor's personality,
mental attributes and grasp of public affairs during
recent years has been little short of amazing; indeed
Mr. Shigemitsu went so far as to predict that the
present Emperor would go down in Japanese his tory,
in point of enlightened outlook, as on a plane with
the Emperor Meiji himself, a statement somewhat sur-
prising to a foreigner in view of the fact that the
present ruler's personality, influence and construc-
tive activity have been hidden behind a veil of
secrecy wnich few have had an opportunity to penetrate.
Mr. Shigemitsu is, however, a direct and hard-headed
official not given to weaving fanciful theories and
his opinion may therefore be listened to with reason-
able respect.
If it is true that the leanings of the Emperor
himself are in principle pacific - or perhaps a better
word is moderate - it can with equal truth be held
that his princinal advisers, notably Prince Saionji,
Count Makino, Viscount Saito, Baron Ikki, and probably
the present Prime Minister and Minister for Foreign
Affairs, would prefer to achieve gradually and by
peaceful means what certain elements in the Army
would accomplish by force. While they unquestionably
support the doctrine of Japan's "stabilizing influence"
in East Asia, her strategic security, her protection
against communism and her economic development west-
ward, it is believed that their views as to the
methods

=== Page 182 === (confidence 0.99)
11-18
-18-
methods of consummating such a position differ from
the views of the more radical school of chauvinists.
Despite the bluster of the occasional utterances of
the spokesman of the Foreign Office and other officials,
Japan's reputation in the world at large is to them
far from inconsequential, nor do they desire to risk
unnecessary embroilment with foreign Powers even while
convinced that those Pawers are highly unlikely, either
from policy or because they are occupied elsewhere, to
fight. In other words, statesmanship is not wholly
V
lacking in Japan today, and at least for the moment
the more moderate elements in the Government appear
to have control of the general situation. There is
a feeling that the military have recently overplayed
their hand in North China, and at a given moment the
curbing rein was pulled from Tokyo just as it was
last June.*
As for the Japanese military themselves, they are
no longer riding on the crest of the wave to the same
degree as in 1931 and 1932. As one of the two bulwarks
of the nation, the Army can hardly become unpopular
within Japan, yet it may be said with assurance that
the Army does not command the same extravagant en-
thusiasm and support which it enjoyed during and im-
mediately after the Manchurian adventure. More and
more Japan is developing into a commercialized and
industrialized nation; business is becoming paramount;
money
*
Embassy's telegram 128, June 11, 1935, 6 p.m.,paragraph 5.
Embassy's telegram 132, June 18, 1935, 1 p.m., paragraph 2.
Embassy's telegram 143, June 29, 1935, 11 a.m.

=== Page 183 === (confidence 0.99)

-19-
money talks; the worker who was content with 30 sen
a day is beginning to reach out for a higher standard
of living; the whole social aspect of the country is
gradually changing from the old feudal conception. In
this state of the public mind the extravagant military
budget, in spite of intensive propaganda, cannot be
popular. Apart from its strategic value, the economio
advantages of the conquest of Manchuria are not yet
apparent;
the costs of its development and defense
are enormous; as an outlet for the over-population of
the home country it has failed;
the promised financial
returns from the investment are not yet in sight and
will not appear for many a day. While the thought of
eventually commanding the raw materials, industries
and markets of North China no doubt appeals to the
Japanese business man as a pleasing future prospect,
the outlook for the steady development of business in
the home land, in industry, commerce, shipping, banking,
is not as yet sufficiently cramped to inspire a publio
demand, or indeed a public desire, for further military
conquest in the present day. The financial and economic
status of the country is sound and active; domestic and
foreign business is steadily developing and expanding;
an inherent urge towards new fields of military conquest
does not at present predominate. Economic conquest
1
more aptly describes the stimulus.
Other considerations may conceivably influence
Japan's future course of action. The stiffening attitude
in China, as manifested especially by the student move-
ment in Peiping and other cities, has not been lost upon
the

=== Page 184 === (confidence 0.99)
1120
-20
the statesmen in Tokyo. My British colleague is in-
formed that the solidarity of the movement has taken
the military by surprise and that it has given them
"furiously to think". My own information is that the
movement is discounted and that army circles believe
that it will soon lapse. More attention is given to
the sanctions against Italy, the ostensibly growing
influence of the League of Nations and especially the
force of public opinion in Great Britain against an
aggressor, as palpably demonstrated in the recent
public outburst against Sir Samuel Hoare and his en-
forced resignation arising out of the obvious effort
to conciliate Italy in the Hoare-Laval peace proposal.
These things are being carefully weighed in Japan.
Now comes the President's address to Congress, and
whatever may be its effect in Europe, I am of the
opinion that it has come at a timely moment to in-
fluence Japanese thought and policy. While Japanese
officials and the press have carefully avoided fitting
the Presidential shoe to their own foot, the implica-
tions of the address and especially the words (as re-
ported in the press here) "The point has been reached
where the peoples of the Americas must take cognizance
of a growing ill will marked by trends toward aggression,
of increasing armaments, of shortening tempers - or
a situation having many of the elements that lead
toward the tragedy of a general war", must inevitably
sink deep if tacitly into the consciousness and con-
science of the Government and publ ic of Japan.
There

=== Page 185 === (confidence 0.99)
1121
-21-
There is another element in the situation which,
if no unexpected incidents occur, I am inclined to
believe may play a not unimportant part in influenoing
Japanese policy during the next few years. The Japanese,
as is well known, are rapidly and effectively becoming
a race of first-rate athletes; their interest in sport
is nation-wide and the public enthusiasm for baseball
especially, and in hardly less degree for track athletics,
swimning, ski-ing, golf, tennis, and other sports, is
quite as intensive as in our own country. Their out-
standing champions and winning teams in the various
sports are acclaimed as public heroes quite as ex-
tensively as their outstanding soldiers. Here is a
healthy and emotional channel into which, if the Govern-
ment plays its cards wisely, public interest can be
directed and concentrated with the Olympic Games of
1940 in view. The Department is aware of the efforts
which are being made to secure the meeting for Japan;
Italy, we are informed, has definitely waived her claim
in favor of Tokyo, and it appears that only the claim of
Finland renains to be overcome. In l940 will be celebrated
in Japan the 26o0th anniversary of the founding of the
Empire by Jimmu Tenno. If the Olympic Games can be
secured for that year, the preparations for the meet
and for the synchronous celebration of the national
anniversary will tend to eclipse all other matters in
popular interest. The Government, too, may appreoiate
the opportunity which such an occasion will offer for
bringing Japan more intimately into the international
circle from which, inevitably, she feels temporarily
on

=== Page 186 === (confidence 0.99)
1-22
-22-
on the periphery. The ASAHI, in an editorial, has
already given a hint in that direction. "The crisis
that did not come in 1935", it says, "is not likely
to come in 1936, but the 2600th anniversary of the
accession of Emperor Jimmu is certain to come.
Preparations for it are necessary........ Preparations
are already in full swing for the big international
exposition and the Olympic Games to be held here at
that time.....it is questionable whether there is need
for reckless crisis propaganda. In this sense we wish
to bury 1935 and look forward with hope to the 2600th
anniversary of the accession to the Throne of Emperor
Jimmu, which is four years away....."
Let us hope that the Government and the statesmen
of Japan will recognize in this a goal upon which to
rivet the attention and interest of the country during
the next few years.
But since this despatch deals with trends rather
than with events, and must therefore give consideration
to the long future pull together with the immediate
outlook, I quote the following paragraphs from "The
Price of Peace" by Frank H. Simonds and Brooks Emeny
(page 230) which seem to me to be sound alike in fact
and reasoning:
"As the situation stands today, despite
an enormous expansion of industry, Japan is
still, as in the past, confronted by two facts
of ominous import, a rising standard of living
and a rapidly expanding population. For the
moment it is still possible to foresee the
preservation of that parity in progress and
population. Doubtless there will be further
gains

=== Page 187 === (confidence 0.99)
11-23
-23-
gains in foreign trade, alike in textiles
and in rubber goods, electric applianoes,
novelties, and in certain other directions.
An adequate supply of cheap labor is also
assured, and costs of production may therefore
for the present fall rather than rise.
"Nevertheless, Japanese exports are
already encountering constantly multiplying
obstacles in the way alike of competition
from India and China and tariff barriers
amongst the Western nations as the march of
economic nationalism continues uninterruptedly.
The further advantage of the depreciated yen,
to which no inconsiderable part of recent
trade expansion has been due, is patently
transitory. Thus, so far as Japan and her
Korean and Formosan possessions are concerned,
it is plain that these cannot long continue to
support their growing population under existing
conditions."
These are facts, not theories, and should be given
due weight in any evaluation of forces influencing the
probable trends in Japanese policy and action during the
years ahead. The procedure to be followed and the methods
to be pursued are open to influence by some or all of the
factors suggested in this despatch, but the expansionist
L
urge is fundamental, and I think there is no doubt that
whether quietly and graaually or openly and aggressively
Japanese energies will be found, from now on, steadily
directed towards consolidating Japan's control in North
China and Mongolia as a primary axiom of her future
strategic safety and economic welfare.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

Tokyo's attached despatch No. 1645,
January 21, 1936, transmits memorandum of
conversation between Mr. Kurusu of the
Japanese Foreign Office and Mr. Dickover
on the subject of the position and
influence of the military in Japanese
politics. According to Mr. Kurusu, the
Japanese military leaders are idealistic
men of simple mind bent on ending
corruption and injustice in the political
parties and the capitalistic group.
Mr. Kurusu says that he has warned some
of them that the army would make a great
mistake to try to construct the economic
and social system which it believes should
displace the corrupt and unjust system
complained of, and he states that a
number of military men have seen the force
of is suggestion, recognizing that
sole ers are not specialists in sociology
and economics. Kurusu expresses the
opinion that the military are gradually
7
coming to realize that they are not
omniscient, and he thinks that Manchuria
has been a good lesson to them.

military in the politics of the nation. Mr. Kurusu is of
the opinion that, having succeeded in eliminating some

attempt

=== Page 193 === (confidence 0.98)
