Enclosure No. 1 to despatoh
No.1645 of January 21, 1936, from
the Embassy at Tokyo.
MEMORANDUM
January 20, 1936.
Conversetion: Mr. S. Kurusu, of the Foreign Office.
Mr. Dickover.
In the course of two informal conversations recently,
Mr. Kurusu discussed the position and influence of the Japanese
K.1
Army in Japanese politics. Mr. Kurusu said that he liked
the Army men, because they were sincere and honest. (He
referred to them several times as "those simple-minded Army
fellows" but I do not think that he used the phrase "simple-
minded" in its usual sense of "feeble-minded". I think that
he rather meant that the Army men are single-minded or narrow-
minded.) He said that the military are undoubtedly sincere
and devoted to the best interests of their country, as they
see those interests.
I told r. Kurusu that some foreign observers in Japan
held the opinion that the military were working toward a return
to the days of the Tokugawa Shogunate, when Japan was ruled
by a military oligarchy and the country was closed to foreign
influence. I asked him if he thought that there was any real
basis for such a belief. Kurusu seid that he did not think
so; that Japan could never now return to the Shogunate days.
He said that of course Japan had a tradition of military rule,
as it had been ruled by military oligarchies for many centuries
before the Restoration, and consequently was perhaps better
adapted to a military rule than to democracy. Nevertheless
Japen would not return to a military dictatorship. That wes
not the underlying idea of the military in any case. Their
conception of the state was that all Japanese are children
of the Emperor, and as such are all equal under the Emperor.
工

=== Page 194 === (confidence 0.98)
13-4
x
-2-
I remarked that this theory of the state seemed rather
idealistic and that in practice it did not seem to work out,
as Japan still had privileged and unprivileged classes,
aristocrats and commoners, rich people and poor people.
Kurusu said that that was quite true, and that the military
could not correct the differences in classes and wealth.
He said that the military were very vague about that part of
their conception of the state. The military felt that capi-
talism and special privilege were wrong but they did not know
how to correct the abuses. During the May 15th incident,
they shot down representative capitalists but they could not
replace them or the capitalistic system, and they had no con-
structive program for Japan. That was where the leftists came
into the picture, and consequently we have the strange sight
of the leftists and the extreme rightists (the reactionaries)
working more or less together with much the same ideas. After
the May 15th incident the Army did not know what to do, but
the leftists had some sort of program and so the Army clutched
at that program and tried to use it.
Kurusu said that it was the special privileges conferred
upon the capitalists by the political parties which caused
the outbreak in 1932 (known as the May 15th Incident) end
which caused the Army to decide to break up government by the
parties. Corruption in the parties had gone too far, and the
military decided that they mist do something to clean house.
I remarked that corruption seemed to be a feature of all govern-
ments and that it seemed to be impossible to eradicate it
entirely. Kurusu agreed and said that he had often told the
military

=== Page 195 === (confidence 0.98)
12-5
- 3 -
military that the Army itself would find corruption
creeping into its ranks if it continued very long to be the
dominant factor in the Japanese Government. He said that
he talks to the military in parables, as they understand
better when he does. He tells them that the Army is like a
servant sent to sweep out a dirty room. The Army has done
the sweeping, but now they must not try to place the chair
here and the table there. That should be left to others
who know better than does the Army the proper way to arrange
the furniture. If the Army tries to arrange the fur-
niture, mistakes will be made, because the military people
know nothing of politics and economies. Things will then
be worse than before, because the Japanese nation will have
no one left to trust. The military should leave the arrang-
ing of the chairs and tables to those who know something
of politics and economies.
Kurusu said that many Jepanese officials and business
men think as he does, but that they are afraid to talk
to the military, because of the fear of assassination.
He himself is not afraid to talk, because the military
know him as sincere and not self-seeking. He had talked
along these lines to General Nagata, who was recently
assassinated, and the General had expressed full agree-
ment with Kurusu's views. I asked if there was any con-
nection between the General's agreement with Kurusu's
views and his assassination. Kurusu said that he thought
not and that Generel Nagata was killed because of a dif-
ference of opinion between the older men in the Army and
the younger, hot-headed officers. Kurusu said that he
had recently expressed his views to General Hishikari
Hi,Takash.
(formerly

=== Page 196 === (confidence 0.99)
12-6
- 4 -
formerly Japanese Ambassador to "Manchukuo") and that
Hishikari had agreed with him and asked him to give a
talk along the same lines to a group of generals. Kurusu
said that he told Hishikari that that would do little good -
what he wanted to do was to talk to a group of colonels
and lieutenant-colonels, who were the men who were making
the trouble.
Kurusu said that the military were gradually coming to
V
realize that they were not omniscient. Manchuria was a good
lesson to them. When they first started to govern Manchuria
after the Manchurian Incident of 1931, they announced that
they were going to run the country for the benefit of all
the people and not for the profit of a group of capitalists.
So in the first stage the military said to the Japanese
capitalists, "We do not want you in Manchuria". Then they
found that Manchuria needed cepital, end so in the second
stage they said to the capitalists, " You can come into
I anchuria". But the capitalists are afraid of the sort of
government that the military have in Manchuria and only
invest money there very reluctantly and under pressure.
So now, in the third stage, the military are seying to
the capitalists, "Why don't you come in?"
I asked Kurusu if he thought that, in case the Diet is
dissolved and a general election is held, the military would
permit a party government to be formed on the basis of the
results of the elections. Kurusu said that he did not think
that a party government would be possible for some time yet,
as the parties were still too corrupt and would have to
carry out many internal reforms before the people and the
military would trust them.
E. R. D.

37, February 26, 10 a. m. (SECTION FOUR).
Six. As far as the Embassy has yet been able to
ascertain the unrising is in the nature of a coup d'etat
engineered by the young Fascist element in the army and
intended to destroy the entire group of elder statesmen
who have been advisers to the Throne and thereby to
effect the so-called "Showa restoration". The Emperor
himself is apparently being held incommunicado in the
Palace presumably to prevent anyone from obtaining access
to him and securing an Imperiak mandate which would inter-
fere with the plans of the army group. The immediate
causes of the uprising appear to have been the recent
election which returned an unexpectedly large number
of tho more liberal candidates and the trial of Aizawa,
murderer of General Nagata, which has excited the feel-
ings of the Fascist element in the army.
Latest reports indicate that General Mazaki is the
leading

Secretary of State,
Washington.
41, February 27, 1 p. m. (SECTION TWO).
Five. According to the best information available
to the mbassy, about half of the insurgent troops have
returned to their barracks; the other half has been grad-
ually evacuating the Government buildings occupied yester-
day and has been concentrated in the Premier's official
residence. Martial law was proclaimed largely to enable
the army to negotiate with the insurgents on an entirely
legal basis without involving other interests. It is
within the power of the General in command of the martial
law area to order the arrest of the men who refuse to
return to thcir commands but it is not likely that this
will be done except as a last resort when all other methods
fail. From the Premior's residence the young officers who
directed the uprising aro negotiating with the present
government and the army authorities. Exact facts in ro-
gard to those negotiations are impossible to obtain but
it is understood that at first the insurgents demanded a
military

=== Page 231 === (confidence 0.99)
20-4
FS
2-No. 41, February 27, 1 p. m. from Tokyo
military dictatorship for Japan. This demand was
immediately rejected by the Government (then meeting
in Council at the Palace) which suggested Prince Konoye
or General Araki as Premier. The insurgents recommended
Admiral Kanji Kato or Baron Hiranuma. The negotiations
are continuing and will probably result in a compromise
Premier. General Araki, Prince Higashi Kuni and Admiral
Eisuke Yamamoto, Supreme War Councilor, are being most
prominently mentioned at the moment.
Six. No forceful measures are being taken nor, as
far as the Lmbassy is aware, are being contemplated against
the insurgent troops. It is oxplainod that to arrest
them would involve fighting in the heart of Tokyo and
might result in the destruction of buildings and loss
of life among the civilian population; that the use of
force against political insurgents is opposed to Japan-
ese custom; that no Japanese army officer would order
the Emperor's soldiers to fire on other soldiers of the
Emperor; that the fighting if it took place would be too
close to the Palace of the Emperor; and that in any case
there is considerable sympathy among the people for the
motives which animated the misguided young men and con-
sequently a certain amount of consideration must be shown
them

20-5
FS
8-No. 41, February 27, 1 n. m. from Tokyo
them or the army will find itself possessed of a erowd
of martyrs which must be avoided at almost any cost.
Soven. The Embassy feels that until the present
emorgency situation is further clarified and settled
there is not sufficient ground to justify diagnosis of
the full import of the affair or conjecture as to its
bearing on futuro policy.
Ropatod to Poiping. (END OF MISSAG).

Three. Censorship on news despatches abroad was

This provilege was rescinded this afternoon and it is

Four. The death of Finance Minister Takahashi on
the 36th has now been officially announced. As yet there
has been little perceptible effect in financial circles,
as the stock and bond markets are closed, but the news-
papers report that there have been heavy withdrawals from
deposits in banks throughout the country.
Five. One light cruiser and eight destroyers from
Yokosuka tied up at Shibaura yesterday morning and the
first fleet arrived in Tokyo Bay this morning for guard
duty. No bluejackets or marines have been landed except
to guard the Ministry of the Navy.
Six. Several vernacular newspapers this morning
commented in regard to the next Premier and Cabinet.

fall within the category of "moderates"; most of them

and today
The events of yesterday/can be interpreted enly in
the light of the past. For some time there has been in
Japan a strong "reactionary-radical" element whioh has
been desoribed by many commentators as "Fasoist". On a
number of occasions there have been politioal assassima-
tions and forceful demonstrations in evidence of the
existence of this element. In each of such affairs there
has apparently been partioipation by so-called "younger"
officers of the Japanese Army and/or Navy. Objectives of
these "radioal revolutionaries" have been, as declared by
their spokemmen, restoration of direot rule of the
Emperor, removal of "false" and "umwise" advisers olese
to the Emperor, and "puriricatien" of the govermment.
The immediate cause of the present outbreak is

"reastionary-radical" element in the Army has been resemt-

military ("warrior") class in Japan have for cemturies

of the country and the guardians of the persem and the
authority of the Emperor. Representative constitusiemal

not effeetively taken root.
Statements put out by participants in the soup sug-

The telegrams received from Tokyo this morning
cover so completely the concluding developments in the
ohapter of this week's tragedy enaoted in that capital
that little if anything in addition by way of eluoidation
894.00/59
or oomment is needed.
On February 29, at 1l a.m. in Tokyo, Mr. Grew stated
that broadcasts were ordering those of the insurgents
who had not yet done so to obey the Emperor's commands
6
6
that they return to their barracks. All traffio had been
stopped in the martial law area and as far as Yokohama.
The populace had been warned to keep out of possible lines
of fire. Communications abroad had been stopped exoept
for Government messages. The atmosphere had been tense
but during the morning a "relaxation of this tense atmosphere
was noticeable". It was evident that the Government was
A
FILED
making every possible effort to persuade the insurgents to
disband without resorting to armed attack.
At 1 p.m., Mr. Grew telegraphed that additional
insurgents had surrendered and it was expeoted that the
disturbance would be settled soon.
At 4 p.m.,
F/1
F

=== Page 307 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
At 4 p.m., Mr. Grew telegraphed "The anmouneement has
just been made that all the revolting troops had surrendered
and that normal conditions will be restored at 4:lo this
morning (sio.?)".
At 6 p.m., Mr. Grew telegraphed that exoellent order
had been maintained and no injury or molestation to Amorican
citizens had been reported to the Embassy.
At 7 p.m., Mr. Grew telegraphed that Baron Hayashi
had informed him that Prime Minister Okada was alive and
uninjured; and that it was reported that Okada had resumed
the Premiership.
In this telegram, Mr. Grew commented "The implications
of this dramatic turn of events are too obvious to need
comment. Our first reaction to the circumstances of the
past four days is that they may well cause a revulsion of
feeling against the military throughout the country and that
the final results may be salutary." It was reported that
from fifteen to twenty officers not higher than the rank
of captain had been involved; their fate was unknown; but
it was generally thought that they might have been allowed
to commit suicide.
Comment:
This Division continues to be of the opinion expressed
in the conoluding sentence of our memorandum of February 26:
"However, the coup itself may be regarded as
simply another incident in an effort which has
been going on for several years on the part of an
element in the military organization toward making
the voice of the military organization completely
authoritative in the affairs of the state."
The

=== Page 308 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
▶
The first and most obvious result of the ceup has beem
the elimination of several powerful figures from among the
moderates in the highest governmental.cirele. The mest
important of those removed was Takahashi -- who had been
the most resolute of all in his fearless and effeetive
opposition to the most extreme of the inclinations of the
Army. In that portion of the Japanese population which has
been antipathetic to the Army, the antipathy will probably
have been intensified. That element, however, has not
been in control and this inoident will by no means bring it
within reach of a controlling position. For the moment the
Army is brought nearer to completeness of authority -- ir
only by virtue of the fact that the grip of those who oppose
that consummation has been shaken and diminished. Whether,
in the presence of a diminished opposition within the Govern-
ment, the extremist or radical view within the Army, intent
upon pursuit of a more "positive" policy in connection with
foreign affairs (especially relations with China), will
result in the immediate future in new manifestations of
military activity (in support of diplomatic pressure) in
China remains to be seen. It is too early to predict, but
the likelihood of such a development has probably been
increased.

Department of State
DIVISION OF
The Military Coup in Tokyo.
The perpetrators of the military coup in Tokyo still
occupy certain Government and other buildings and negotia-
894.
tions on the part of the Japanese Government direoted toward
their peaceful return to their quarters have so far proved
unsuccessful.
894.00/603
In the event that the Government continues to be
unsuccessful in its efforts to effect by peaceful methods
the return to their barracks of the soldiers who participated
in the coup, it is probable that the Government will feel
compelled to resort to force. Should fighting ocour, the
effect on the future course of government in Japan would be
highly problematical. It is possible that continued defiance
of the Government on the part of the recalcitrant soldiers
might cause a popular revulsion of feeling in Japan against
the soldiers and a resultant diminishing of the influence of
4 1936
the military.
ED
At present the weight of evidence points toward a
compromise settlement which would result in the attaininbya
the military element in Japan of an even stronger influence
in the affairs of the state than they have hitherto had.
m.mid
F/FG
M.m.H.
MMH/REK

1936 NAR 6 AM 9 30
9
March 4, 1936.
THE JAPANESE AMBASSADOR, MR. HIROSI SAITO.
Trade restoration; Japanese rebellion.
The Japanese Ambassador called and handed me the
following note in writing from Foreign Minister Hirota:
894.00/604
"I am most grateful to you for your kind
message of condolence transmitted to me by the
American Ambassador in Tokyo, and, on behalf of
the Japanese Government, I wish to convey to you
and through you to the Únited States Government
our most sincere thanks for this manifestation
of sympathy."
The Ambassador and I exchanged a few general state-
ments about the restoration of international trade, in
the course of which I emphasized the broad program which
this Government was pursuing, and, without referring to
Japan, I endeavored to point out the necessity for the
restoration at the earliest possible date of the normal
processes of international finance and commerce. The
Ambassador suggested that our two nations had similar
views on this general subject. He then added that there
were some small minor troubles in our trade situation
MAR 6 1936
FILED
which had been the subject of more or less discussion
and misunderstandings. I replied that this Government
was
F/11

=== Page 314 === (confidence 0.99)
40-2
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
THE SECRETARY
-2-
was carrying all the political load possible in its
efforts to go forward with our program of liberalizing
commercial and general trade policies; that the small
overflows of imports with respect to given commodities
were very combustible politically; and that it was neces-
sary that we should compose the troubles which they
presented by amicable agreements, if and when at all
possible; otherwise, we were obliged to take other
necessary steps.
The Ambassador seemed agreeably disposed towards
special agreements, such as we had been making. I re-
plied further that it was either this or a possible
cessation of our trade restoration movement and the
still further increase of tariff that would soon fol-
low, with the result that Japan would be much worse off
than if she entered into these special agreements, re-
stricting voluntarily certain quantities of exports
from Japan to the United States.
The Ambassador volunteered the suggestion that the
recent explosion in Japan, planned at the 1nstigation
of certain young army officers with the result that a
number of Japan's statesmen were assassinated, was not
a

=== Page 315 === (confidence 0.99)
40-3
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
THE SECRETARY
-3-
a controversy between the ultra-radical and conserva-
tive elements in the military group, but that it was the
reaction of the soldiers to seriously discriminatory
economic treatment of farmers and small business men in
Japan by what we would call big business in this country.
The Ambassador said that these officers, without the
knowledge of their superiors, sought to resent the action
of the Government in playing into the hands of what we
would call the big predatory interests at the expense
of the farmers and small business men whose suffering
was growing steadily worse. The Ambassador went on to
say that seven generals had resigned because they were
in charge of the military forces of which the rebellion
companies were a part; he said that the generals knew
nothing about the plans for this uprising and the sys-
tematic assassination, but that, of course, the generals
were chargeable with the responsibility for the results,
and hence their resignation.
The Ambassador stated he felt now that conditions
were comparatively calm and normal the Government un-
questionably would work out a list of cabinet members
who would make up a satisfactory government, and by that
the

=== Page 316 === (confidence 0.99)
40-4
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
THE SECRETARY
-4
the Ambassador said he meant a government more con-
siderate of the rights and interests of the farmers
and the small business men and less under the domina-
tion of big business in Japan, which he said had rapidly
accumulated wealth by trading, while the other classes
mentioned suffered correspondingly.
C. H.

Hirota has succeeded in forming a Cabinet. He will
at least tomporarily continue as Foreign Minister owing
to the army's disapproval of Yoshida. Other Ministers
of importance are Babe, Finance; Count Terauchi, War;
Nagano, Navy; Ushio, Home Affairs; Hayashi, Justice.
894.00/608
Baba is a well-known banker of conservative lean-
ings who has been President of the Hypothec Bank for
several years. The choice is generally considered a
good one as he has no strong affiliations with any
political or financial groups, knows the agricultural
land situation and is thoroughly familiary with long-
term financing.

chi and has the roputation of being indopendont and

new Cabinet will be sent by mail. The Cabinet appears
to have the support of the two principal political
parties as members of each of them have portfolios.
The choices for Home and Justice posts indicate an in-
vestigation of those departments which the public has
long demanded.

One. The choice of Hirota as Prime Minister and
his determination to hold the direction of foreign
affairs in his own hands owing to the army's disapproval
of Yoshida tends to support the statement made to me
by the Vice Minister on March 2nd to the effect that
the improvement of Japan's foreign relations was given
careful consideration in the selection of the new Cab-
inct.
Two. The primary causes of the recent insurrection
FILED
1
were domestic rather than international issues. The in-
2
1936
cident was an expression of basic social and economic
maladjustments in Japan having root in the poverty of
the masses of the people especially in certain rural
distficts, the absence of the stabilizing influence of
a large powerful middle class and the alleged exploita-
F/FG
tion of the nation by the very wealthy who assertedly
use

=== Page 327 === (confidence 1.00)

LMS 2-No. 66, March 9, 11 p. m. from Tokyo.
use the politicians and statesmen as their tools.
Three. While the "direct action" group of younger
officers in the army responsible for the recent inoi-
dent it comparatively small a considerable section of
the army as a whole believes that it has a duty under
the Emperor to relieve the alleged exploitation of the
people, to abolish political corruption and to unify
the nation in a manner which they vaguely relate to
ancient Japanese conceptions. They desire inter alia
"new deal" and in this they are supported by the great
mass of the people. To achieve this "new deal" will be
the task of the new government, the third "emergency
cabinet" organized since May 15, 1932. As no govern-
ment in Japan can long exist under the opposition of
the army the new Cabinet will have to deal drastically
and effectively with the root caues of the recent
insurrection or be foräed out of office with the possi-
bility of further violence.
Four. Predictions as to foreign policies would
be premature but it is logical to assume that in con-
centrating on essential domestic problems and readjust-
ments the tendency of the Government as long as Hirota
is in control will be to tranquilize Japan's foreign
relations. Only time will show whether Hirota will

ATMENTSTAT
MAR 1 1 1936
MAR 7
March 6, 1
Department of State
NOTED
956 MAR 7
the Secretary of State and the
1 Japanese Ambassador, March 4, 1936.
TIONS
The explanation given by the Japanese Ambassador, as
recorded in the paragraph which begins at the bottom of
page 2 of the memorandum under reference, of the assassi-
nations which occurred in Tokyo last week is altogether
too "easy" and tends to throw the whole matter out of
perspective. Economic factors unquestionably contribute
894.00/6
to the feeling among certain large classes in Japan of
discontent and unrest, as well as to the feeling of
antipathy toward the commercial giants and their associates
which notoriously is widespread in Japan. But these
factors are, as regards the occurrences of last week, back-
ground factors. Extreme chauvinism exists in the Army and
has been by no means confined to one regiment or to the
so-called younger officers. The chauvinistic element in
the Army has resented the moderates' policy of restraint
for which the influences around the Emperor were considered
C2
(G
tha)
responsible. The chauvinistic element in the Army beldoyes,
apparently sincerely, that the policy of restraint is not
in the best interests of the country. In this view it has
widespread
F/FG

=== Page 338 === (confidence 0.96)
44-2
- 2 -
widespread support throughout the people of Japan. Political
assassination is, in Japan, a well recognized -- one may
almost say a time honored -- method of expressing political
resentment and disposing of officials who are felt to be
pursuing a mistaken policy. The perpetrators of last week's
assassination were expressing a view and making a political
move which was not original with or exolusive to them. They
represented the desire of many people, in many walks of life,
that the restrictions on the military favored by such men as,
for instance, Takahashi, be done away with, and the views
of the military be given the ascendancy.
SH

FE:SKH:CC/REK

THE SECRETARY OF STATE
WASHINGTON
SIR:
I have the honor to report that the recent coup
d'etat and political assassinations in Tokyo have had
considerable repercussions in Secretariat circles.
The opinion seems to be general that the military ex-
tremists will from now on have an even greater say in
the formation end execution of Japanese policy, and that
as a result the Far Esstern situation has reached a stage
which may imminently have to receive the consideration of
Geneva.
This would be particularly so if the new Govern-
MAR17 1936
FILED
ment in Jspan should, as may be expected, adopt a stiffened
attitude and a more positive policy towards China and the
Soviet Union, both League members.
This morning a high official of the Secretariat who
is unusually well informed on the Far Eastern question told
a member of my staff that yesterday he had received a private
telegram from T. V. Soong despatched the day the new Japanese
Ambassador landed in Shanghai (the day the Tokyo assassi-
F/FG
nations

=== Page 341 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
nations took place).
In his telegram Soong asserted
that before leaving Tokyo the Ambassador had received
instructions to demand China's assent to the establish-
ment of the autonomy of the northern provinces and the
status quo in Manchukuo.
The conclusion my informant
drew from Soong's assertion was that the plans for the
coup were known in advance to high officials of the
Japanese Government who accordingly issued instructions
in the sense mentioned to the newly appointed Ambassador
in anticipation of the success of the coup and to appease
in advance the military extremists who they expected
shortly would establish their control of the Government.
I send the foregoing information to the Department
for what it may be worth considering the source.
My informant particularly requested that the fact
that he is in communication with Soong be kept strictly
confidential.
Respectfully yours,
fumli Br
Prentiss B. Gilbert,
American Consul.

would procced on the basis of the three points already

He did so as follows.
(a) Cessation of anti-Japanese propaganda and

(b) Recognition of the existence of Mauchukuo
and regularization of factual relations such as communi-
cations, transit, customs, et cetera. This need not
(repeat not) imply de jure recognition.
(c) Sino-Japanese cooperation to combat the spread
of communism.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE

Situation in Tokyo
6.2
FAR EASTERN 
NOTED
AMAR 7-1936
Hirota as Premier: Possible Effeot
#2
on Japan's China Polioy.
The appointment of Mr. Hirota as Premier of Japan,
reported in this morning's press, suggests the possibility
89
of oloser cooperation between the military element and the
"civilian" element -- especially the Foreign Office segment --
than has prevailed for some time past.
894.00/614
When Hirota was made Minister for Foreign Affairs, the
first accounts forthcoming from Tokyo characterized Hirota
as being emphatically a nationalist. Soon there followed
denials of this. But no denial was made of the early state-
ments that Hirota was, as among the "diplomats", persona
grata to the Army and that his appointment to the Foreign
fice was satisfactory to the military element.
As Foreign Minister, Hirota has had no small amount
of difficulty with both the Army and the Navy, but the
contests have been over matters of method rather than over
FILED
objectives in the determining of foreign policy and the oon-
4
duoting of foreign relations.
169
Japanese-Chinese cooperation. One feature of this program
calls for a "common front against communism". There has
since been between Hirota and Japanese Army officers engaged
F1
in diplomatic activities in China close cooperation in
/FG
strenuous

=== Page 351 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
strenuous efforts to cause the Nanking Government to aose
and act upon Hirota's "three-point program". In the now
of the past few days, it has been indicated that the new
Japanese Ambassador to China has been pressing the Nanking
Government with regard to this matter. Thus, Hirota is
clearly, completely, and at the present moment, committed
to the policy of "putting over" an acceptance by China of
that proposed program.
The elevation of Hirota to the premiership in consequence
of the tragic removal last week of several of the foremost
"moderates" from the directorate of Japan's political affairs
and in pursuance of an unquestionable desire on the part of
the Emperor and such statesmen as Prince Saionji to heal the
breach between the extreme elements in the Army and the
"moderates" in the higher administration suggests that Hirota
is regarded as a man possibly capable of resolving the confliot
between the chauvinistic inclinations of the one element and
the inclination to proceed cautiously of the other element.
Hirota's appointment to the premiership at the present junoture
would indicate that he is still, as he was when he was made
Foreign Minister, persona grata to the military element. It
suggests that there is expectation on the part of that element
that he will be vigorous in pursuance of a "positive" polioy
in regard to China.
Sab

PERI INER TAGEEIATT
February 27,1930.
Zu den Ereignissen in Japan
P. Sch. Die Verhängung des Kriegszustandes über
Japan und die Beorderung von Geschwadern in die Näbe
Tokios und Osakas, der grössten Handelsstadt des Landen,
beides Massregeln, die während der Nacht verfügt wurden.
könnten den Eindruck machen, dass unmittelbare Gefahr
für die Ordnung in Japan noch im Verzuge sei. Aber da
die Börse in Tokio geöffnet ist und damit alle anderen
Geldmärkte in Japan, und da die Banken arbelten, die
Schalter auszahlen, so handelt es sich bei jenen Verfü-
gungen offenbar um Vorsichtsmassregeln. Der Putsch, der
einen Augenblick den Eindruck machte, er könne zum
Staatsstreich werden, wird nun mit grosser Bedächtigkeit
abgewickelt. Selbst nach den letsten Nachrichten ist an-
zunehmen, dass die Aufrührer noch in gewissen Mini-
sterien sich befinden, so im Innenministerium. Es wird
nicht mit scharfen Massregeln gegen sie vorgegangen, dies
allein beweist schon, dass die Regierung überzeugt ist.
Herr der Lage zu sein.
Da jeder sich selbst der Nächste ist, so hat die Welt
die Ereignisse des Mittwochmorgen zunächst vom auasen-
politischen Standpunkt genommen. Es ist viel darüber
geschrieben und geredet worden, dass sich die Kriegs-
gefahr in Fernost, durch einen japanisch-sowjetischen
Krieg, nun erheblich verstärkt habe, ja, dass sie nun ganz
dringend geworden sei. Das Umgekehrte ist das Wahr-
scheinlichere. Es handelt sich mehr um innerpolitische
Fragen als um äussere. Ihre Austragung zieht von Unter
nehmungen über die Grenzen hinaus ab, fördert sie nicht.
Die politischen Bluttaten, die seit 1932 Japan erschüttert
haben, sind in wesentlichen aus den tiefen innerpolitischen
Spannungen hervorgegangen, die in Japan seit langem be-
stehen. Sie sind durch die Wirtschaftskrise der Welt seht
verschärft worden. Man denke nur an die Ausfälle dei
Seidenbauern, deren Erzeugnisse vor allem nach den Ver-
einigten Staaten ausgeführt wurden und dort ihren Markt
stark eingebüsst haben. Die japanische Armee. es sei
noch einmal wiederholt, ist eine Bauernarmee.
Die städtische Arbeiterschaft lebt verhältnismässig gut
und hat Beschäftigung, aber es sind eben die Bauern, die
mit hohen Schuldzinsen und Pachten dafür aufkommen
müssen. Die Mannschaften fühlen das, die unteren
Chargen erleben es mit ihnen. So stellt sich die Lage der
jungen Offiziere dar — die jungen Offiziere", das ist all-
mählich ein politischer Begriff geworden. Sie sind im-
perialistisch, sie mögen panasiatisch gesonnen sein, aber
gegenwärtig schwebt ihnen vor allem der Gedanke vor.
dass es nicht möglich ist, im Inneren Gerechtigkeit zu
schaffen, noch hoffen sie, eine auswärtige Politik, die
Japans Lebensrechten genug tut, zu sehen, solange nicht
im Inneren Reformen durchgeführt sind. Sie müssen vor
allem die Verfassung treffen, den Missbrauch des Parla-
ments, wie sie sagen. Dass sie aber zur Feudalverfassung.
zu dem Shogun-System zurückkehren wollen, ist sehi
zweifelhaft.
Ex exisliert bisher nur eine offizielle Darstellung der
Ereignisse. Sie stanmt von dem japanischen Kriogs-
ministerium. Dort finden wir im letzten Absatz ohne alle
weitere Bewertungen und Erläuterungen die Ziele der
jungen Offiziere" aufgezählt. Diese Aufzählung und ihre
Form bestätigen die Auffassung, die der Leser soeben ver-
nommen hat. Es ging, so sagt das Kriegsministerium kühl.
gegen den Rat der Alten, die alten Minister, die Kapita-
listen, die Parteien, da sie in schwerer Zeit unsere Staats
form zerstören wollten". Mit zerstören" ist offenbar der
Missbrauch gemeint, den die jungen Offiziere jenen Kreisen
in der Handhabung des Parlamentes vorwerfen zu dürten
glauben.

dass nicht eine andere Stelle, sondern das Kriegsministe-

welcher Ar', liegt Anlass, sich die Tat der jungen Offi-

=== Page 359 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No.2
Translation
from
BERLINER TAGEBLATT
February 27,1936.
No "Putsch"
Concerning Occurrences in Japan.
P.Sch.
A state of war being declared in Japan and
the first fleet being commanded to the vicinity of
Tokyo and Osaka, the largest commercial city of the
country, which measures were both ordered during the
night, might create the impression that danger is
still threatening order in Japan.
.But as the stock
exchange in Tokyo is open, and consequently all the
other money markets in Japan, and as the banks are
operating and the tellers are paying out money, the
orders mentioned above are apparently precautionary
measures.
The putsch, which for a moment appeared
as if it might develop into a coup d'atat, is now
being settled with great deliberation. Even accord-
ing to the latest reports it is to be assumed that
the rebels are still to be found in certain minis-
tries, for instance, the Ministry of the Interior.
No severe measures are being adopted against them,
which alone proves that the Government is convinced
that it is master of the situation.
As everyone is most interested in himself, the
world viewed the occurrences of Wednesday morning
primarily from the foreign political angle.
Much
has

=== Page 360 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
has been written and said about the menace of war in
the Far East being greatly aggravated, in fact, hav-
ing become very acute by the danger of a Japanese-
Soviet war.
The contrary is more probable. It is
more a matter of internal than foreign political ques-
tions.
The settlement of these questions derogates
from such undertakings beyond the borders of the coun-
try - does not enhance them.
The political murders which have been upsetting
Japan since 1932 originated chiefly in the deep inter-
nal political tension which has existed for a long time
in Japan.
They were very much aggravated by the econ-
omic crisis throughout the world.
For example, the
case of the silkworm growers, whose products were ex-
ported mainly to the United States and who lost their
market there to a considerable extent. The Japanese
army - let it be repeated once more - is an army of
peasants.
The laborers in the cities live comparatively
well and have employment, but it is the peasants,
after all, who have to answer for it with high inter-
est on debts and high leases. The troops feel that,
the lower ranks experience it with them.
This is how
the situation of the young officers presents itself -
the "young officers" - a term which has gradually
come to have a political meaning.
They are imperial-
istic, they may incline towards being pan-Asiatic, but
at the present time the idea they have in mind is chief-
ly that it is not possible to bring about internal jus-
tice

=== Page 361 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
tice, nor do they hope to witness a foreign policy
that does justice to Japan's vital rights, so long
as no internal reforms are carried out. They must
first of all strike at the Constitution - misuse of
Parliament, as they say.
. But that they intend to
revert to the feudal constitution, to the Shogun sys-
tem, is very doubtful.
So far there has been only an official exposi-
tion of events. It comes from the Japanese Ministry
of War.
In the last paragraph thereof we find an
enumeration of the aims of the "young officers," with-
out any further comment or explanations.
This enumer-
ation and the form thereof confirm the opinion which
the reader has just read. This, says the Minister of
War cooly, was an attack on the "council of elders, the
old ministers, the capitalists and the parties, since
they determined to destroy our form of government at
a difficult time." By "destroy" apparently is meant
the misuse in the manipulation of parliament of which
the young officers feel they are justified in accus-
ing those circles.
In this wording of the communiqué, in the fact
that it was not issued by some other department but
by the Ministry of War, without any counter-signature
whatever, offers ground to look upon the deed of the
young officers as a starting point for an intensive
and decisive controversy between both sides. In that
case the revolution of the "young officers," which
must

=== Page 362 === (confidence 0.99)
-4
must be in close connection with the victory of the
government in the elections of last Sunday, was not
just an episode, a mere putsch, but a part of a po-
litical development, the course of which cannot yet
be foreseen.
AC:EM

report the coup at Tokyo on the previous day.

2/+7

Enclosure No. 1 to Despatch No.920
of February 29, 1936, from the Embassy at Istanbul.
MEMORANDUM OF CONVERSATION
between S. Walter Washington, Second Secretary
of Embassy, and Mr. Otosugu Saito, Seoretary of
the Japanese Embassy in Istanbul.
February 28, 1936.
After reading about the assassinations in Japan I called
the Secretary of the Japanese Embassy on the telephone yester-
day morning.
. He greeted me in an excited voice, saying "I
 told you so, I knew there would be more trouble".
I asked
him whether he would like to come to my house in the after-
noon and he immediately accepted my invi tation.
He arrived
about 5 o'clock and there is no doubt but that he was very
much agitated. He told me that these assassinations were
inevitable for the reasons which he had explained in our pre-
vious conversation. (See Memorandum of January 30, 1936.)
He said that until the civilian statesmen of Japan adopt the
views of the younger military officers the latter will con-
tinue these acts of violence both for the purpose of punishing
the persons whom they consider to have betrayed their country
and for the purpose of elimina ting them from posi tions of
power. He said that he hoped the rec ent incidents would be
sufficient to convert the civilian statesmen to the views
of the younger officers.
I asked whether the younger officers did not hold rather
naive views which they would find impossible of fulfillment
if they should come into power. He replied that after the
Manchurian incident of 1931 the younger officers had begun
to

=== Page 383 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
to take the advice of certain selected advisors on economic
and related matters.
He said that furthermore they had been
studying very hard themselves.
He said that the civil of-
ficials of the Japanese Government should be ashamed of them-
selves for their comparative lack of energy in studying about
their own country.
He said that he himself had been with
the younger officers both in Japan and in North China and
had found them extremely serious and hardworking in studying
political, social, and economic conditi ons.
He said that he
for one would be very willing to en trust the government to the
hands of the younger offic ers.
He insisted that the aims of the younger military offi-
cers are directed purely toward internal reforms and that when
one of the army leaders of whom they approve, such as General
Areki, becomes premier they will be satisfied.
I asked him
whether he thought they could really accomplish their purpose
of distributing the wealth of the country more evenly without
destroying the efficient organizations which have developed
Japan's foreign commerce. He admitted that here lies one
great danger. I also asked him whether the public debt of Ja-
pan was not already very high.
He said that it was not as
high per inhabitant as that of many other countries of the
world. I stated that when I was in Japan Finance Minis ter
Takahashi had fixed a limit of ten billion yen for the public
debt and that this limit had already been exceeded.
He re-
plied that the Finance Minister had fixed such a limit so
that there would be potential sources of revenue for Japan
to tap in case of a foreign war.
I asked Mr. Saito vhat he thought was the attitude of
the

=== Page 384 === (confidence 0.97)
-3-
the Japenese Emperor toward the young military group. He
said that he believed that there was no doubt that the Em-
peror sympathized with them. He said that the Emperor un-
doubtedly was sorry to see his old advisors assassinated
but that his views on policy would undoubtedly agree with
those of the militarists. I said that I had heard it said
in Tokyo in 1932 that the young military officers had con-
sidered disposing of the Emperor in some way so that prince
Chichibu, whom they considered to be more favorable to them,
might become Emperor. He insisted that this had never been
in the minds of the officers;
that they consider the Em-
peror to be on their side but that he had had the wrong
advisors.
He said that all of the violent plots in Japan
would cease if the Emperor would appear in public and make
a statement indicating his views on the ma tters about which
there are differences of opinion between the military and
the civilian officials.
.He said that unfortunately the Em-
peror's advisors had forbidden him to do this.
1
I asked him why Prince Tokugawa had apparently never
been threatened by the military and he replied that the
Prince had successfully held himself aloof from these dis-
putes. He added that the Prince's son, the present Ambas-
sador to Turkey and his own chief, also appeared to be un-
1
concerned about the internal political dissensions in Japan
01.1
and that he would undo ubtedly be able successfully to take
his father's place when he is called upon.
He said that Mr. Arita, the newly appointed Minister
to China, has never been in sympathy with the military's
aspirations and he does not know why he received the appoint-
ment.
In reply to my question, he said that most of the
Japanese

=== Page 385 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
1
Japanese diplomats have become converted to the military ?
cause.
I asked Mr. Saito whe ther he thought that there would
be a change in Japan's policy in the continent of Asia as a
result of the recent assassinations. He replied that he
did not think so as the younger officers are interested en-
tirely in internal refoms. In reply to my inquiry regarding
North China he stated that some kind of autonomy for North
China in financial and administrative matters is inevitable
but he denied my suggestion that it might be eventually in-
corporated with Manchuria.
He stated that the Japanese
army would never continue its support of the Emperor of Man-
chukuo if he should go below the Great Wall.
He reminded me
that Chang-tso-lin had tried to extend his power below the
Great Wall against the advice of the Japanese and had
failed.
With regard to Russia he continued to insist that Japan
does not want a war with Soviet Russia and that the Japanese
were not committing any kind of aggression against that
761.
country.
. He sta ted that the whole world knows that the Ja-
panese are not talkative and that for that reason they are
not good propagandists. He said that the Soviets therefore
have the advantage in gaining the support of world opinion.
He said that the Soviets have been committing acts of aggres-
sion against Manchukuo and that the Japanese soldiers have
kept quiet and suppressed their anger.
.He said that there
is a danger that eventually the Japanese officers situated
on the border will act to repel these aggressions and that
Japen will of course be blamed by world public opinion for
starting a war.
Mr.

=== Page 386 === (confidence 0.99)
-5 -
Mr. Saito said that his grea test desire is that Japan
and the United States should become more friendly.
He said
that the American people should put themselves in the shoes
of the Japanese.
He asked what policy the Japanese could
follow other than the one they are following, considering their
growing population and the fact that all the nations of the
world which are sui table for Japanese settlement have closed
their doors against them.
He added that the only course
open to them is to build up their ccuntry's foreign trade and
her sphere of influence in Asia.
I asked whether a large
army was necessary to build up foreign trade and he replied
that he thought that it was essential.
I s ta ted to him
that in my opinion the Uni ted Sta tes Government had shown
in many ways its understanding of Japan's need for commercial
expansion.
I told him that one Department of our Government
had recently published a statement analysing the recent
expansion of Japan's foreign trade and showing conclusively
that we do not lose anything by it, and even may be said to
gain because of the necessity for Japan to purchase increas-
ed supplies of raw materials from us.
I told him that this
was one of the many attempts by informed people of the Unit-
94
ed States to counteract the alarmists on the subject of Ja-
711.
pan's expansion.
I stated that, ho wever, the American
people are certainly alarmed by the rise of the military in
Japan and that I supposed the recent incidents would not
improve ma tters.
I said that we are naturally suspicious
of control by militarists.
He asked what Japan could gain
from a war with the United States.
When I mentioned the
Philippines he replied that they would not be valuable
enough to Japan to warrant a war.
I replied that I could
unders tand

=== Page 387 === (confidence 0.97)
-6-
understand this and that I wondered whe ther the Ameriean
people would consider them valuable enough to warrant re-
capturing if Japan should grab them. I added that I cer-
tainly agreed with him that a war between the two nations
would be unprofitable to both. We have a much larger com-
merce wi th Japan than with China and from an economic point
of view can gain much more by increasing our trade with his
i country than by trying to defend the Chinese trade.
Our interview was closed when I turmed on the radio
and we heard the announcement from London that Mr. Taka-
hashi, the Finance Minister, had died as a result of his
wounds.
This seemed to affect Mr. Saito very much and
I got the impression that he felt that violence had gone
too far. He very suddenly said good-bye and departed.

Tokyo's attached despatch No. 1721,
March 6, 1936, is a detailed, well-pre-
pared chronicle of the chief events
connected with the Tokyo uprising of
February 26 up until noon of March 6.
Most of the information was reported in
the Embassy's telegrams of that period
which were unusually complete.
Beginning on page 9 of the despatch
is an account of the military measures
taken to put down the uprising.
On page 8 is noted an interesting
comment made by a captain in the Japanese
army to the assistant military attaché
to the effect that the army in Japan
shared the sentiments of the insurgents
with the exception that the army in
general still believes in discipline.

SUBJECT: CHRONICLE OF THE EVENTS OF THE FEBRUARY 26, 1936,
UPRISING THROUGH NOON OF MARCH 6, 1936.

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
KFC
At about 5 o'clock on the morning of February 26
junior officers and men, numbering altogether about
1400 and belonging to the 3rd Infantry regiment of the
Imperial Guards Division and the 1st and 3rd regiments
and the 7th Heavy Artillery regiment of the 1st Tokyo
Division

=== Page 390 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Division, attacked the following places: the Premier's
official residence; the private residence of the Lord
Keeper of the Privy Seal, Viscount Makoto Sai to; the
private residence of the Inspector General of Military
Education, General Jotaro Watanabe; the Itoya Inn at
7
Yugawara where the former Lord Keeper of the Privy
Seal, Count Nobuaki Makino, was residing at the time;
the official residence of the Grand Chamberlain,
Admiral Kantaro Suzuki; the private residence of the
Finance Minister, Mr. Korekiyo Takahashi; and the
1/2
offices of the TOKYO ASAHI SHIMBUN. The officers
and soldiers were armed with machine guns, rifles and
bayonets and proceeded in trucks. Viscount Saito, Gen-
eral Watanabe, and Colonel Matsuo, the Premier's brother-
in-law, were instantly killed. The last named was mis-
taken for Premier Okada, who escaped. Mr. Takahashi
died shortly afterwards from his wounds. Admiral
Suzuki was seriously injured and his condition remains
uncertain. It is reiorted that the insurgents also
attempted to kill Prince Saionji, the Genro, but that
he escaped in a manner unknown as yet by the Embassy.
This information is not given in official bulletins.
The rebel group seized control of the Metropolitan
Police Board, the War Office, the General Staff Build-
ing, and the official residence of the Premier.* The
rebels flew a banner from the latter building, a rough
translation of which is as follows: "Loyalty to the
Emperor and up with the pure Army". They held these
buildings at least until the evening of the same day.
Subsequently
* Embassy's telegram No. 38, February 26, 1936.

=== Page 391 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
Subsequently the Peers' Club was taken over and held for a
short period. The new Diet Building was also ocoupied by
the insurrectionists, although it is not known when this
building was seized.
It should be remarked in passing that there is a current
report to the effect that the insurgents rehearsed the murders
two weeks previously under the guise of conducting military
maneuvers.
A mimeographed statement was left by groups of soldiers
at all the principal newspaper offices on the morning of the
attempted coup d'état. This statement, which was signed by
Captains Nonaka and Ando of the 3rd Infantry Regiment, alleged
that the present Government had been drifting away from the
true spirit of Japan and usurping the Emperor's prerogatives
and as evidence of this the signing of the London Naval Treaty
and the dismissal of General Mazaki were cited. It was further
stated that if such a condition continued the relations of Japan
to China, Soviet Russia, Great Britain, and the United States
would "become explosive in nature".*
The first official document of the War Office, issued at
8:15 p.m. on February 26, stated that according to the written
documents of the officers involved the purpose of the uprising
was to eliminate persons who were perpetrating the destruetion
of the national polity, such as the Genro, statesmen close to
11
the Throne, the financial magnates, the military clique, bureau-
crats, and the political parties at this moment of serious
crisis at home and abroad. The announcement further stated
that Premier Okada had been instantly killed.
News
* Embassy's telegram No. 37, February 26, 1936.

=== Page 392 === (confidence 0.99)
News of the uprising was received by the Embassy at
8:15 a.m. The Counselor of the Embassy succeeded in going
past the sentries at the Prime Minister's Residence at
about 9 o'clock on the morning of the outbreak and in en-
tering the Foreign Office, where the officials interviewed
seemed to have very little information about the affair. A
censorship was placed on outgoing news despatches in the
morning and foreign news correspondents were unable to send
cables out of the country. There was also a ban on the use
of the international telephone.
General Kawashima, the War Minister, Admiral Prince
Fushimi, Chief of the Naval Staff, Princes Higashi Kuni and
Asaka, Generals Mazaki, Araki, Hayashi, Abe, and Honjo and
Admiral Osumi, the Navy Minister, proceeded to the Palace
and met in conference to discuss what measures should be
taken by the Army. It is amusing to note that the Navy
Minister, apparently not caring to take any chances with
the Army, surrounded himself with a squad of bluejackets
equipped with machine guns when he proceeded to the Imperial
Palace.
1/
At 3 o'clock on the afternoon of the first day "a state
of emergency" was declared and Lieutenant General Kohei Kashii,
Commander of the Tokyo Garrison, was put in charge of the
situation. An official announcement of the Tokyo Garrison
1/
Headquarters was addressed to the general public by radio
broadcast at 7 o'clock explaining the "state of emergency".
The Navy Minister announced at 8:40 p.m. that the 1st
and 2nd Fleets had been ordered to Tokyo Bay and Osaka Bay
and

=== Page 393 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
and that the Yokosuka defense squadron had arrived in Tokyo
1/
Bay in the afternoon. At 9:10 p.m. the Home Office announoed
that peace and order were being maintained. Lieutenant Gen-
eral Kashii issued a statement at l0:25 p.m. to the effeot
that he had moved troops throughout the Capi tal to preserve
1/
peace and to protect important buildings.
On the evening of February 26, Mr. Goto, the Minister for
Home Affairs, was appointed as temporary Acting Premier.* It
was reported that Admiral Osumi had been offered the post but
had declined on the ground that Goto was his superior in the
Cabinet. Thus ended the first day.
February 27.
Mertial Law was declared at 3 o'clock on the morning of
3/
February 27 in accordance with an Imperial Ordinance.
During the morning it was reported to the Embassy that
the young rebel officers in the Premier's residence were ne-
gotiating with the Army authorities and the provisional Gov-
ernment.** It was understood that at first the insurgents had
demanded a military dictatorship under either Admiral Kanji
Kato, or Baron Hiranuma of the Privy Council while the Govern-
ment reportedly suggested Prince Konoye, President of the
House of Peers, or General Araki of the Supreme War Counoil
for the post of Premier. At first it appeared that no force-
ful measures were being contemplated against the insurgent
troops.***
The principal reasons for this were probably the
following: that the use of force against political insurgents
is not in keeping with the Japanese custom; that there was
sympathy among the people for the motives which prompted the
younger

** Embassy's telegram No. 41, February 27, 1936.
***Embassy's telegram No. 41, February 27, 1 p.m.

younger officers; and that the Army wanted above all to
avoid making mertyrs out of the insurgents.
On the morning of February 27 the Ambassador communi-
cated to Mr. Hirota's Secretary the message of condolence
of the Secretary of State sent on behalf of the American
Government and people.* Also on the same morning the Am-
bassador left a p.c. card with Mr. Kishi, the Secretary
of the Minister for Foreign Affairs, to be delivered to
the family of Premier Okada, then thought dead. In addi-
tion, he called at the houses of Admiral Saito and Mr.
Takahashi to express sympathy and he inquired after the
health of Admiral Suzuki at the latter's residence. The
War Office sent a statement to foreign Embassies and Lega-
tions in Tokyo briefly explaining the reasons for the in-
4/
surrection and saying that there was no danger with regard
to the lives and property of foreign and other residents
of the city. By noon of the same day the American Embassy
was heavily guarded by Government troops.
5/
Another statement was issued by the Tokyo Garrison
Headquarters at 4:40 o'clock in the afternoon suspending
the publication of newspapers and magazines and advertise-
ments which "are recognized as being harmful for the pres-
ent situation" and prohibiting the sale, purchase, giving
and accepting of weapons and ammunition. Censorship on
news telegrams going out of Japan and also on internation-
al telephone calls was lifted later in the night.
Some time during the evening the rebels took over the
Sanno Hotel, which is close to the Premier's residence.
Occupants
* Department's telegram No. 25, February 26 and Embassy's
telegram No. 40, February 27, 1936.

=== Page 395 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
Occupants of the Hotel were politely advised by the Manager
to go elsewhere and this advice was followed without any
untoward events, as far as is known. The Government's first
ultimatum was served on the rebels at 8 p.m. on February 27.*
Subsequently some sort of temporary compromise was reported
to have been reached. In any case, if such a compromise was
reached it later proved abortive.
February 28.
Mr. Takahashi's death was announced to the publio by the
Finance Ministry on the morning of February 28. The news of
Takahashi's death was not officially announced before, prob-
able to avoid disturbing financial circles.** Government
censorship on news despatches from Japan was relaxed during
the morning but put in force again in the afternoon. The
First Fleet arrived in Tokyo in the morning for protective
purposes but no bluejackets or marines were landed except
those who were detailed to guard the Navy Ministry.
At about 10:30 on the morning of February 28, Captain
Kisou Ouchi, a member of the American-European Section of
the General Staff attached to the Martial Law Headquarters,
called at the Embassy and stated that an attack by the Gov-
ernment forces was planned against the rebels in the Sanno
Ho tel, the Koraku Restaurant nearby, and the Premier's
residence during the afternoon; that therefore the Embassy
was situated in a zone which might become dangerous from
stray bullets if fighting took place; and that therefore
the authorities of the Martial Law Headquarters had ar-
ranged
*Embassy's telegram No. 43, February 27, 1936.
**Embassy's telegram No. 41, February 27, 1936.

=== Page 396 === (confidence 0.99)
-8-
ranged to transfer the Embassy's personnel to the Military
Academy as a secure place in the event that the Ambassador
agreed to such a step.* The Ambassador declined the offer
(the Captain)
with thanks. He went on to say that similar arrangements
had been made for other missions situated in the probable
danger zone. It is interesting and significant to note
that Captain Kisou Ouchi, a responsible officer of the
"loyal" forces, asserted to the Assistant Military Attaché
that the officers and soldiers causing the trouble should
not be called rebels and added: We feel as they do but we
believe in discipline.* It should be remarked here that
this statement was made before the official condemnation of
the insurgents as "rebels", which came after the latters'
refusal to obey the Imperial command in the early morning
of February 29. In the late afternoon, a responsible offi-
ciel of the Foreign Office advised the Ambassador that the
Connander of the First Division had approached the insurgents
in the afternoon with an order from the Emperor that they
obey his commands.** The insurgents were reported to have
agreed to obey the Emperor's commands but continued to nego-
tiate on the question of when and under what conditions they
would return to their Barracks. A friendly Japanese in
close touch with high Government officials informed the Em-
bassy at 9:45 p.m. that an Imperial ultimatum had been de-
livered to the insurgents at 3 o'clock in the afternoon or-
dering them to disband and return to their Barracks and that
the insurgents had signified that they would disregard the
order*** The Embassy's informant further stated that under
these circumstances the Government had decided to take mili-
tary
Embassy's telegram No. 44, February 28, 1936.
** Embassy's telegram No. 45, February 28, 1936.
*** Embassy!s telegram No. 46, February 28, 1936.

=== Page 397 === (confidence 0.99)
-9-
tary measures against the insurgents in the early part
of the following morning, probably at daybreak. Shortly
after this information had been communicated to the Em-
bassy three radio announcements were issued stating that
the Tokyo Garrison would take "a certain step" against
the remaining insurgent officers and troops.
February 29
At about 4:40 a.m. on February 29, and within the
succeeding few minutes, several members of the Embassy
staff observed four tanks and from 200 to 250 soldiers proceed-
ing quietly past the Chancery in the direction of the Premier's
residence. This fact and subsequent movements of troops and
setting up of machine gun barricades, coupled with a notice-
able vigilance on the part of the insurrectionary foroes in
the Premier's residence and in the Sanno Hotel, seemed to in-
dicate that military action would take place. At 5 a.m. an
order to the insurgents to obey the Emperor's command was
broadcast asking that they reconsider their position and as-
serting that they would be considered as rebels if they re-
fused to obey.* General Kashii also issued a communiqué at
6 a.m. explaining the general situation to the public and
advising the citizens "to remain at their homes without be-
ing misled by wild rumors." According to a radio announce-
ment in the morning, about 3o0 of the rebel troops in the
vicinity of the Sanno Hotel had surrendered and returned to
their barracks. Later, General Kashii, as Commander of the
Martial Law Headquarters, issued an instruotion to the rebel
soldiers
* Embassy's telegram No. 47, February 29, 1936.

=== Page 398 === (confidence 0.99)
-10-
soldiers, but not to their commissioned officers, to the
effect that they should not continue to disobey the Imperial
6/
Order and should return to the "flag of the Army". Between
9 and 10 o'clock, a Government airplane made two flights
over the rebels' headquarters, each time dropping leaflets
addressed to the non-commissioned officers and soldiers
stating that it was not too late, that all who continued
to resist would be rebels and would therefore be shot, and
that the relatives of the non-commissioned officers and
soldiers were "weeping" because they might become national
7/
traitors. By 10 o'clock about 400 insurgent troops had
surrendered. Shortly after ll o'clock, three Government
tanks started up the hill toward the Premier's residence,
whereupon the rebels who had taken positions on the hill
fled into the house. A few minutes later several groups
of insurgents gave themselves up at the bottom of the hill
on which the Premier's residence is situated. Throughout
the morning radio broadcasts continued to state that addi-
tional numbers of disloyal troops had surrendered.
At about 3:30 p.m. General Kashii issued a statement
to the public that the insurgents had been "entirely sup-
8/
pressed in the afternoon". A few minutes later the Martial
Law Headquarters addressed a statement to the populace to
the effect that those who had sought refuge were free to
return to their homes and that the traffic restriotions in
8/
the danger zone would be removed at 4:lo p.m.*
The sensational news that Premier Okada had not been
killed but was alive and well was published by a TOkYO ASAHI
extra
* Embassy's telegram No. 49, February 29.

=== Page 399 === (confidence 1.00)
-11-
extra edition on Saturday, February 29, in the form of an
announcement issued on that day by the Acting Cabinet.*
March 1
Conditions that were almost normal prevai led in Tokyo
on the afternoon of March 1.** At 2:30 p.m. the provisional
Government issued a statement expressing its regret over the
incident that began on February 26 and at the same time ex-
pressing satisfaction over the favorable manner in which the
Government had handled the affair, the latter having been due
10/
to "His Imperial Majesty's incomparable virtue".
The Metro-
politan Police Board issued a statement in the afternoon that
five of the policemen detailed to guard the Premier's resi-
11/
dence had been killed in the "recent incident". The Cabinet
issued a statement urging the people to "unite to oppose
radicalism, respect moderation, and follow the paths of
justice, thereby fulfilling the duty of the nation". The
Cabinet also announced that Imperial sanction had been ob-
tained for depriving the twenty rebel officers of their court
ranks and medals. An announcement was issued by the Martial
Law Headquarters in the afternoon to the following effect:
that the officers of the rebel army had been dismissed on
February 29; that the non-commissioned officers and soldiers
who had surrendered had been put in barracks in isolation;
and that the majority of the officers participating in the
revolt had been put in the military prison together with the
ex-officers Koji Muranaka, Isaichi Isobe, and Zensuke Isobe.
The
**Embassy's telegram No. 52, March 1, 1936.
*Embassy's telegram No. 51, February 29, 1936.

=== Page 400 === (confidence 0.99)
-12-
The participation of the three former Army officers and
their imprisonment are significant in view of their con-
nection with the trial of Lieutenant Colonel Aizawa.*
March 2
It was disclosed on the afternoon of March 2 that
Major Takesuke Amano, probably feeling responsibility for
the uprising of the officers under his command in the 3rd
Infantry Regiment of the lst Division, had committed suicide
by shooting himself with a pistol. It was also revealed on
March 2 that Major Chu Katakura, of the Military Affairs Bu-
reau of the War Office, had been shot at and wounded by
rebel officers at the War Minister's residence on the morn-
ing of February 26 when he attempted to enter the building.
The Ambassador adaressed a letter to the Minister for
Foreign Affairs asking him to convey to the competent auth-
orities his sincere appreciation of the thorough measures
taken by various Departments to ensure the safety of the
personnel and property of the Embassy during the recent up-
rising.** In the afternoon the Protocol Bureau of the For-
eign Office communicated to the Embassy a message from the
Home Office that although the mutineers had surrendered, the
Home Office could not state that everything was quiet and
that it was still anxious about the safety of the American,
Soviet Russian, and British Ambassadors, Counselors, Military
and Naval Attachés; that the Home Office would have to as-
sume responsibility for anything that might happen to these
persons
** Embassy's telegram No. 53, March 2, 1936.
* Embassy's despatch No. 1707, March 5, 1936.

=== Page 401 === (confidence 0.99)
-13-
persons; and that the Home Office would, therefore, like to
have a plainclothesman assigned to these officials to sit
beside their chauffeurs when they drove around the city of
Tokyo.* In continuance of its policy of following the seem-
ingly reasonable suggestions of the Home Minister, the Em-
bassy accepted the proposal offered and on the following day
plainclothesmen were assigned to the persons concerned.
March 3
It was reported that from three to five of the rebellious
officers were still at large, which report may have accounted
for the police measures suggested the day before in connection
with the American, Soviet Russian, and British Embassies. As
far as the Embassy could ascertain there were still no evidences
of anti-foreign feeling in Tokyo. It was reported by the JAPAN
ADVERTISER of March 4 that on behalf of his colleagues, General
Senjuro Hayashi had called on the War Minister on the previous
day and informally handed to him for submission to the Throne
the resignations of himself and the following members of the
Supreme War Council, who assumed responsibility for the Febru-
ary 26 incident: General Nobuyuki Abe, General Jinsaburo Masaki
General Baron Sadao Araki, General Kenkichi Ueda, General Giichi
Nishi, and General Juichi Ueda.
March 4
Premier Saionji was reported to have recommended to the
Emperor the appointment of Prince Fumimaro Konoye, President
of the House of Peers, as the most logical man for the post
of Premier. According to press reports, Prince Konoye replied
to
* Embassy's telegram No. 55, March 3, 1936.

=== Page 402 === (confidence 0.99)
-14-
to the Emperor that he could not comply with the Imperial
command to form a Cabinet on account of his poor health.
An Imperial Ordinance (no. 21) published in the OFFICIAL
GAZETTE of March 4 decreed that a Tokyo Army Court Martial
under the presidency of the Minister of War would have juris-
12/
diction over "those officers in the incident of February 26,
1936."
According to a report in the JAPAN ADVERTISER of March 5,
on March 4 General Giichi Nishi, resigning member of the
Supreme War Council and former Commander of the Tokyo Garri-
son, was formally appointed Inspector General of Military Edu-
cation to succeed General Jotaro Watanabe, one of the victims
of the February 26 uprising.
March 5
There have been several reports concerning the way in
which Premier Okada escaped death from the men who attacked
his official residence on February 26. The Embassy has been
unable to v rify vich of the several stories is true. How-
ever, the account given to the press by Mr. Ko Fukuda, Premier
Okada's secretary, was substantially corroborated to a member
of the Embassy's staff by Mr. Shigemitsu, Vice Minister for
Foreign Affairs. According to "THE JAPAN ADVERTISER" of
March 6, Mr. Fukuda made the following statement: when the
Premier's residence was attacked by the rebel troops, Admiral
Okada was asleep, guarded by his brother-in-law, Colonel
Denzo Matsuo, and two policemen; the rebels killed one of
the policemen and Colonel Matsuo, mistaking the latter for
the

=== Page 403 === (confidence 0.99)
-15-
the Premier, and made no further search; Premier Okada hid
in a "recess in the room"; on the afternoon of February 87
people who called at the residence to offer condolences were
allowed to enter; mingling with these people, the Premier
13/
safely left the building, proceeding to the home of Mr.
Kiyuji Sasaki, a friend; on the same day Okada consulted
members of the Cabinet and as a result of their advice post-
poned his desire to pay his respects to the Emperor for fear
that the situation might be "aggravated"; on the morning of
February 28 Admiral Okade submitted his resignation to the
Emperor through the Acting Premier, Mr. Goto, and on the
afternoon of the same day Okada was received in audience
13/
by the Emperor and apologized to His Majesty for the incident.
March 6
Mr. Kurahei Yuasa, former Minister of the Imperial House-
hold, was formally installed as Lord Keeper of the Privy Seal.
in the morning. In the place of Mr. Yuasa, Mr. Tsuneo
Matsudaira, former Ambassador to Great Britain, was installed
as Minister of the Imperial Household.
It was announced March 6 that Mr. Hirota, Minister for
Foreign Affairs, had received the Imperial command to form
a Cabinet and that he was conferring with various leaders.*
The report was widespread that Mr. Hirota was having diffi-
culties in forming a Cabinet and it was also stated that he
was reluctant to assume the post of Premier on account of the
Army's opposition to him.**
No copies were available of several of the official radio
broadcasts. The Embassy tried to ob tain copies from the

=== Page 409 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 4 to despatch
No.1721 dated March 6, 1956,
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
To: Foreign Embassies and Legations in Tokyo.
The state of things that has presented itself in
Tokyo is an outcome of the movement carried out by a
section of army officers who had been contemplating a
reformation by means of direct action. Measures for the
restoration of order and peace are being considered and
taken steadily and at present there is no fear of the
uneasy situation to further aggravate.
A little before 3 o'clock this morning (Feb. 27)
martial law was proclaimed within the city limits of
Tokyo and Lieut. General Kashii was appointed Martial
Law Commander.
There is no danger to the lives and properties of
foreign residents and other people.
February 27, 1936.
The Rikugunsho (War Office).

=== Page 413 === (confidence 0.99)
No.1721 dated March 6, 1936,
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
The Japan Advertiser.
Tokyo, Sunday, March 1, 1936.
Kashii States Vigilance Being Maintained Following
Complete Suppression of Insurgents in Kojimachi;
Refugees Go Home and Traffic Restored
20 REBEL OFFICERS DISMISSED FROM ARMY
Broadcast Early in Morning Tells of Decision to Use Force,
Though Avoidance of Bloodshed Hoped—Privates
Considered Duped by Superiors
After 81 hours, the rebellion, as it was designated yesterday for the fust
time, which started at about 3 o'clock last Wednesday morning, was brought
to an end at 2 o'clock yesterday afternoon with the surrender of all the
troops involved, the martial law headquarters announced an hour later,
adding that complete calm had been restored.
Martial law continued in force, however, and Lieutenant-General Kohei
Kashii, martial law commander, issued the following statement late in the
evening:
"The insurgents in the southern section of Kojimachi Ward were en-
tirely suppressed this afternoon, but the commander is still maintaining
a strict guard as before in order to assure peace and order. It is hoped
that citizens will be cautious in word and deed, be on their good behavior
and co-operate in the work of assuring peace and order."
As all means of traffic within the city were suspended yesterday morn-
ing and citizens were prevented from getting about through no fault of their
own, the Justice Ministry announced that it had been decided to treat the
situation as an unavoidable calamity and therefore to legalize postponement
by two days of fulfillment of all commitments dated yesterday, including
promissory notes, in accordance with Articles 161 and 54 of the Civil Code,
according to Domei. As order was rapidly being restored, no need was
seen for promulgating, as was done immediately after the 1923 earthquake
and fire, an urgent Imperial Ordinance postponing payments.
Police Officials Submit Resignation
Holding themselves responsible for the incident, officials of the Home
Ministry and the Metropolitan Police Board tendered their resignations to
acting Home Minister Fumio Goto yestenday, the Nichi Nichi reports.
Among them were Vice-Minister Asaharu Akagi, Mr. Motoki Karasawa,
director of the police affairs bureau of the Ministry, other bureau direc-
tors, Mr. Kazuo Oguri, superintendent of the Metropolitan Police Board,
and his immediate subordinates.
Twenty minutes after the announcement at 3 o'clock of the surrender
of the rebels, the following statement was made public by the martial low
headquarters:
"1. Those who have sought refuge are now free to return to their homes
in accordance with instructions that will be given by the gendarmerie and
police.
"2. The restrictions on traffic in the area within the belt line are to be
removed at 4:10 o'clock this afternoon."
At 3 o'clock, the martial law headquarters announced:
"As all the troops involved in the rebellion incident surrendered at 2
o'clock this afternoon, complete calm has been established."
Lieutenant-General Kashii made the following announcement at 6
o'clock yesterday morning:

=== Page 414 === (confidence 0.99)
"Applying the whole of Article 14 of the martial law, we intend to sub-
jugate the rebels who have been responsible for the commotion in the nelgh-
borhood of the southern part of Kojimachi Ward. We believe, however,
that the disturbance that will be caused by suppressing the nebels will not
extent very widely, for the area held by the rebels is very narrow. Gov-
ernment officials and civilians are requested, therefore, to appreciate the ob-
jective of the mobilization of troops as proclaimed in my previous notifica.
tion and to maintain composure."

=== Page 415 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 9 to despatch
No./72/ dated March 6, 1936,
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
Translation of Article
TOKYO ASAHI EXTRA, No. 3, Saturday February 29, 1936.
PREMIER OKADA IS ALIVE;
His Brother-in-law Shot and Killed.
The following announcement was made by the Cabinet
today:
The report that Premier Okada was shot and killed by
the rebels in the recent insurrection was received with
profound regret by all, but it has now transpired that the
victim who fell to the foul act of the insurgents and who
was believed to be Premier Okada was not the Premier but
Colonel Matsuo, brother-in-law of Premier Okada. Premier
Okada is alive and safe.
Yesterday morning Premier Okada tendered resignation
to the Throne through Mr. Goto (Home Minister), acting
Premier ad interim. Late in the afternoon, Premier Okada
repaired to the Imperial Palace and being received in
audience by the Emperor he expressed himself profoundly
regretful for the inauspicious happening and apologized
to the Emperor for having given great anxiety to His
Majesty on that account. His Majesty the Emperor was
pleased to bestow upon the Premier a gracious message
in response. The Premier, profoundly moved with the
gracious Imperial words, respectfully retired from the
Palace.
Subsequently, Mr. Goto, Home Minister, who temporarily
held the post of acting Premier ad interim, was relieved
of his additional post.

How Premier Okada escaped death at
the hands of the army men who at-
tacked his official residence early last
Wednesday morning was officlally ex-

his secretary, Mr. Ko Fukuda, in a
