"Early on the morning of February
26." he said, "the residence of the Pre-
mier was attacked by the rebel troops.
At the time, the Premier was asleep
in a Japanese-style room. Guarded by
Colonel Denzo Matsuo and Policemen
Murakami and Doi, he sought refuge
in a recess in the room.
"The rebels killed Colonel Matsuo,
mistaking him for the Premier, and
made no further search. I was soon
made aware that the Premier was un-
injured and tried to get him out of
the residence. Because of the vigil-
ance of the rebels, however, I could
not get him out during the night.
"On the afternoon of February 27.
people calling at the residence to offer
condolences were allowed to enter.
Mingling with the crowd, the Premier
safely left the building. While he was
there, he was safe in a Japanese-style
room protected by self-sacrificing gen-
darmes. It seems that two or three
privates caught sight of the Premier
but did not challenge him.
"On the afternoon of February 27,
the Premier left the residence and went
to the home of Mr. Kyuji Sasaki, a
friend. There he bathed and changed
clothes.
He wished to go to the Imperial
Palace to pay his respects to the Em-
peror. A Cabinet meeting, it happen-
ed, was proceeding at the Palace. He
consulted the Cabinet members, and
as a result he postponed paying res-
pects to the Emperor for fear that the
situation might be aggravated. On the
morning of February 28, he submitted
his resignation through the acting Pre-

SUBJECT: AN ANALYSIS OF THE CAUSES LEADING TO THE INCIDENT
OF FEBRUARY 26, 1936.
894.
894.00/623

(C(N)

MAR 2 5 1936
Department of State
The Hononable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to submit the following report on the
basic and immediate causes leading to the outbreak of the
A
clif
junior officers of the Japanese Army on February 26, 1936,
FILED
88
in which Admiral Viscount Saito, Finance Minister Takahash?
statesmen were attacked. The precise causes impelling the
young officers will not be known until they are disclosed
at the trials of the officers (if they are made publio even
then), but in the meantime the Embassy will attempt to de-
soribe

=== Page 426 === (confidence 0.99)
47-2
- 2-
scribe such of the factors leading to the outbreak as are
known to the staff of the Embassy.
In any attempt to analyze the causes leading to the
insurrection of February 26, 1936, it is necessary to
take into consideration the fact that Japan, despite its
well-known national unity in times of emergency, is in
fact composed of many different and oftentimes warring
factions. Moreover, the fact must be taken into considera-
tion that, in order to understand the motives of modern
Japan, one must examine the customs and procedure of ancient
Japan, especially the Japan of the Tokugawa Shogunate, when
Japanese conventions and modes of thought were largely
crystallized.
1. Basic Causes.
a. Mental Unrest.
The mental unrest in Japan, according to a high Japa-
nese official, dates back to the time of the World War
which, in Japan as in other countries, disturbed the usual
ordered processes of thought and substituted therefor
vague questionings and ill-formed ideas. In Japan the
disturbance of thought was especially intensified by the
industrialization of the country which resulted from the
War. The mode of life of the Japanese was to a considerable
extent altered by industrialization; a wealthy and influ-
ential class came into being; and new influences and abuses
appeared in governmental circles. The Japanese nation was
not entirely prepared for the transition from a predomi-
nantly agricultural state to a modern industrial and com-
mercial nation, with the natural result that certain factions
of

=== Page 427 === (confidence 0.99)
473
-3 -
of the nation found difficulty in adjusting themselves
to altered conditions. This difficulty was particularly
apparent in the military and reactionary factions, which,
accustomed as they were to government by military oligarohies,
could not understand or appreciate the new and powerful
elements in the Japanese Government. The mental unrest
was intensified by the results of the Washington Conference
of 192l-22, which appeared to the Japanese to relegate
Japan to a position as a second-rate Power, and later was
further intensified by the American Immigration Act of 1924,
which, the Japanese believe, stigmatized the Japanese as an
inferior race. A large part of the Japanese nation has been
able to adapt itself to changed conditions and to inter-
pret truly the meaning behind the seeming slights to Japan,
but the remaining part is bewildered and resentful and,
perhaps, to some extent desirous of a return to the old
and more understandable days of feudalism. This disturbance
of thought, caused by the breakdown of the old standards
and the non-assimilation of the new standards, is believed
by many observers to account in part, not only for the
recent incident, but also for the incident of May 15, 1932,
and for the more recent murder of Lieutenent-General Nagata.
b. Economic Mal-Adjustment.
Another basic ceuse of the incident of February 26
(as well as that of May 15, 1932) lies in the economic
situation. It appears undeniable that a considerable section
of the Japanese Army is distinctly opposed to capitalism
as a system of economics. To understand this one must
again remember the Tokugawa and previous eras, when wealth
was

=== Page 428 === (confidence 0.98)
474
- 4 
was considered largely in terms of returns from agri-
culture and when capitalism, as it is known today, did
not exist. A considerable part of the Japanese nation
(consisting largely of the military and unpropertied
classes) does not understand the part played by the capi-
talists in the world of today and resents the growth of
the financial and industrial interests, which they be-
lieve are battening on the common people. As an indication
that the resentment is directed only against the modern
capitalists, it might be pointed out that the resentment
does not extend to the great landowners of the old aris-
tocracy, who acquired their lands and wealth in the feudal
days. The feeling only applies to the modern capitalists,
and principally against the Mitsuis, Iwasakis, Sumitomos,
Yasudas and Okuras. The Army officers feel this resentment
especially keenly (according to a Japanese official) because,
during the Hamaguchi Cabinet, at the stert of the world
depression, the salaries of Government officials, including
Army officers, were drastically reduced, for purposes of
retrenchment. The reductions did not create trouble at the
time, because all the Japanese were poor together. Then
the industrialists took advantage of the Governmental action
to reduce the wages of their employees, while the continued
depression reduced the earnings of the farming class. Later
the yen was devaluated, export trade increased tremendously,
and the industrialists became very wealthy. But this pros-
perity was not passed on to the poorer classes, and the
farmers and the military officers remain poor today. Asa
result of their resentment of this condition some of the
Army

=== Page 429 === (confidence 0.99)
47-5
- 5 
Army officers have evolved a curious conception of state
economy, consisting, as far as the Embassy can ascertain,
in a sort of state socialism or collectivism, with the
Emperor as absolute owner of all property and with the
people as administrators. There is also a unique ad-
mixture of what might be called "samurai-ism", whereby
all are to work together with loyalty to the Emperor for
the good of the state. Naturally, capitalism is opposed
to any such conception of state economy, and therefore the
radical group in the Army is opposed to the capitalists.
As will be explained later, this opposition extends to
the "senior statesmen bloc", which the radical military
group believes to be the tool of the capitalists.
c. The Emperor's Perogatives.
Probably the most powerful factor in the situation
is the political factor bound up with the so-called "Minobe
Theory dispute, which, in brief, is a dispute as to whether
the Emperor is simply an administrator of the law or is
The Law itself. The young officers who engineered the
insurrection believed, and undoubtedly most sincerely,
that the group of senior statesmen surrounding the Throne
were relegating the Emperor to the position of being simply
an administrator of the law and that they were usurping
his perogatives. This is indicated in the written state-
ment which, according to the War Office announcement, the
insurgents distributed on February 26 and which has been
translated as follows:
The

=== Page 430 === (confidence 0.97)
47-6
- 6 -
11
"The purpose of the uprising was to eliminate
those destroying the national polity, such as the
Genro, statesmen close to the Throne, financial
magnates, the military clique, bureaucrats and poli-
tical parties, at the present crisis at home and
abroad, in order to clarify great principles and to
protect the national polity."
The "national polity" in this case is understood to mean
the theory that the Emperor is The Law. The inference
is that the Genro (Prince Saionji), the stetesmen close to
the Throne (Count Makino, Admiral Suzuki, Admiral Saito,
Admiral Okada, Baron Ikki and others), the financial mag-
nates (the Mitsuis, Iwasakis, Sumitomos, Okuras, Yasudas
and others), the military clique then in power(General
Kawashima, General Watanabe, General Hayashi and others),
the bureaucrats(Mr. Takahashi, Mr. Goto, Mr. Machida and
others) and the political parties were usurping the au-
thority of the Emperor and thereby destroying the national
polity. The reactionary element in the Army and outside
believe not only that these groups are usurping the au-
thority of the Emperor, but also that they are using the
Emperor and even the Army itself to further their own
selfish interests. They believe that the Genro, the senior
statesmen surrounding the Throne and the moderate element
in the Army are in league with the great financial interests,
and that they are using their power in the Government
to further those interests at the expense of the great
mass of the people, This point of view is well brought
out in an interview which Captain Weckerling, Assistant
Military Attaché and Mr. J.G. Parsons, of the Embassy, had
with Dr. James A.B. Scherer, a friend of General Jinzaburo
Mazaki, whose opinions Dr. Scherer is presumed to have
1/y. expressed during the interview. A memorandum of this
conversation

=== Page 431 === (confidence 0.98)
477
一7 -
conversation is enclosed. The Embassy cen by no means
agree with all of Dr. Scherer's statements, some of which
seem almost fantastic. For example, Dr. Scherer implied
that Finance Minister Takahashi was the tool of the capi-
talists, who fattened off the profits made on munitions.
But it is undeniable that Mr. Takahashi tried by every
means in his power to reduce the military budgets. Again,
Dr. Scherer implied that the campaigns on the Asiatio con-
tinent were sponsored or instigated by the capitalists,
whose tools the senior statesmen are. But again it is un-
deniable that the campaigns into North China by the Kwan-
tung Army were stopped by command of the Emperor, pre-
sumably on the advice of the same senior statesmen. De-
spite these obviously illogical implications, however,
it is believed that Dr. Scherer's statements contein a
germ of truth and undoubtedly represent the opinions of a
section of the Japanese Army.
It will be noted that the insurgent officers, on
February 26, attacked principally the senior statesmen
surrounding the Throne. Viscount Saito, Lord Keeper of
the Privy Seal, was killed; Admiral Suzuki, Grand Chamberlain,
was wounded; Premier Admiral Okada was attacked but escaped;
Count Makino, now not in high office but probably the
most disliked (by the chauvinistic element) of any states-
men in Japan, was attacked but escaped; and an attack was
planned on Prince Saionji, the Genro, according to reports,
but the insurgents were arrested on the train en route to
the resort where the Prince was spending the winter. The
assumption therefore is that the insurgent officers considered
the

=== Page 432 === (confidence 0.99)
41-8
-8-
the senior statesmen surrounding the Throne to be the most
destructive element in the Japanese Government and decided
to eliminate them first. They apparently did not attempt
to assassinate any of the great financial magnates. Ac-
cording to a Japanese official, however, the insurgents
had a second program, which was designed to eliminate
some of the more prominent financiers, but lack of support
from the people and the prompt measures taken for the sup-
pression of the insurgents prevented them from carrying out
the second program.
d. Dissatisfaction with the External Political Situation.
The military believe that the treaties concluded as a
result of the Washington Conference of 192l-22 were well-
intentioned, but in the end did more harm than good. In
effect, they say that the treaties gave China a "blank
check" which China believed could be used at any time to ob-
tain help from Great Britain and the United States. The
Chinese therefore not only did not help themselves out of
the moress into which they had fallen, but became arrogant
toward Japan. According to a Japanese official, they
refused to recognize Japan's interests in Manchuria, which
brought on the Manchurian Incident of 1931. The same factor
epplies to China Proper. China has diligent and intelligent
people, like the Japanese, but whereas the Japanese have made
a great deal out of little, the Chinese have made nothing
out of much, due primarily to poor government. The Chinese
are using their "blank check" of the Washington treaties
to keep the Japanese from developing China, and the Japanese
themselves are more or less estopped by those same treaties.
The

=== Page 433 === (confidence 0.98)
24/1-9
- 9 -
The military are resentful of this fact and the resentment
leads to direct action against those considered responsible
for the treaties.
e. Indiscipline in the Javanese Army.
Another very serious basic factor in the situation
lies in the fault in the training of the officers of the
Japanese Army which impels them to take direct action when-
ever they feel that they should correct some real or fancied
abuse. To understand this peculiar tendenoy to take direct
and violent action, one must again go back to feudal days in
Japan. Then the samurai, the soldiers, were loyal only to
their feudal lords. When attempts were made to organize
en Imperial Army after the iciji Restoration, it was found
that the soldiers, accustomed as they had been to giving
loyalty only to their clan chiefs, tended to transfer this
loyalty to their immediate superior officers, with the re-
sult that insurrections occurred and there was little
loyalty to the Empire. To correct this defect, the Emperor
Meiji, in 1882, issued en Imperial Rescript to the Army and
Navy, emphasizing the necessity of complete loyalty to
the Emperor. The following passages from the Rescript will
serve to illustrate the emphasis placed upon this point:
" The supreme command of Our forces is in Our
hands, and although We may entrust subordinate
commands to Our subjects, yet the ultimate
authority We Ourself shail hold and never delegate
to any subject."
" Soldiers and Sailors, We are your supreme Commander-
in-Chief. Our relations with you will be most in-
timate when We rely upon you as Our limbs and you
look up to Us as your head."
The

=== Page 434 === (confidence 0.98)
4710
-10-
The Rescript accomplished its purpose of transferring
the loyalty of the soldiers from their immediate chiefs
to the Emperor, but the trouble today arises from the
fact that the Rescript was not allowed to sink into ob-
scurity after it had accomplished its purpose but remains
today, in a modern state, as the Bible of the Army. The
Japanese Army places great importance upon the "spiritual
training" of its soldiers, and uses the Imperial Rescript
for the purpose of such training. The result has been to
over-emphasize the officer's direct connection with the
Emperor and his loyalty to the Emperor, end to under-em-
phasize the necessity of absolute obedience to his superior
officers. Consequently the young officers feel that, if
their conception of their duty to the Emperor conflicts
with orders received from their superior officers, they can,
with a clear conscience, disregard the orders and act ac-
cording to their lights. This fact was well illustrated
by the advance upon North China by the Kwantung Army in
1935. It will be remembered that the units of the Kwantung
Army could only be stopped at the Great Wall by the issuance
of an Imperial order to the effect that the Army should not
move south of the Wall without Imperial sanction. In any other
country, a simple order from the general commanding the army
would have been sufficient to stop the advance, but in the
case of the units of the Kwantung Army, an Imperial command
was required to stop the young officers in command of the
units. This tendency toward direct and individual action
lay behind the recent incident of February 26, 1936, as well
as behind the previous one of May 15, 1932, and behind the
murder of General vagata in 1935. In the recent incident,
the

=== Page 435 === (confidence 0.99)
47-11
- 11 -
the young insurgent officers refused to obey the repeated
commands of their superior officers that they return to
their barracks, and eventually an Imperial command was
issued. The privates obeyed the Imperial command and
surrendered, but the officers held out for some time longer,
refusing to surrender until their demands had been met.
The inconsistency of refusing unquestioning obedience to the
Emperor, while professing to be fighting to restore the
Emperor's euthority, does not seem to have occurred to
the insurgent officers.
2. Immediate Causes.
There were several minor causes which impelled the
insurgent officers to take direct action at this particular
time, but, as was expressed by a Japanese official, they
constituted only the fuse which touched off the powder, the
powder (the basic unrest) being alreedy there.
a. The Transfer of the lst Division to Manchuria.
The lst Division, stationed in and near Tokyo, was re-
cently ordered to vanchuria as replacements for another
division which has served its time in the Kwantung Army.
Some of the regiments of the lst Division were scheduled
to start for Manchuria within a few days, and the dis-
satisfied young officers felt that they were compelled
to act quickly, before their departure, if they hoped
to attempt to correct the abuses which they believed
existed in the Government. Moreover, having been equipped
with ammunition and other supplies for active duty in Man-
churia, they were in a position to make an effective attack
on the Government.
b. The Aizawa Trial.
The hearings in the trial of Lieutenant-Colonel
Aizawa

=== Page 436 === (confidence 0.98)
47-2
- 12 -
9
Aizawa, who murdered Lieutenant-General Nagata last year,
have been proceeding for the past month and have served
to excite the group of younger officers in sympathy with
Aizawa. It developed at the trial that Aizawa held much
the same ideas in regard to the national polity and abuses
in the Government as did the insurgent officers, and that
he had killed General vagata because he believed him opposed
to the best interests of the State. The rumor became current
that Aizawa was not being given a fair trial, the court
martial being composed of officers not in sympathy with
Aizawa's views, and on February 25, General Mazaki, who
had been called as a witness, is reported to have walked
out of the courtroom in indignation at the methods used
in the trial. It is possible that this precipitated the re-
volt of the troops, as it is rumored that one of the demands
of the insurgent officers was that Aizawa be freed. The
Aizawa trial has now been indefinitely postponed, undoubtedly
in order to avoid further exciting the Army. This triel
is discussed more fully in despatch No. 1707 of March 5, 1936.
c. The Results of the Recent Diet Election.
As was reported in despatch No.1706, of March 5, 1936,
the recent election of Diet members resulted in the return of
an unexpectedly large number of Minseito and Shakai Taishuto
members and the defeat of the Seiyukai, the party opposed to
the Okada Gover ment. the Okada Cabinet was thus assured of
support

=== Page 437 === (confidence 0.99)
47-13
- 13 -
support during the coming special session of the Diet. The
young, radical officers of the Army were bitterly opposed to
the Okada Cabinet, which they believed to be corrupt and
weak, and apparently decided to overthrow it by force if it
could not be overthrown by orderly constitutional methods.
3. Conclusions.
While it is yet too early to venture any definite pre-
dictions in regard to the effects of the recent uprising in
the Army, it seems fairly certain, according to the observa-
tions of this Embassy, that certain specific results will flow
therefrom. The first, and perhaps the most importent, will be
definite attempts to instil a better sense of discipline
into the Army. It is not known how this will be done, but it
is possible that less emphasis will in the future be placed
upon the high-flown "spiritual" teachings of men such as
General Araki and General Mazaki, and more upon the soldier's
practical duty to the State.
There will almost undoubtedly be attempts to organize a
"Japanese New Deal" and to correct the economic maladjustments
which lie at the root of much of the present unrest. In Japan
there is, in the opinion of many students of the situation,
too great a spread between the very rich and the very poor.
The distribution of wealth is too unequal, and as long as this
condition lasts the nation will be in danger of further out-
breaks similar to those of May 15, 1932, and February 26, 1936.
There is a limited public sympathy and support for the young
officers who engineered the recent uprising, but one frequently
hears it said that there is a considerable amount of truth in
the accusations of the young officers and their clique in the
Army in regard to maldistribution
of wealth and corruption in
high

=== Page 438 === (confidence 0.95)
47-14
-14-
high places. It is believed that most of the ferming class
and a considerable section of the Army are in sympathy
with the ideals of the insurgents, and it is freely predicted
that there will be other and perhaps more serious outbreaks
unless something is done to remedy the conditions giving rise
to the unrest. There is therefore little doubt that the new
Government, whatever its complexion may be, will strive to
evolve concrete policies which will tend to accomplish the
seme purposes as the New Deal in the United States has as its
aims.
It is also possible that the recent incident will cause a
swing in popular feeling toward the left. It will be remem-
bered that at the time of the great earthquake of 1923, Osugi
and other persons with socialistic tendencies were murdered
in cold blood by police officers. The brutality of the
murders shocked the public and caused a swing to leftist
sentiments in Japan. Later several communist plots were
discovered by the authorities, and there was a revulsion of
feeling in Japan, resulting in the popularity during recent
years of the reactionary elements. The public is now be-
coming fatigued with the numerous plots and uprisings of the
extreme right elements, culminating in the recent incident,
and it is predicted that there will be a decided swing
toward the left in public feeling, although the swing will
not reach to socialism or communism.
Respeotfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

Enclosure No. 1 to despatch
No. 1708 of March 6, 1936 from

Conversation.
Februery 28, 1936.
Dr. James A.B. Scherer with
Captain Weckerling, Assistant Military
Attaché, and r. Parsons.
Background of the Present Crisis.
About lunch time today, Dr. Scherer telephoned and
said that on reading a press despatch from Washington
concerning the present crisis here in Japan he had felt
that perhaps he would be able to contribute a slightly
different point of view on the situation if he could
talk to someone from the Embassy. He said that he could
not come and call on the Ambassador as the building in
which he was located was isolated by troops enforcing
martial law. He suggested that perhaps I would be able
to reach him if we thought that he could contribute use-
ful information. ie then reminded me that he had intro-
duced General wazaki to the Ambassador and that he had
been in close touch with wazaki for the past three years.
About 2:15, Captain Weckerling, the Assistant Militery
Attaché , and myself, called on Dr. Scherer at his apart-
ment in the Nishi Ginza district.
Dr. Scherer began by repeating what he had told me
over the telephone and then elaborated on his relation-
ship with Mazaki. He stated that because he was a former
teacher of Mazaki years ago, Mazaki had treated him as an
intimate friend and had even commissioned his son, who is
in the Foreign office, to act as a sort of honorary secretary
to him. He said that through this relationship, he had been
ab1e

=== Page 440 === (confidence 0.98)
47-10
- 2 -
able to follow the situation within the Army as it has
developed during the past few years.
In Dr. Scherer's opinion the present serious crisis)
was the direct result of four previous incidents. The
first of these incidents was that of May 15, 1932, in which,
although three leading statesmen were assassinated, the
dissatisfied faction in the Army accomplished very little.
He made it evident that the young officers who carried out
the assassinations were identified with the Mazaki group.
The second incident was an attempt on Mazaki's life sche-
duled for November 20, 1934, but which was unsuccessful because
of the vigilence of Mazaki's informants. This attempt was
made by the group in the Army who opposed the reforms
advocated by azaki's adherents.
The third incident was the attempt to remove General
Mazaki from his post as Director General of Military Edu-
cation. As will be remembered, this attempt was successful
in August of last year. At this point Dr. Scherer read
a letter from Mazaki's son(Scherer's "honorary secretary")
in which Mr. Mazaki explained the reasons for his father's
resignation. It was not, as stated in the press, dis-
satisfadtion over certain appointments but on the other
hand a fundamental difference of opinion with General Hayashi,
the Minister of War, concerning the basis on which the Army
should be administered. Dr. Scherer then stated that
General Mazaki was a soldier pure and simple, that he be-
lieved the Army should be an Army and not meddle in politics
and that furthermore it should not be the tool of the
 exploiting class in Japan. The fourth and last incident
in

=== Page 441 === (confidence 0.98)
47-17
- 3 -
\ in the series which lead directly to the present assassi-
nations was the murder of General Nagata by Lieutenant
Colonel Aizawa last sunmer. Dr. Scherer identified Nagata
with the Hayashi-Watanabe group who, in Mazaki's opinion,
were under the control of the capitalists and their in-
struments, the Elder Statesmen. This case, as will be
seen through a study of the testimony in Lieutenant
Colonel Aizawa's trial, brought to a head the question
of whether or not the Army are being exploited by a plu-
tocratic Government which ignored the welfare of the mass
of people in Japan and most particularly the agrarian class.
Dr. Scherer now digressed to develop a fundamental
idea, which was quite evidently azaki's, concerning evils
of the present day capitalistic organization of Japan.
mentioned striking economic and social injustices. He
said that the boys who went out and shot Saito, Takahashi,
and Okada, were typical examples of those victimized by
the present system. ie said that their revolt was against
a system which sent them out to be shot on the plains of
wanchuria while their sisters in Japan were being sold
into prostitution. He then said that he had made inquiry
of two intimate Japanese friends as to who was the real
ruler of Japan. He led them to admit that of course they
recognized that the Emperor was not Divine but purely
biological. On pressing them further they stated that
they supposed the Elder Statesmen were the real rulers.
Dr. Scherer asked them who was behind the Elder States-
men. They replied"The Big Five" (the capitalistic families,
Mitsuis, Iwasakis, Sumitomos, Yasudas and Okuras). To
narrow

=== Page 442 === (confidence 0.98)
47-11
- 4 -
narrow it down to a single man, Seihin Ikeda of the Mitsui
organization was the real ruler of Japan in their estimation.
Dr. Scherer developed at some length the theory that
the Government was purely the instrument of the capitalists.
He mentioned the disproportionately light burden of taxation
placed upon the very rich. He mentioned the immense profits
of the munitions industry which went into the hands of these
same wealthy families. He cited the common saying that
Takahashi and Saito were the "banto" (clerks) of the capi-
talists. He said that Okada, Kaweshima, and Hayashi were
the rubber stamp type of official, readily controlled by
these interests. He mentioned that the prosperity of these
plutocratic interests was further served by the chauvinistic
campaigns sponsored by them and which lined their pockets
through the resulting profits on munitions and armament.
(H did not make clear to Captain Weckerling or myself
just how Takahashi could be regarded as other than a re-
straining influence on the predatory clamor of the armed
services for more money.)
In Dr. Scherer's opinion the state of affairs described
above was dramatically challenged on February 26. The insur-
rection seemed a Japanese expression of the world-wide trend
toward collectivism. To Japan, the date February 26, 1936
would be as important as March 4, 1933 was to the United
States. It was the beginning of the Japanese "New Deal".
Moreover, since the ideas which led the younger officers to
direct action were in general similar to áazaki's, General
Mazaki had now become the real ruler of Japan. But, Dr.
Scherer hastened to say, Mazaki himself had no pre-knowledge
of

=== Page 443 === (confidence 0.98)
17/9
- 5 -
of
the insurrection. He was a believer in discipline.
Upon being asked if Mazaki had any real program to effeot
the fundamental changes which the inauguration of a Japa-
nese collectivism seemed to imply, Dr. Scherer described
Mazaki's views as follows:
First of all General Mazaki was an opponent of the
capitalist class. He went so far as to oppose the big navy
advocates whom he regarded as fattening on the armaments trade
because each time a battleship is built a big profit went to
the Mitsuis or whoever happened to be the builder. He was
distinctly disappointed at the failure of the present
London Naval Conference. As evidence of this attitude
towards the naval problem, Dr. Scherer said that if one
read between the lines of Colonel Mitsui's speech at
the Aizawa trial, published last Sunday, one could see that
the Mazaki group in the Army did in fact regret the failure
of the Conference which rendered more likely increased ex-
penditure for warships. Moreover, General Mazaki was op-
posed to extreme policies in North China for the seme
general reasons. The type of nationalism which impelled
big expenditures for munitions and drastic action on the
part of Japan's ermies on the continent did not seem to
General azaki in line with true Japanese patriotism. He
wished instead to cultivate the true spirit of old Japan
as opposed to the more hysterical plutocratic nationalism
of the present day. He favored the economic rehabilita-
tion of the agricultural classes for whom nothing effective
has been done by the present Government and he also favored
measures which would promote the welfare of the common
man in the cities. As for foreign affairs, Dr. Scherer
described Mazaki as being very friendly to the United States
and mentioned how Mazaki had restrained General Araki
when the latter's propaganda was persuading schoolchildren

=== Page 444 === (confidence 0.98)
4720
- 6 -
throughout the netion that they would fight the United
States when they grew up. On the other hand, General
Mazaki considered Soviet Russia to be Japan's real enemy.
This, in brief, is Dr. Scherer's conception of the man whom
he feels the present emergency has put in the saddle. He
does not think that General Mazaki himself will care to
assume high office. ne prefers to stay in the background
and he believes no formal Government can be organized
for a week. Nor, it should be stated, was Dr. Scherer
able to answer our questions as to whether General Mazaki
had a specific program in mind.
In discussing the events leading up to the present
insurgent movement, Dr. Scherer said that one of the con-
tributory factors was General iayashi's action last summer
in sending so many of the so-called reactionary army officers
to anchuria. This, he said, left the faction subservient
to the capitalists in control at home. He said that the
Aizawa trial had developed in such a way as to make it quite
plain that these men were still in control and that no re-
cognition of the grievances of the dissatisfied element,
the would be champions of the people, could be expected.
He said that it was for this reason that General Mazaki, who
had been called as a witness, left the courtroom in disgust.
He went on to interpret the influence of the recent general
election in an unusual way. He said, of course, the real
victory in this election was won by the proletariat party
which on Takahashi's advice had not been stifled as on former
occasions. However, the election strengthened the hold of the
okada

=== Page 445 === (confidence 0.98)
47-2
-7 -
Okada Government for the time being and, as they believed
this Government to be the instrument of the plutocrats,
younger officers in the Army were goaded to desperation.
Then occurred the circumstance tnat the First Division
was ordered to Lanchurie. Here Dr. Scherer repeated his
striking simile that these boys who were going to be shot
on the plains of Manchuria while their sisters were sold
into prostitution at home had to do what they could before
leaving.
Dr. Scherer was asked what he considered to be Mr.
Hirota's position in all this. He replied that, as we know,
Mr. Hirota was a member of the Black Dragon Society, a re-
actionary organization. He felt that Mir. Hirota would not
be forced out of office but did not know whether he himself
would prefer to resign or not.
Captain Weckerling and I thanked Dr. Scherer for his initia-
tive in creating an opportunity for the Embassy to hear his very
interesting views. We then took our leave and made our way
back to the mbassy.
J.G.P.

S i r:
In the midst of the current political turmoil
in Tokyo, questions of form and ceremony assume trivial
importance, yet it may interest the protocol officers
of the Department to compare the contents of the
American communications of courtesy to the Japanese
Government, called forth by recent events, with those
emanating

=== Page 447 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
emanating from our British confrères, copies of which
1/4. are enclosed for comparison.
In the original message of condolence for the
deaths of the high Japanese officials, that of the
Secretary of State carefully and very wisely avoided
details. The British message on the contrary referred
specifically to the death of the Prime Minister at the
hands of "assassins". While the insurrectionists
undoubtedly were assassins, the atmosphere of uncertain-
ty which pervaded those early days of the uprising
caused both Government officials and the press in Japan
to be very chary of attaching specific labels to men
or to deeds. It remained for a foreign Government to
determine the correct designation.
In our own letter to the Foreign Minister ex-
pressing appreciation of the measures taken for the
protection of the Embassy, all specifications were
avoided. The British Ambassador, however, was already
placed in the position of having to convey expressions
of his pleasure at learning of the Prime Minister's
safety - somewhat in the nature of a recantation - and
he also referred specifically to the "tact and skill"
with which the insurrection was quelled. Am I mistaken
in perceiving therein a degree of gratuitous deviation
from the field of international relations toward that of
domestic affairs and, incidentally, just a shade of a
patronizing tone? I merely wonder whether the British
Secretary

=== Page 448 === (confidence 1.00)
-3-
Secretary of State for Foreign Affairs would have
welcomed just that type of congratulation from the
Japanese Ambassador in London under similar circum-
stances.
But this, of course, is all quite trivial.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

LETTER FROM AMERICAN AMBASSADOR TO FOREIGN MINISTER
March 2, 1936.
My dear Mr. Minister:
In connection with the distressing events of the
past few days, I wish to express to you, and through
you to the other Departments concerned, my deep ap-
preciation of the thorough and efficient measures
which were taken by the Japanese authorities to accord
full protection to my wife and myself and our staff
as well as to the property of the Embassy. Nothing
was left undone in this direction and the thoroughness
of these protective measures gave us a sense of full
security throughout the period of tenseness. We are
consequently very grateful to the authorities con-
cerned and I do not wish to let the occasion pass
without this word of our lively appreciation of all
that was done.
With high respect, I am, my dear Mr. Minister,
Sincerely yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

Mr. Koki Hirota,
His Imperial Japanese Majesty's

The election resulted as follows:
Minseito
205 (previously 127)
Seiyukai
174 (previously 242)

Shakai Taishuto
18
(proletarian)
Kolumin Domei..

("fascist")

Thus no party succeeded in obtaining a
majority, and the minor parties hold the
balance of power.
Press opinion seems to indicate that the
successes of the Minseito and the smaller
parties were less due to popular approval
of the Ckada cabinet than to popular disgust
with the Seiyukai. Even Dr. Susuki, presi-
dent of the Seiyukai, lost hs seat in the

cently organized and which is reported to

be pro-government, had as its slogans

prosperity".
The Shakai Taishuto is the one pro-
letarian party which has survived the
vicissitudes of the past decade. It is said
to resemble in some respects the British
Labor Party and advocates a form of state
socialism combined with a "soak the rich"
program. In many respects its social aims
parallel those expressed in various army
pamphlets. The Embassy reports that there
is little likelihood that the plotters in
the recent outbreak were alermed over the
success of the Shakai Taishuto.
The Kokumin Domei is the personal
following of Nr. Adachi, a shrewd politician
who is a thorough opportunist and who has
attempted to curry favor with the military
by advocating a fascist program.
It is believed that this memorandum
covers the main points in the despatch,
which need not be read.

APR 6 1936.
The results of the Diet election held on February
FILED
20 appear to have been one of the immediate causes of
the uprising that broke out six days later with the
murder of Admiral Viscount Saito, Finance Minister
Takahashi, General Watanabe, and Colonel Matsuo, the
Premier's brother-in-law.* However, the fundamental
causes beneath the outbreak date back several years and
are bound up with a feeling of discontent in the Army
over political and social conditions in Japan.
In
*Embassy's telegram No. 37 of February 26,1936.

=== Page 457 === (confidence 0.98)
48-2
- 2 -
In their campaigning before the election the two
principal political parties were at variance in several
important points. Although both appeared to stand for a
"positive foreig policy", for the improvement of agri-
cultural interests, and for the relief of local finance,
the Seiyukai advocated political reform for the abolish-
ment of bureaucracy and insisted upon the resignation of
the Okada Cabinet, which the Minseito continued to support.
The Seiyukai was reported to be desirous of the abolition
of the"senior statesmen bloc", while there was no evidence
that the Minseito was opposed to consultation of the
senior statesmen by the Emperor and the Government, a prac-
tice maintained during the incumbency of the Saito and
Okada Cabinets.*In a speech delivered on February 3 Mr.
Chuji Machida, President of the Minseito Party and Minister
for Commerce and Industry, stated that in the belief of
the Party the Japanese people had sufficient political
ability to solve Japan's problems; that the situation
could not be handled by a single faction, but that the
whole nation must set its mind to it; and that to show its
conviction in this belief and to carry out its function as
a national party the Minseito had supported the Saito
v Cabinet and later the Okada Cabinet. As a part of its
platform the Kokumin Domei advocated consistency between
military and diplomatic action, and promotion of public
health. The Showakai, the newly organized pro-Government
party, had the slogans "Down with those who disturb
national
*Monthly Report for January, Section III (c).

=== Page 458 === (confidence 0.99)
48-3
- 3 -
national unity" and "From Takahashi finance springs.
business prosperity". The Shakai Taishuto, or Social
Mass Party, was said to favor establishment of demo-
cratic parliamentary politics, opposition to higher
taxes on the masses, immediate adoption of the nation-
al pension system, and State management of important
industries as opposed to capitalistic management.
Various members of the Cabinet made speaking tours
during the last week of the Diet campaign and on February
17 the Premier addressed a public meeting at the Hibiya
Public Hall held under the auspices of the Cabinet, an
action which the ASAHI termed unprecedented in the history
of Japan. Premier Okada reiterated the platform of the
Government adopted at the formation of the Cabinet*, set
forth the Government's reasons for dissolution of the
Diet on January 21, and attributed the sound basis of
Ja an's finances to the untiring efforts of Finance Minis-
ter Takahashi.
The Army and the Navy were reported to desire as a
result of the election the resignation of the Cabinet and
the formation of a "stronger" Government. According to
the NICHI NICHI of February 12, both services hoped for
the emergence of a strong cabinet "reflecting the prevail-
ing international situation" and held the belief that the
nation could not solve its present difficulties so long as
the Okada Cabinet failed to grasp the emergency nature of
the international situation and remained under the restraint
of the political parties "and other forces at odds with
national
*Embassy's despatch No. 918 of August 3, 1934.

=== Page 459 === (confidence 1.00)
48-4
-4-
national defense and the execution of the national
polity". It was reported by NIPPON DEMPO on February
19 that the military insisted that regardless of the
outcome of the election the Government should resign
because "it was too weak".
Before February 20 observers generelly were of the
opinion that the Seiyukai would maintain its position
in the House of Representatives and that the Government
would be forced to resign. In any case, the pronounced
gains of the Minseito came as a surprise. The election
reduced the Seiyukai Diet seats from 242 to 174 and in-
creased the Minseito seats from 127 to 205. According
to the figures published by the JAPAN TIMES of February
24, which are believed to be official, the candidates
elected were as follows:
Minseito
205
Seiyukai
174
Showakai
19
Shakai Taishuto
18
87
Kokumin Domei
15
Others
35
The election thus resulted in giving no party a majority
in the Diet. In addition to the relative good fortune of
the Minseito, the other surprises afforded by the election
were the defeat of Dr. Kisaburo Suzuki, President of the
Seiyukai, and the success of the Shakai Taishuto, whose
vrepresentation was increased from 3 members to 18. In
this connection it is pertinent to remark that this political
party

=== Page 460 === (confidence 0.99)
48-5
- 5 -
party is in no way communistic and that the closest
parallel to it in foreign countries would appear to be
the British Labor Party.
Shortly after the election results became known,
Mr. Adachi, President of the Kokumin Domei, was reported
to have proposed to Premier Okada the resignation of the
Cabinet "en bloc" and its reconstruction in cooperation
with the Seiyukai for the purpose of forming a national
coalition government. According to the JAPAN ADVERTISER
of February 26, the Government decided on February 25 to
reject Mr. Adachi's suggestion and to defer consideration
of the question of Cabinet reconstruction until after the
adjournment of the extraordinary session of the Diet,
scheduled to convene on April 20. This newspaper also
stated that at the same time the Cabinet had arrived at
the belief that the Government would be able to control
the Diet without having to rely on the support of the Kokumin
Domei.
For the most part, public opinion appeared to be opti-
mistic over the results of the Diet election. There was
nevertheless a noticeable difference of opinion as to
whether the political situation would be stabilized by the
election. The JAPAN TIMES of February 24 stated that
financial and economic circles were sanguine over the
election results on the ground that Mr. Takahashi's finan-
cial policy would be maintained by the continuance in office
of the Okada Cabinet through the support of the Minseito.
On the other hand, the TOKYO ASAHI of February 25 and the
OSAKA

=== Page 461 === (confidence 0.99)
48-6
- 6 -
 OSAKA ASAHI of February 26 attributed the victory of
the pro-Government parties to popular disgust with the
Seiyukai and expressed doubt that the policy of the
Okada Cabinet had won the confidence of the nation.
Amid all this speculation the outbreak of February
26 occurred. It is probable that the plot of the younger
Army officers was carried out at this juncture partly
because of despair over the Minseito victory, coupled
with the knowledge that the Okada Government would con-
1 tinue in power. There is little likelihood that the
plotters were alarmed over the success of the Shakai
Taishuto, or so-called proletarian party. On the con-
trary, some of the aims of this Party were known to coincide
with the objectives of the radical element in the Army,
such as control of industry by the State rather than by
capitalists, less taxation of the masses, and increased
taxation of the capitalists. The position of the Shakai
Taishuto was later made clearer with the issuance of a
statement on March 2 by Mr. Hisashi Aso, a Diet member of
the Party. According to Domei (news agency), after stating
that hasty comment on the February 26 incident would be
withheld, Mr. Aso repeated the party platform as follows:
(1) State management of important industries and State
control of finance, insurance and foreign trade; (2) State
management of land, with the right of tillage remaining with
the holder of the land; (3) State control of staple agri-
cultural products; (4) A fundamental reform in taxation
to increase the levy on capitalists; (5) Creation of a
national

=== Page 462 === (confidence 0.98)
48-7
-7-
national pension system; (6) Establishment of credit in-
stitutions or smaller industrialists and businessmen;
(7) Radical readjustment of agrarian debts; (8) Enact-
ment of a labor union Law and a tenancy law; and (9)
`Public managoment of medical institutions. A comparison
of the Party's platform with the desiderata of the Army,
as expressed in the various Army pamphlets, reveals the
fact that both have very similar socialistic aims.*
To return to the question of the Minseito, it may be
reiterated that this Party had not expressed opposition
to the senior statesmen while the Seiyukai, like the in-
surgents, desired the abolition of the whole senior states-
men system. In the announcement of the War Office issued
on the evening of February 26, it was stated: "According
to the written documents of these officers, the purpose
of the uprising was to eliminate those perpetrating the
destruction of the national polity, such as the Genro, the
statesmen close to the Throne, financial magnates, the
military cliques, the bureaucrats, and the political parties
at this moment of serious crisis at home and abroad in
order to clarify great principles and to protect and make
manifest the national polity".
Respectfully yours
Nosathren
Joseph C. Grew

March 17, 1936.
Mr. secretary:
Herewith  brief memorandum on the subject
of the coup and the new Cabinet in Tokyo.
According to news despatches of today,
there is going to be a substantial shift of
personnel in Japan's diplomatic service. It
is forecast that Mr. Arita, now Japanese
Ambassador to China, will be made Japanese
Foreign Minister; that Mr. Shigemitsu, now
Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs, wiil be
made Ambassador to China; that Mr. Yoshida,
who was nominated by Hirota to be Foreign
Minister but was opposed by the Army, will
be made Ambassador to London. These changes
may or may not eventuate.

Department of State
The Coup and the Formation and Composition
of the New Cabinet.
Copier sant to to
0
At Tokyo on February 26 occurred one more incident in
the effort which has been going on for several years toward
D
94
making the voice of the military organization completely
authoritative in the affairs of the state. The lord keeper
94.
of the privy seal, the minister of finance, and the inspector
C
2
general of military education were assassinated, and unsuc-
94.
894.00/627
8
cessful attempts were made to assassinate other important
8
leaders. The attack was directed against the moderate
influences in the Japanese Government, particularly those
surrounding the throne.
With the cooperation of the army the government put
 wn the disorder. But the situation was so serious that
formation of a new cabinet was necessary. The authorities
recognized that the incident was an outgrowth of something
much more important and fundamental than a mere conspiracy
of young officers.
MAR
FILED
The insurgents apparently were in favor of a military
dictatorship and active control of governmental administra-
tion by military men. In meeting the emergency resulting
from the incident of February 26, the influences surround-
ing the throne came to the conclusion that the formation
F/FG
of

=== Page 466 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
of a civilian cabinet was essentially desirable. As a
Tsunled
preliminary, Matsudaira (former ambassador in Washington)
Kilvalhe
and Yuasa, both men of high civilian type, were named to
the two most important appointive positions as advisers
to the Emperor.
The situation required that the army make clear its
WE
aversion to the incident. The insurgents had disclpsed
Sadas
their high regard for General Masaki and General Araki,
both men who have conceived the role of the army as a
highly political role in Japanese public life (a conception
in opposition to which General Hayashi, minister of war,
brought about the removal in July 1935 of Masaki from one
of the highest military posts in Japan). To mend the schism
in the army, and to wipe the past from the record in an
attempt to make a clean start, resignations were accepted
from Masaki, from Araki, from the commander in charge of
putting down the uprising, and from other generals who had
taken a position on one or the other side of the issue.
The army cleared itself of blame for the incident, first by
putting down the uprising, and second by many resignations
among high officers of both the previously existing factions.
It was announced on March 5 that Hirota, foreign minis-
ter in the previous cabinet, had received the imperial command
to form a cabinet. He immediately encountered difficulties,
and it was not until the night of March 9 that the Hirota
cabinet was installed. During that period there had been a
reluctance

=== Page 467 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
of one of its officers as minister of war until satisfactory
assurances had been made. The army had been willing to
approve the naming of Hirota as prime minister, but when he
had once undertaken to form a cabinet it held out for certain
pledges on the part of the new administration; and by this
not unusual stratagem the army was able to make its exactions
felt.
There have been a number of intimations with regard to
the nature of the assurances required before the army would
support the new cabinet:
(1) On March 6 General Tepauchi, who had been asked
to serve as war minister, gave out a statement in which he
said that the government must have determination and courage
to effect radical reforms, to do away with traditional evils,
and to carry out a strong and positive policy in providing
adequate national defense, and asserted that a compromise
solution of the nation's problems would not only fail to meet
the situation but would even aggravate it.
Eiche
(2) After the first meeting of the new cabinet, Baba,
the finance minister, who is one of Japan's many well-trained
bankers, chosen for his ability and for his willingness to
depart from the policy of the assassinated minister of finance,
announced his intention to increase the revenue of the govern-
ment in order to bear the cost of completion of Japan's
Manchuria

=== Page 468 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
Manchuria policy and of national defense and rural relief
Abandoning Takahashi's policy of continual restraint, Baba
stated that increased expenditure must be faced.
(3) A "positive diplomacy" Hirota himself has defined,
in conversation with the American Ambassador, as meaning the
speeding up of Japan's already enunciated policies toward
794.00
Russia and China. Hirota defines the three points of his
China program as (a) cessation of anti-Japanese propaganda
and activity, (b) recognition of the existence of "Manchukuo",
not necessarily involving de jure recognition, and (c) Sino-
Japanese cooperation to combat the spread of communism; and
he affirms that these points have been accepted in principle
by the Chinese Government. The wide range of activities in
Asia which Japan might see fit to pursue in accordance with
Hirota's assertions of policy should be borne in mind when
evaluating his statement, repeated on March 13 to the
Ambassador, that Japan will engage in no war while he is in
office.
(4) A new man, Baron Hiranuma, has become president of
the privy council immediately upon the formation of the new
cabinet and undoubtedly in accordance with the settlement
agreed upon between Hirota and the military. The new president
displaces a scholar (Ikki) who, in the recent political acri-
mony raging in Japan, has supported the constitutional inter-
pretation of the position of the Emperor not accepted by the
military. Hiranuma is vigorously opposed to this interpreta-
tion

=== Page 469 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
tion of the position of the Emperor, and in other respeets
also is aggressively pro-military.
The incident of February 26 was a violent expression of
political protest. In meeting the resultant emergency the
army made clear its opposition to such violence, but in the
formation of the cabinet the army has insisted that the
political protest which it conceives to have been the
motivating force behind the incident be given full considera-
tion. In consequence, the formation of the cabinet has been
dependent on the giving of pledges involving larger appropria-
tions for the military, speeding up of policy toward China,
and increase of defenses against the Soviet Union. In
consideration of these pledges, and the general pledge of
active measures to meet the nation's needs, a non-party
cabinet with military men serving only as ministers of war
and of navy has, with the sanction of the military, assumed
office.
(Note: For list of members of cabinet, see
following page.)

6

weli be.

Inendation For the last few
years the conflict bet ween the
ounger cotitary officers and the
scasoned
Japanese
statesmen
backed by the so-called vested in-
terests has been brewing with in-
creasing intensity. The last straw
seems to have been provided by the
recent trial of Lieutenant-Colorel
Aizawa for the assassination of
General Nagata.
Whatever tomorrow
bring by way of explanation of the
"army revolt," it is certain that,
should the ultra-patriots gain the
reins of government. the Orient
may soon become the theater of
another international conflagra-
tion. It is to be hoped for the sake
of the island empire as well as in
the interest of world peace that
order may be restored at the
carliest possible moment.
The growth of assassination-
mania in Japan may be traced to
the popular regard for those who
commit violence in the name
of patriotism.
Patriotism has
often been invoked to cover a
multitude of crimes, and the price
which Japan has to pay in the
loss of able administrators may
yet prove too dear for the na-
tion to bear.

will be accomplished with as much

possible. The Emperor himself is

of the Government and can be relied

who, filled with an intense spirit of
patriotism, have chafed against what
they have considered to be unnecessary

The assassination of General
Nagata, for which .Colonel Aizawa is
now undergoing trial, had its motive in
this sense of dissatisfaction, and one
has only to 'recall the assassinations of
Premier Inukai, Baron Dan and others
in recent years by self-sacrificing
patriots to realize that there is in
Japan a type of patriotism which some
times bursts out into violent protest
against the established order.It is
impossible to believe that these violent
means further in any way the ends
which their sponsors would serve, and
Japanese public opinion, which has
been lenient towards such things, will
now doubtless demand that violence
be sternly- discountenanced. World-
wide sympathy will be evoked by the
news that several prominent figures,
including Premier Okada, Mr. K
Takahashi, Minister of Finance, and
Admiral Viscount Saito, have lost
their lives in this latest incident,
and the hope will be as equally
widespread that the responsible Japan-
ese leaders—civilian, military and
naval—will rally around constitutional
authority and re-establish the confid-
ence which was only so recently shown
in the Government of the day.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
April 16, 1936.
EHD:
Peip1ng's despatch No. 283 March 6,
1936, Nanking's despatch No. 123, March 13,
1936, and Shanghai's despatch No. 51,
February 29, 1936, all forwarding press
comment on the Tokyo incident of February 26.
Peiping reports Chinese press comment
as (1) hoping that Japanese officials would
henceforth devote themselves to an adjust-
ment of the internal situation instead of
to expansion, (2) fearing the Far Eastern
situation will become more disturbed, and
(3) predicting that Japan's international
position will become unfavorable. A
Japanese-controlled Chinese language
paper welcomes the coup and hopes it will
strengthen military dictatorship and
economic control and will result in a more
active China policy.
Nanking forwards one editorial which
describes the incident as detrimental to
constitutional government and sees in the
action of the militarists a lack of organi-
zation.
Shanghai forwards press comment from
English language papers which is highly
critical of the military and attributes
the

=== Page 498 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
一 2 -
the action to the feeling aroused by
the trial of Lieutenant Colonel Aizawa,
murderer of Lieutenant General Nagata,
and to an over-wrought nationalism.
The NORTH CHINA DAILY NEWS comments
on the lack of Japanese expressions of
regret. The SHANGHAI EVENING POST AND
MERCURY sees a strengthening of militarism
and foresees increased likelihood of a
conflict with the Soviet Union and
stronger measures in China.

JAPAN S COUP D'ETAT.
As a result of the general election in Japan, the
Government and the Party have won an absolute vietory.
Speaking from the viewpoint of the usual principle of
constitutional administration, since the Government and
the Party have won a great victory, the position of the
Government should be even stronger. However, since the
year 1932 the political situation in Japan has deserted
the normal way. During last year it was frequently
rumored that a coup d'etat would take place. It was
fortunate in the end that the yesr pas ed quietly.
